# RK3576 SoC parameter sheet

The RK3576 NPU runs through the same mainline `rocket` drm/accel driver as the RK3588, with
an in-flight RFC series adding the SoC. This sheet covers identity, SoC integration and the
state of the port. It records what external reverse-engineering and the RFC establish, and
what an H96 MAX M9 confirms live. The companion sheet [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md)
carries the register layer: the encoding written out as an emitter, and what hardware
settles about it. The load-bearing difference from the RK3588 is the **re-packed CNA
register offset map**.

The port is validated on kernel 7.1.6 with the whole series in-tree (`0001`-`0024`, uAPI
interface 1.5). All gates return `rc=0` (57 of 57), the taint is unmoved, and `dmesg` shows
zero WARNINGs. The five graphs run at 4.9 / 6.5 / 6.6 / 7.0 / 33.8 ms, indistinguishable
from the same list on an out-of-tree `0023` module. So `0024` (do not let a stale poll
retire the next job) costs nothing measurable and breaks nothing [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
2026-08-04].

The uAPI header assembled from the series' own patch hunks is byte-identical to the one that
kernel installs. That match shows the userspace and the loaded module agree on the
interface, beyond both building. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

This sheet draws on three sources:

- Ga Hing Woo's mainline bring-up (blog + `github.com/gahingwoo/linux-rk3576-npu`, including
  `notes/rk3576-npu-values.md`)
- The RFC series "accel/rocket: RK3576 NPU (RKNN) enablement" (Jiaxing Hu, patchwork
  `linux-rockchip` series 1129921, 8 patches, 2026-07-18)
- The Radxa RK3576 vendor kernel `linux-6.1-stan-rkr5.1`

The load-bearing documents in the gahingwoo repo are:

- `vendor-capture/RK3576_CNA_MAP.md` and the CORE/DPU map
- `predict.py`
- `FINDINGS-FLOATSURFACE.md`
- `MATMUL-PIPELINE-ANALYSIS.md`
- `vendor_regcmd_full.txt`, a full 139-entry vendor register program

## NPU identity

- An rknpu-derived NPU with 2 cores (`npu0`, `npu1`), marketed as 6 TOPS. The RK3588 has 3
  cores.
- The same `rocket` uAPI and `/dev/accel/accel0`, with no interface change in userspace.
  The mainline `rocket` core landed in kernel 6.18, and RK3576 support is the RFC applied to
  it. [source-confirmed: gahingwoo repo, kernel fragment `CONFIG_DRM_ACCEL + ROCKET + CRC32C`]
- DT compatible string: `rockchip,rk3576-rknn-core`. [source-confirmed: RFC patch 1]

### Second-core throughput

Core 1 binds and runs jobs, and buys nothing. Measure a second core against a one-core
control, not against one fd.

In the configuration measured here, core 1 is enabled at its real address (`0x27708000`, see
"Live-hardware notes") and has the six clocks `rocket_core_init()` hard-gets. The kernel then
fans an open fd's work across both cores' schedulers with no userspace change. Jobs land on
both, and each core reports an equal share of runtime-active time.

Two worker fds are worth 1.3-1.7x over one. That gain is submit pipelining, and one core
delivers it on its own. With core 1 unbound, two fds give 1.51-1.69x at 8×64×32, 1.62-1.66x
at 32×1024×512, 1.49-1.51x at 128×1024×1024 and 1.33x at 256×512×2048. That matches or
beats the same measurement with both cores bound (1.55-1.74x, 1.49-1.68x, 1.38-1.42x). One fd
does not saturate a single core at a ~340 us submit floor. Filling its dispatch gaps is the
whole gain, so a second core has nothing left to add [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-28,
`tests/rk3576_multicore.c`].

### Two jobs in flight

**Two rocket jobs in flight at once compute wrong answers, and that is the reason to leave
core 1 disabled.** The condition is simultaneous execution, not the second core's existence,
not anything a job inherits from the one before it, and not the mapping. A mutex can hold
the pair to one job in flight, with two fds, both cores bound and jobs alternating cores.
Under that mutex every call is exact: 96 calls a cell at 256×512×2048, 32×1024×512 and
128×1024×1024. The same two fds driving at once are wrong on 96-100% of calls. The wrong
calls cover as much as 94% of the surface, with deltas spanning the whole int8 range
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_core_pair.c pair`].

The correspondence with overlap is one to one, and it is read from the driver rather than
inferred. Both cores report runtime-active time for the whole wall in every cell, because
both stay resumed throughout. That shows they are powered, not that a cell's jobs were
spread over them. A cell whose jobs all landed on one core is the one-core control under a
different label. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

An instrumented `rocket` counts jobs per core, domain swaps per core, and jobs started while
another core already had one in flight. At 256×512×2048 one library call is one job. The
concurrent cell runs 48 jobs a core with 48 overlap events, every job in a simultaneous pair,
against 96 of 96 calls wrong. Every clean cell records zero overlap. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

The vulnerable window is the job's own duration, so the contended resource is held for the
whole job rather than for a phase. The stagger test delays one worker past a common barrier,
one call each per rep. The share of wrong calls is 100% at +0 and +100 us, 91.7% at +250 and
4.2% at +500. None is wrong from +750 us out to +5 ms. The cliff is sharp, and the driver's
overlap count reaches zero at the same rung. The per-submit floor is ~340 us, so the window
is the submit [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_core_pair.c stagger`].

**The corruption crosses processes, so no userspace mitigation exists.** A library mutex
cannot serialize two processes holding one fd each. They corrupt exactly as two fds in one
process do, with 96 of 96 calls wrong. A single process doing the same total work gets zero
wrong. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_core_pair.c xproc`]

### Refuted mechanisms

Four candidate mechanisms are refuted, and the refutations are worth keeping:

- *Residual per-core register state.* Anything a program inherits from a previous one would
  corrupt the serial cell too, and the serial cell is clean. That includes the
  differing-weights cell, where the two workers hold different weights. That cell is the one
  that can see a worker being handed the other's operands. The plain matrix gives both
  workers the same operands, so run the differing-weights cell before concluding anything
  about inputs.
- *The two register banks being one bank aliased twice.* They are distinct hardware.
  Different values written into core 0's and core 1's PC `TASK_DMA_BASE_ADDR` and CNA
  `S_POINTER` each read back as written.
- *The IOMMU domain.* Three results refute it. Each core has its own MMU (`27702000` and
  `2770a000`) in its own group, so no attach can displace the other's. With every context
  sharing one domain, the counter confirms zero domain swaps in every cell, and the
  concurrent cell stays at 96 of 96 wrong. A page-table mix-up would also show in the
  differing-weights serial cell, which is clean across 36 domain swaps a core.
- *The vendor's RK3576-only `state_init` and its `pc_dma_ctrl` PC serialization.* This
  project applied the two together as one delta, because a one-at-a-time pass could not have
  found a condition of two. The delta seeds both CNA pointer groups at every power-on and
  holds a device-global lock across the whole register-programming sequence. Under it the
  concurrent cell is 100% wrong, against a 97.9% control. The seeding is counted, so this is a
  negative about the vendor's sequence and not about a knob that never fired. Since the whole
  delta does nothing, no subset can.

### The shared CBUF

What is left is a datapath resource the two cores share, and the TRM names one candidate.
There is exactly one `CBUF` on this part: 1 MB at `0x3fe80000` in the address map, outside
either core's register bank. Its 16 banks are gated from a single `NPU_GRF_MEMGATE_CON0`
field ("clock gate enable for cbuf bank 0-15") in the shared NPU GRF. The CRU carries one
`ACLK_RKNN_CBUF`/`HCLK_RKNN_CBUF` pair, which both cores' nodes list. [TRM]

The vendor driver is the only Rockchip NPU configuration with a `cache_sgt_init`. Its
`rk3576_cache_sgt_init()` hands each core a disjoint slice of exactly that megabyte: core 0
`[0,448K)` and `[896K,960K)`, core 1 `[448K,896K)` and `[960K,1024K)`
[source-confirmed: `rknpu_drv.c:123`]. The table there is
`block_size_kb = {448, 64, 448, 64}` at `block_offset_kb = {0, 896, 448, 960}`, indexed
`[i * core_num + j]` with `core_num = num_irqs = 2`. At 16 banks over 1 MB that is 64 KiB a
bank, and the split is bank-aligned. Core 0 gets banks `{0-6, 14}` and core 1 `{7-13, 15}`,
eight each.

This library's own single-core CBUF pool measures 448 KiB (7168 granules of 64 B), bank for
bank the vendor's core-0 primary block. That pool was measured with core 1 unbound and with
no knowledge of the vendor table.

The register program sets the CBUF banking. On the RK3588 `CNA_CBUF_CON0` carries
`DATA_BANK`, `WEIGHT_BANK` and `FC_DATA_BANK` bank selects over 16 banks. On the RK3576 the
same register carries a granule allowance F, which is an allowance and not a bank base.
Nothing an encoder emits depends on the core, so both cores stage into the same banks.

That is a hypothesis with a named mechanism, not a measurement. Nothing here has watched the
two cores write the same CBUF address, and the 448 KiB coincidence points both ways. A
hardware per-core partition would mean the two cores *cannot* collide, which would refute the
candidate. One core's addressing reaching 448 KiB of a shared pool would confirm it.

Two distinctions limit what the vendor table shows. The word `cbuf` appears nowhere in the
vendor driver. The driver expresses the partition as an allocator plus an IOMMU mapping
(`rknpu_iommu_map_with_cache_sgt`, `rknpu_remap_pfn_with_cache_sgt`), never as a register
write. Whether a job's buffers land in that region is an alloc-flag opt-in (`rknpu_gem.c`,
the `nbuf_size > 0` branch). So `cache_sgt` governs CBUF-as-allocatable-memory, a different
use of the same RAM from CBUF-as-staging. It is not what keeps vendor staging from colliding:
an external RK3576 YOLOv8 benchmark, described below, runs two per-core contexts correctly
*without* the SRAM opt-in.

No other public effort covers the two-core case either. The one other mainline RK3576 NPU
bring-up (gahingwoo, `linux-rk3576-npu`, ROCK 4D) runs a single core. The wall it stops at
is the chained-task one that `patches/rk3576/npu/0008` closes.

Two-core concurrency is not a silicon impossibility, and the missing piece is not expressible
in the current uAPI. The vendor driver schedules independent jobs on the two RK3576 cores
concurrently by design. It keeps a todo list and an in-flight job per core, with
`RKNPU_CORE_AUTO_MASK` load-balancing by queued task count. The SDK exposes per-core context
placement alongside its combined mode. [source-confirmed: `rknpu_job.c`, `rknn_api.h`] So the
part is meant to run two jobs at once.

An external benchmark measures it doing so. A reComputer RK3576 YOLOv8 run
(hackster.io/wurengmin) puts two `spawn` processes on separate cores, through
`init_runtime(core_mask=NPU_CORE_0)` against `NPU_CORE_1`. It releases them together through
a barrier, and reports ~2x throughput with plausible detections over 394 frames x 5 repeats
x 3 model sizes. Its script carries no numerical check. It records output shapes and timings
and never diffs outputs across configurations. So read it as establishing that correct
two-core concurrency is software-reachable here, not as a bit-exactness result.

What `rocket` lacks is a per-core partition of whatever is shared. On the CBUF hypothesis
that partition lives in the *register program*. Userspace authors that program without
knowing, and without being able to choose, which core will run it. The vendor's own uAPI is
the precedent: `init_runtime(core_mask=...)` fixes the core at context creation, before any
inference. So its closed runtime can bake per-core state into the program it builds. A fix
therefore needs the core identity to reach the encoder, not just a driver patch.

### CBUF feature staging

The resource is the CBUF feature staging area, and the trigger and the extent are different
things. The symmetric cells above give both workers the same shape, so none of them can
separate a capacity from a control path. The asymmetric sweep holds one worker fixed as a
victim (256×512×2048, 2048 feature granules) and sweeps the other's footprint as an
aggressor. It runs `tests/rk3576_core_asym.c` with 160 victim calls a cell, over two runs
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-28]:

| aggressor | feature granules | weights | victim calls wrong | surface destroyed per wrong call |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| tiny  | 4    | 4 KiB    | 71.9-75.6% | 0.6-0.7% |
| small | 32   | 16 KiB   | 56.2-70.0% | 2.7-3.0% |
| mid   | 256  | 64 KiB   | 22.5-32.5% | 12.9-16.9% |
| large | 1024 | 256 KiB  | 20.6-22.5% | 27.9-38.9% |
| equal | 2048 | 1024 KiB | 95.6-98.8% | 48.5-55.3% |

The trigger is not capacity. An aggressor whose whole feature plane is four granules, 256
bytes against a 448 KiB pool, corrupts about three quarters of the victim's calls. Nothing is
being exhausted, so the collision is unconditional.

The extent is the feature plane, not the weight path or the occupancy. Footprint and submit
duration co-vary across those five rows, so two more shapes pull them apart. The `wideN`
shape (4 granules behind a 128 KiB N-tiled weight cube: a small plane and a long submit)
destroys 5.6-6.5%. The `flatN` shape (a 2048-granule plane against 32 KiB of weights: a
large plane and a short submit) destroys 30.4-34.9%. That is five to six times the damage
from four times less weight and a shorter occupancy, but 512x the feature plane.

So the two cores stage their feature planes into the same CBUF, unconditionally, and an
intruder destroys however much plane it staged.

A cell is a measurement only once the driver's overlap counter is non-zero, and that is not
automatic. A small aggressor running a fixed call count finishes inside the victim's
host-side packing. One run of this sweep recorded zero overlap for every asymmetric cell for
that reason. The aggressor must drive continuously for the victim's whole run.

### CBUF placement

Placement does not separate the cores, so the collision is not two jobs writing the same
CBUF granules. The CBUF base is a real, honored address, and moving it is not enough. That
retires the search for a bank-select field, because the field that moves a job is already
decoded and already emitted. The low half of `CNA_CBUF_ENTRIES` (`0x103c`) and of
`CNA_CBUF_CON0` (`0x1040`) is a granule offset: where a task's window starts and where its
fetch resumes. The row-reuse path drives it. Every plain entry point leaves it at zero, so
every job stages from granule 0 (`tests/rk3576_cbuf_base.c`, `ROCKET_RK3576_CBUF_BIAS` /
`rocket_rk3576_set_cbuf_bias()`) [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-28].

The base is honored, and it reaches only inside the task's own allowance window. The last
base at which a task still computes is `4096 + F - plane`. That holds at every plane
measured (64, 256, 1024 and 2048 granules), each exact at the prediction and wrong 64
granules past it. So a job cannot be staged outside the budget it programmed. Two jobs can
be placed apart only when their planes together fit one window.

Placed apart, the two jobs corrupt exactly as they do at a shared base. The victim is
256×512×2048 at base 0 (2048 granules), against the `tiny` aggressor with its base swept
clear of the victim. The share of victim calls wrong is 78% at base 0 and 68-72% at 2048,
3072 and 4032. That is flat, inside the run-to-run scatter, with the driver's overlap counter
at 57-60 a cell throughout.

The knob is demonstrably live on the worker thread. A base past the aggressor's *own* bound
(4156 against a bound of 4092) takes it to 896 of 896 calls self-wrong, where every scored
row leaves it at 2-4%. Without that positive control, "biasing changed nothing" and "the
bias never reached the emitter" are the same table.

Nothing else in `CNA_CBUF_CON0` is a bank base. The emitted word is `bit28 | F<<16 | base`.
A sweep of the only bits this library never varies leaves the register fully accounted for.
Bits 30 and 31 are inert. Bit 29 is live but corrupts the job at every combination that sets
it.

Nor is there anywhere else to put a partition. The NPU GRF carries exactly one CBUF
field, `NPU_GRF_MEMGATE_CON0` bits[15:0] ("clock gate enable for cbuf bank 0-15"), shared
rather than per core, and no base, mask or window register
[TRM, `Rockchip_RK3576_TRM_Part1_V1.2`].

So no userspace-reachable partition of the CBUF exists, and a perfect address partition
would not have helped anyway. The mechanism this leaves is a shared feature-staging datapath
rather than shared storage [hypothesis]. This IP's ancestor gives the CBUF four ports,
feature-write and weight-write among them separately [nvdla.org/hw/v1/hwarch.html].
Contention on one shared feature-write path predicts all three measurements at once. The
three are extent proportional to the feature plane, indifference to weight bytes and to
occupancy, and immunity to placement.

Reading the CBUF SRAM at `0x3fe80000` while a job runs would say whether the two cores'
windows sit at the same physical base. That reading is diagnosis, not a fix. It is also a
`/dev/mem` poke at an SRAM in the NPU voltage domain. The domain must be held resumed during
the read, or the SoC hangs. The one experiment still decisive on the mechanism diffs the
vendor's live submit program for one model at `core_mask = NPU_CORE_0` against `NPU_CORE_1`.
That needs a vendor-BSP RK3576 with an rknpu regcmd dump, and the H96 is mainline.

### The six-register CBUF window

An externally read CBUF window is six registers, and this library's bias moves two of them
[hypothesis, not measured here]. The `charsiu` project derives the vendor's CBUF window from
a start bank S off a 2026-09-04 census of the Llama-3.2-1B `.rkllm`. In that census every op
appears twice, and the two copies differ in exactly six registers (`src/job.c`,
`charsiu_cbuf_window`):

| register | window 0 | window 1 | as a function of S |
|---|---|---|---|
| `0x1018` | `40000404` | `4000040b` | low byte `4 + S` |
| `0x1038` | `00000007` | `0000010e` | end bank, `\| 0x100` for S > 0 |
| `0x103c` | `xxxx0000` | `xxxx1c00` | low half `S*0x400` |
| `0x1040` | `10000000` | `2c001c00` | `(S*0x400 + 0x1000) << 16 \| S*0x400` |
| `0x2818` | `00000000` | `1c000000` | `S*0x400 << 16` |
| `0x2820` | `00000000` | `00000038` | `S*8` |

On that reading the CBUF is two mirror-image windows of 7 banks inside 14 (`0x3800`
entries), and the `charsiu` runtime hands each core its own (`charsiu_job.cbuf_window = di`).
The registers `0x2818` and `0x2820` appear nowhere in this tree, neither emitted nor
documented. The `ROCKET_RK3576_CBUF_BIAS` knob moves only the low halves of `0x103c` and
`0x1040`. The bias moves two of six, and not the end bank. It therefore moves a window's base
while leaving its extent at bank 7, a shape that predicts exactly the flat corruption
measured above.

So the negative "no userspace-reachable partition exists" rests on moving part of a bundle,
and a negative established that way is not established. Float mode on this part obeys the
same rule: it is three registers that must move together. The positive control proves the
bias reached the emitter. It does not prove the bias was the whole unit. The discriminating
test emits all six as a function of S, gives the two cores different S, and re-runs
`tests/rk3576_core_asym.c`. Until that runs, the shared-feature-write-datapath hypothesis
above is one of two live explanations, not the surviving one.

### Undervolt as a competing explanation

An undervolt is a competing explanation, and the two-core corruption was measured at the
operating point it names [hypothesis, not measured here]. The `charsiu` project reports the
same class of fault from two cores in flight, and removes it entirely by moving the rail or
the clock. In that fault the job on the second core writes one word of a row wrong, the right
value plus 1024. The error is a few bits around bit 10 of the accumulator. It hits one row in
a few thousand, always at the same array position. Their sweep runs four DTBs on one board,
with the kernel and binary held, in four passes of 5400 rows each:

| NPU clock | `vdd_npu_s0` | wrong words a pass |
|---|---|---|
| 786 MHz | 750 mV | 11 to 25 |
| 594 MHz | 750 mV | 0 0 0 0 |
| 786 MHz | 800 mV | 0 0 0 0 |
| 786 MHz | 850 mV | 0 0 0 0 |

With no `assigned-clock-rates` on its nodes, this NPU runs at 786.43 MHz off the audio PLL,
the rate the boot firmware leaves. It has no OPP, and the rail sits at the U-Boot default of
750 mV. The upstream series assigns 594 MHz in the SoC device tree. Whether 786 MHz at
750 mV is under the vendor's voltage depends on the chip's bin, which no mainline kernel
reads. The vendor's 800 MHz step asks 800 mV of the lowest bins and 725 mV of the highest
(per-bin table under
[Memory system, NPU rail, and thermal envelope](#memory-system-npu-rail-and-thermal-envelope)).

**Every two-core measurement on this sheet was taken in the failing cell, on both halves.**
For those runs the H96 MAX M9 read 750 mV, `enabled`, `num_users=1` and 786431952 Hz on
`scmi_clk_npu` (debugfs, root only). Neither NPU node carried `assigned-clock-rates`
[live 2026-09-25, kernel 7.2.3-1]. With `assigned-clock-rates = <594000000>` on both nodes,
the same board reads 594000000 Hz [live 2026-10-02, kernel 7.2.8-1]. That image ships core 1
`disabled`, so no two-core run exists at 594 MHz here.

How the rail is held is a variable too. The two-core runs here had `vdd_npu_s0` pinned by
`regulator-always-on` on the PMIC node, and `num_users=1` was that pin alone. The regulator
core counts an always-on regulator as one user [source-confirmed: `drivers/regulator/core.c`
`set_machine_constraints()`]. An `npu-supply` on a core node is inert, because `rocket` calls
no regulator API.

The upstream series hands the rail to the NPU power domain as `domain-supply`, with
`npu-supply` on both cores and no `regulator-always-on`. The series reports the rail then
following the domain, at `use_count` 0 and disabled at rest. The H96 MAX M9 reads the same
under that arrangement. At rest the rail is `disabled` with `num_users=0`, and with a job in
flight it is `enabled` with `num_users=1` [live 2026-10-02, kernel 7.2.8-1]. "Clocks, resets,
power" below has the detail.

A pinned rail and a domain-switched one are not the same electrical condition, and neither
project has held that difference constant against the other. The `charsiu` fix is a runtime
check rather than a serialization. Their `overlap_safe()` reads the rail from sysfs and the
clock from debugfs, and checks both against the vendor's table at its lowest bin. At 800 mV
they overlap every batched call, with nine models identical to the serialized arm.

The `charsiu` fault and the one here are not obviously one mechanism. Their fault is tens of
wrong words a pass. The fault here destroys 71.9-75.6% of a victim's calls and scales with
the aggressor's feature plane, which an undervolt does not straightforwardly predict. But the
two negatives sit on the same silicon at the same operating point, and the one here was
never run at any other. Before the corruption is treated as an encoding-level property, the
discriminating test re-runs `tests/rk3576_core_asym.c` at 594 MHz, or at 750 mV against 800.
Either arm needs core 1 enabled in the device tree, and a 786 MHz control under the same rail
arrangement.

### The per-core `S_POINTER` bit

A third explanation is the per-core bit in `S_POINTER` [expected, untested]. The RK3576
encoder here writes CNA and CORE `S_POINTER` from the regcmd, as `0xE`, or `0x30` for a LUT
program. That drops the per-core bit, `0x10000000` times the core index, which the vendor
`rknpu` driver and mainline `rocket` write before every task. On the RK3588 the same write
hangs every job that runs off core 0, 24 of 24
([../encodings/regcmd-task-model.md](../encodings/regcmd-task-model.md)). On the RK3576
serialized core-1 jobs are exact, so the bit can matter only with both cores in flight. The
discriminating test drops the two writes, confirms the kernel's value stands, and re-runs
`tests/rk3576_core_pair.c pair` with both cores driving.

### Caller envelope

One rocket job in flight at a time is safe on this part however many cores are bound. Two
are not, whether they come from two fds or two processes. Single-fd work is therefore
unaffected, and the whole gate suite is green with core 1 bound. A multi-fd caller is
affected. Core 1 comes out without a device-tree change:

```sh
echo 27708000.npu | sudo tee /sys/bus/platform/drivers/rocket/unbind
```

Writing the same name to `bind` puts it back.

**The library refuses to build the fan-out itself.** There is no encoding that avoids the
corruption, and userspace can do nothing about another process. What is left is to stop a
caller walking into it alone. On this part `rocket_open()` counts the bound cores under
`/sys/bus/platform/drivers/rocket`. With more than one bound, it warns once and then returns
`-EBUSY` for the **second** fd. Two fds are what put two jobs in flight.

The `ROCKET_RK3576_ALLOW_MULTI_FD=1` override lifts the refusal, for the probes that provoke
the corruption deliberately and for a caller that has serialized its own submits. The library
leaves one bound core entirely alone. Multi-fd is not just safe there, it is where the 1.5x
lives. The refusal is verified both ways on the part: silent with one core bound, refusing
with two, override honored [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]. The durable fix is still
`status = "disabled"` on the core 1 DT node.

### Corruption pattern

The corruption is gross, not sparse. Checked against a CPU model of the DPU's integer
requant, it reads instead as "a handful of positions off by exactly +/-2". That model
disagrees by one count on a fraction of a percent of elements in every configuration, one
core included. The disagreement is noise larger than the signal the model was used to find.
Check a concurrency question against a solo run of the part, so the requant gap cancels.

Part of that gap is the tie rule. The convertor rounds ties to even, where the models used
for this check rounded half up ([out-cvt-converter.md](../encodings/out-cvt-converter.md)).
The discipline stands whatever the model's remaining error is: a solo run cancels it, and a
model does not.

With unit operands and an identity requant the delta reads directly in MAC terms. The wrong
elements at the boundary of the damage are off by two or three terms, while the bulk are off
by far more. That pattern is a staging corruption rather than an unwritten surface. With each
worker given its own weights, 3.3% of wrong elements hold the *other* worker's correct value,
against 0.4% by chance. That is real cross-talk but not a clean swap, so the dominant effect
is neither job's arithmetic.

### Shared power domain

**The two cores share one power domain**, and that is load-bearing for the wide-output
hazard below. The domain collapses only once both cores are runtime-suspended. So a guard
that waits on one device can let the next submit through while the other core still holds
the domain up. Jobs land on both cores, so either can be the active one at any instant.
Drive and wait on every bound device, not the first. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

## What is shared with the RK3588

Ga Hing Woo reports that the command stream matches the vendor line-for-line once the offset
map is right [source]. The match covers:

- The register command stream content
- The state-init sequence
- The soft-reset and its IOMMU re-attach
- The submit handshake

So the IP-inherent datapath is unchanged, and all of these transfer:

- The matmul-as-1×1-conv mapping
- The CNA→CORE→DPU sequence
- The precision-field encodings
- The BS/BN/EW/LUT semantics
- The tile-layout algebra

The block bases are also shared: within a core, CNA is at +0x1000 and CORE at +0x3000,
exactly as on the RK3588 (pc version register at +0x0).

## CNA register encoding

The RK3576 CNA block uses a different geometry-register encoding from the RK3588's, not a
shifted copy of it. Three things change at once. Some registers move, and several registers
that are unused gaps on the RK3588 are live on the RK3576. At shared offsets the bit-packing
differs.

Barely any CNA geometry register coincides with the RK3588 by both offset and meaning:
`0x1014` (stride) and `0x1110` (weight / DCOMP address) do. **The feature-data address does
not**: the RK3576 carries it at `0x1088` on *both* datapaths and leaves `0x1070` (the
RK3588's slot) at zero. A straight RK3588 regcmd generator therefore miscomputes on the
RK3576.

Ga Hing Woo's `vendor-capture/RK3576_CNA_MAP.md` (github.com/gahingwoo/linux-rk3576-npu)
reverse-engineers the full closed-form RK3576 CNA/CORE/DPU encoder. The method converts the
same Conv2d to `.rknn` one parameter at a time and diffs the vendor RKNN regcmd. A
`predict.py` then validates it, reproducing 34/34 geometry / channel / tiling / const
registers with zero mismatch across 7 MobileNetV1 captures. The captures cover first-conv
ARGB, normal `in_ch>4`, and depthwise, including the pointwise `k=1` case, the
matmul-relevant shape. The map covers the vendor-toolkit conv path, cross-referenced against
what Mesa emits with the RK3588 map. This project has not exercised it on its matmul path or
run it on hardware.

The table gives the CNA delta on the normal `in_ch>4` path. In it `dw` is depthwise, `wbpk`
is weight bytes per kernel (`ic·k·k·2`, or `k·k·4` for dw), and `surf` is
`max(in_w/4, ic/2)`. The RK3588 names come from
[rocket-userspace `include/npu_hw.h`](https://github.com/gregordinary/rocket-userspace/blob/main/include/npu_hw.h):

| Offset | RK3576 field | RK3588 register at that offset |
|---|---|---|
| `0x1014` | `(s<<3)\|s` stride | `CNA_CONV_CON3`, **coincides** (stride) |
| `0x1018` | `0x40000404` (precision/ARGB) | gap |
| `0x101C` | total weight bytes | gap |
| `0x1020` | `(dw?oc:ic)·k·k` | `CNA_DATA_SIZE0` (not DATAIN dims) |
| `0x1024` | `k_word<<16 \| (oc-1)` | `CNA_DATA_SIZE1` |
| `0x1028` | `(in_w·in_h·ic/64)<<16 \| (ic-1)` | `CNA_DATA_SIZE2` |
| `0x102C` | `(in_w-1)<<16 \| (in_h-1)` | `CNA_DATA_SIZE3` |
| `0x1030` | `wbpk<<16 \| (out_w-1)` | `CNA_WEIGHT_SIZE0` |
| `0x1034` | `out_w·out_h - 1` (universal) | `CNA_WEIGHT_SIZE1` |
| `0x1038` | `0x07` const | `CNA_WEIGHT_SIZE2` |
| `0x103C` | `surf<<16` (CBUF surface stride) | gap |
| `0x1044` | `in_w<<16 \| surf` | `CNA_CBUF_CON1` |
| `0x1048` | `0x00071C70` (Mesa hardcodes `0x000E38E0` = 2x -> wrong) | gap |
| `0x1078` | `(in_w-1)<<16 \| (in_h-1)` | `CNA_DMA_CON0` |
| `0x107C` | `ic-1` | `CNA_DMA_CON1` |
| `0x108C` | `0x000F000F` const | gap |
| `0x1090` | `in_w·4` (CBUF input line stride) | gap |
| `0x1094` / `0x1098` | `in_w·in_h` (equal) | gap |
| `0x118C` | `(in_w-1)<<16 \| (in_h-1)` | gap |

The first-conv `in_ch<=4` ARGB path packs about sixteen of these differently again. Its
input is a packed image rather than a cube. So `0x1090` is the packed row's granule count
`in_w·in_ch/16`, and `0x1078` carries that count in its high half instead of the width. It
is its own sub-encoding, written out in full in [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).

The CORE and DPU blocks also re-pack. On the normal path the RK3576 CORE writes the output
geometry at `0x301C` as `(oh_task-1)<<16 | (ow-1)`, an offset the RK3588 uses for
`CORE_CLIP_TRUNCATE`. The DPU carries the same height (`0x4024 = oh_task-1`,
`0x4034 = (oh_task-1)<<16 | (ow-1)`). The DPU `OUT_CVT` triple lands at
`0x40AC`/`0x40B0`/`0x40B4` (RK3588: `0x4080`/`0x4084`/`0x4088`) and the output address at
`0x4018` (RK3588 `DPU_DST_BASE_ADD` is `0x4020`).

The height field is the rows *this task* writes, minus one, and it is not halved. A capture
whose task covers half its plane reads as `(oh-1)>>1`, which looks like a halved field. A
single-task capture of the same conv writes the full `oh-1`. See
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md), which carries the encoding as an implemented emitter.

Still open in the capture are the tiling-derived fields, the `0x1080` surf-stride formula,
and the multi-task slicing. The tiling-derived fields mirror Mesa's CBUF allocator output
(`input_banks`, `data_entries`, atomics), but packed at RK3576 offsets. In the multi-task
slicing, the input-height field carries a per-slice height set by the CBUF allocator.

**The fields the part computes with are narrower than their register halves.** Width and
output channels have 13 bits, the kernel 5 and the stride 3. Past each one a task returns a
wrong surface, and a wrapped width breaks the next job in the process too. The table and the
evidence are in [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md) §"The geometry fields are narrower than
their register halves".

### What the RK3576 map reveals about RK3588 gaps

The RK3576 capture names geometry quantities that the RK3588 must also configure. The
`librocketnpu` RK3588 register map (`npu_hw.h`) leaves them as gaps or drives them as Mesa
hardcodes. They are:

- Input line stride (`0x1090` = `in_w·4`)
- Input CBUF surface stride (`0x103c`)
- Total weight bytes (`0x101c`)
- A CBUF data-entries word (`0x1048`)
- The precision/ARGB format word (`0x1018`)
- Duplicate feature dims (`0x118c`)

Two are direct RK3588 leads. Because the blocks re-pack, each RK3588 equivalent is a
semantic-function match at a different offset. Dumping the `librocketnpu` RK3588 matmul
regcmd on-device settles it [HW sweep, RK1]: the equivalents exist and the generator already
emits them. The line stride (`in_w·4`, RK3576 `0x1090`) is written at `CNA_DMA_CON1`
`0x107c`, and the surface stride (RK3576 `0x103c`) at `CNA_DMA_CON2` `0x1080`.

RK3588 `0x1090` is itself a genuine clock-gating register (`*_DISABLE_CLKGATE` fields per
Mesa `registers.xml`). Both Mesa and the `librocketnpu` generator leave it at its reset
default. So the RK3576's reuse of that offset for line stride is an offset collision, not a
missing RK3588 register. The remaining RK3576-only offsets
(`0x1018`/`0x101c`/`0x1048`/`0x108c`/`0x1094`/`0x1098`/`0x118c`) are absent from Mesa's
RK3588 driver and never emitted by the bit-exact `librocketnpu` matmul. They are RK3576 IP
additions with no RK3588 counterpart.

So the RK3576 map exposes no missing RK3588 register. The apparent gaps are the re-pack
reusing offsets that the RK3588 stream fills elsewhere or correctly leaves at default. That
is verified on the matmul path, and the vendor conv path is separately Mesa-validated. The
`0x1048` slot, where Mesa's RK3588 value is 2x the RK3576 one, is a forward RK3576-porting
concern (Mesa's CBUF-entries math misapplied), not an RK3588 correctness gap. The RK3576
conv capture does not touch the other RK3588 unknowns (`CORE_3030` `0x3030`, `DPU_40C4`
`0x40C4`, `DPU_SURFACE_ADD` `0x40C0`).

## Design implication for rocket-userspace

Supporting the RK3576 needs two per-chip layers, not one:

1. A `rocket_hw_profile` for the RK3576 machine parameters (CBUF geometry, tile caps,
   core count, datatype menu), the mechanism that already exists for the RK3588.
2. A per-chip regcmd encoder for the CNA/CORE/DPU geometry registers, not merely an
   offset remap.

The geometry registers differ from the RK3588's in four ways, so a single
address-translation table is insufficient:

- Offset moves.
- Different bit-packing at shared offsets.
- RK3576-only registers.
- Registers whose field carries a per-task quantity packed differently.

So the geometry-register emission itself is per-chip. What stays shared is the datapath
semantics (the precision-field encodings, the BS/BN/EW/LUT meanings, the CNA→CORE→DPU
block sequence) and the block bases. Those are the IP-inherent parts, and the vendor stream
matches them line-for-line.

That encoder is
[rocket-userspace `src/npu_regcmd_rk3576.c`](https://github.com/gregordinary/rocket-userspace/blob/main/src/npu_regcmd_rk3576.c)
for the int8 conv path. The gate `tests/regcmd_rk3576_gate.c` diffs it register-for-register
against the vendor programs. The encoder starts from the closed-form `predict_cna(params)`
in `RK3576_CNA_MAP.md`. Read that map with the four corrections in
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md). Each of the four, left uncorrected, silently
mis-programs the part.

`rocket_hw_rk3576` carries the machine parameters. Its CBUF budget is measured on the
part: 4096 granules = 256 KiB of feature capacity. That is the default allocation out of a
shared pool of roughly 448 KiB. A granule-allowance field in `CNA_CBUF_CON0` raises the
feature side to 384 KiB against the weight path. The emitter plans that field per task, so
a plane between the two computes exactly instead of corrupting silently (see
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md)).

The matmul tile cap and the weight N-group are measured on this part too, and neither is
the RK3588's. The tile cap, `max_tile`, is 2048 output channels rather than 256, and the
N-group, `ngroup`, is 32 rather than 16. Only `kgroup_4b` is carried over unmeasured,
because no 4-byte input path has run on this part. The chip autodetect selects this profile
and warns which datapaths have an encoder, rather than pretending the part is supported.

The per-chip dispatch that warning describes is enforced rather than advisory, and it
covers the whole op library. The refusal sits at the **generator**: every public `gen_*` in
`npu_regcmd.c`, which is the RK3588 encoder. The op library composes those generators, and
all of these reach the part through them:

- Conv.
- Pooling.
- The LUT activations.
- The norms.
- The FFN.
- Softmax.
- Reduce.
- The ViT encoders.

So one check covers every one of them by construction instead of by enumeration, and every
call site already treats a generator failure as fatal. The gate
`tests/rk3576_refusal_gate.c` holds the refusal. The entries that have their own encoder
(`rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576()` and its int32 sibling) dispatch ahead of it.

The datatype capability mask cannot do that job. It says which *precisions* a chip can run,
and the RK3576 runs int8 and fp16 perfectly well through its own encoder. The check that
belongs at the seam is on the encoding.

Refusing does not make the part usable. The RK3576's own conv encoders compute
bit-exactly, but only the test harnesses and `rocket_matmul_rk3576.c` reach them. The
library's `rocket_conv2d_int8()` and its siblings refuse on this part rather than
dispatching to them. Closing that means lifting the driving logic in
`tests/rk3576_conv_gate.c` into the library: the BO management, the operand packing, and
the `ic` and row planning. That is the largest single gap between the reverse engineering
being done and the chip being usable.

Two details of the reference map to carry into any encoder:

- **The feature-data address is `0x1088` on both CNA datapaths.** `0x1070` reads zero in
  every capture, including the one program that provably needs a non-zero feature address
  (a row-slice whose `0x1088` carries exactly that slice's byte offset). Only the weight
  address (`0x1110`) coincides with the RK3588.
- **The vendor stream carries no PC trailer.** The captured 139-entry program is CNA +
  CORE + DPU + RDMA register writes only. The vendor kernel fires the job through its own
  `enable_mask`/`task_number` in the task descriptor. The RK3588 regcmds in `librocketnpu`
  end with the `OP_ENABLE` trailer that writes `PC_OPERATION_ENABLE`, and under `rocket`
  that write triggers compute. An RK3576 emitter built by transcribing the vendor capture
  must add that trailer back. Without it, the pipeline is configured and never fires.

## The cold-start wall

`PC_TASK_CON` packs `TASK_NUMBER` in the low bits, with `TASK_PP_EN`, `TASK_COUNT_CLEAR`
and `TASK_LAST_LAYER_CLEAR` directly above it. The field is **12 bits wide on the RK3588
and 16 on the RK3576** [source-confirmed]. The vendor `rknpu` config carries the width as
`pc_task_number_bits`, and Rockchip gave the RK3576 bit assignment on the `linux-rockchip`
list [source-confirmed] (see [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md)).

Mainline `rocket` builds the word from the RK3588 accessors unconditionally. So on this
part, `0x7001` asks the PC for a task count of 28673 instead of 1. The PC starts that
program, runs the one task it was handed and is left mid-stream. The first submit of an NPU
power session computes, and every later submit in the same session leaves its output buffer
untouched. A gap past the 50 ms runtime-PM autosuspend hides it, because the power cycle
resets the PC.

Carrying the width per SoC in `rocket_soc_data` and building the word from it clears the
wall. It costs the RK3588 nothing: at 12 bits the driver still emits `0x7001`, bit for bit.
An int8 conv was submitted back to back with no gap. Without the per-SoC width, 1 of 8
submits produced output. With it, 8 of 8 did (32 of 32 on a longer soak), byte-identical to
each other and bit-exact against a CPU model. A row-windowed 112×112 k3 conv is bit-exact
over the whole surface with no inter-task gap [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-25].

Sweeping the control field at each width isolates the cause to `TASK_NUMBER` alone. With
the field correctly placed, every control-bit value tried (`0x0`-`0x3`, `0x5`-`0x7`)
passes. With it misplaced, every non-unit task count fails. The RK3576-only `state_init`
hook in the vendor driver is not needed, and neither is `pc_dma_ctrl`. The driver does need
`pc_task_status_offset`. The register it moves to `0x48` is the live task counter a chained
stream's completion is read from [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-25].

The patch is `rk3576/npu/0008` in the `rk3576-npu` profile. On an unpatched `rocket`, put a
gap between runs in every register sweep on this part. Without one, the sweep measures the
wall instead of the register.

A different open userspace (mesa/rocket + Teflon) first reported the behavior. There, a
chained `task_number=N` submit produced an empty result for every non-first task, and
splitting into independent single-task submits did not help. That effort concluded the arm
was per-power-session rather than per-submit [external, gahingwoo
`MATMUL-PIPELINE-ANALYSIS.md`]. Reproducing it on this project's own encoder and submit
path places it in the driver rather than in the register program. See
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).

Their analysis also settles a structural question. RK3576 matmul is the same
CNA->CBUF->CSC->CORE->DPU pipeline in fully-connected mode, with no separate GEMM unit, as on
the RK3588. So the matmul-as-conv framing of these notes transfers unchanged.

## SoC integration

Everything here is [source-confirmed] from the vendor kernel, the RFC and BL31, per
`notes/rk3576-npu-values.md`:

- **Core bases**: npu0 `0x27700000` (cna `0x27701000`, core `0x27703000`, mmu
  `0x27702000`), and npu1 `0x27708000` (cna `0x27709000`, core `0x2770b000`, mmu
  `0x2770a000`). The MMU is at core+0x2000. The NPU GRF syscon is at `0x26018000`.
  **Cores are 0x8000 apart, inside one 64 KB `RKNN TOP` window**, where the RK3588 uses a
  0x10000 per-core stride.

  The upstream RFC device tree places core 1 at `0x27710000`, which is wrong. The TRM
  memory map gives `RKNN TOP` 64 KB at `0x27700000` and a *separate* `RKNN_NSP` 64 KB block
  at `0x27710000` [TRM]. The vendor kernel's `rknpu` node reads
  `reg = <0x27700000 0x8000>, <0x27708000 0x8000>` [source-confirmed]. On live silicon,
  `0x27708000` returns core 0's exact version pair, while `0x27710000` returns a word that
  changes between boots [HW sweep]. The RFC's own `rknn_mmu_1` at `0x2770a000` is already
  correct, so its core node contradicts the core+0x2000 rule.

  A driver pointed at `0x27710000` drives the NPU MCU subsystem. The version word reads as
  garbage, every job times out, and the ensuing `rocket_core_reset()` takes core 1's IOMMU
  down (`MMU_DTE_ADDR is not functioning`). This project's `patches/rk3576/npu/0007`
  carries the fix.
- **IRQs**: GIC_SPI 247 (core0 + mmu0), 248 (core1 + mmu1). A core and its MMU share the
  line.
- **Power domains**: `RK3576_PD_NPU` / `_NPUTOP` / `_NPU0` / `_NPU1` (the vendor DT
  powers two core domains from one node).
- **Clock**: the compute engine is clocked through a separate, firmware-managed (SCMI)
  domain, floating free of the CBUF bus clock. That is the same clock-parking shape as the
  RK3588. The SCMI NPU clock is `CLK_RKNN_DSU0`. Related gates: `ACLK_RKNN0/1`,
  `HCLK_RKNN_ROOT`, `ACLK_RKNN_CBUF`, `HCLK_RKNN_CBUF`. OPP rates reach ~1 GHz
  (`1000000063` Hz), and a PVTPLL ring/length table is present in the source.
- **Resets**: `SRST_A_RKNN0/1`, `SRST_A/H_RKNN_CBUF`, `SRST_A/H_RKNNTOP_BIU`. There is no
  `SRST_H_RKNN0/1`, unlike on the RK3588. The vendor node lists both core resets and both CBUF
  resets, and its soft reset asserts all four together. `rocket`'s binding names the core's
  `srst_a` alone, so this stack never resets the CBUF short of a power cycle. What that means
  for the poisoning is in [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md) §"A per-job core reset is not
  the way to clear the poisoning".
- **Core work state.** `NPU_GRF_RKNNST` (NPU GRF `0x26018000` + `0x1C`) is read-only, and
  the TRM names bit 0 `core0_work_on` and bit 1 `core1_work_on` [TRM]. Neither `rknpu` nor
  `rocket` reads it. Read from the CPU, it holds 0 while core 0 computes, so it is no
  core-busy witness [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-09-27]. A 20 s loop took 68.6 M
  reads at 292 ns each, and no read had a bit set. The load was a resident fp16 matmul bench
  with a device floor of 35.9 ms a call at 512×1536×8192.

  The control for that negative is the rest of the NPU GRF. Every other documented word
  read its TRM reset value, the non-zero ones included. So the read decodes, and the zero is
  the register's content. The instrument is `rocket-userspace/tools/rk3576-npu-grf.c`. The
  method does not reach a bit behind an enable no driver sets, or a state only the NPU MCU
  raises. The `0x30000055` hold, the DPU drain and a two-core overlap stay with `RAW_STATUS`
  and timing.
- **Read the NPU GRF only while the NPU is powered.** Its bus clock, `pclk_nputop_root`, is
  `rocket`'s `pclk`, so it is gated whenever the device is runtime-suspended, along with all
  four NPU power domains. Hold `power/control` at `on` before a `/dev/mem` read and set it
  back to `auto` after. A read with that clock gated is untested, and a bus stall there is
  [expected].
- **SRAM read margin.** `NPU_GRF_MEM_CON0`-`2` (`0x08`-`0x10`) configure the NPU's SRAMs.
  `MEM_CON0` names a read-margin field `RM` at bits [4:2], where all three reset values hold 4
  [TRM]. The vendor sets `RM` from the rail voltage with the NPU OPP table's
  `volt-mem-read-margin` rows (1 at 855 mV and above, 2 at 765, 3 at 675, 4 at 495)
  [source-confirmed: BSP `rk3576.dtsi`, `rknpu_devfreq.c`, `rockchip_opp_select.c`]. `rocket`
  leaves the reset value, 4, in all three words [HW sweep, 2026-09-27], where the vendor
  programs 3 at 750 mV. The TRM gives no direction, so that 4 is the conservative setting
  is read off the vendor's table, not measured [expected].
- **IOMMU**: per-job attach/detach with an MMU reset per attach, as on the RK3588. The two
  enabling IOMMU fixes are in mainline from v7.3-rc1. `841363ebb508` takes every clock the DT
  lists, and `b10d5920cafa` clears a stale page fault before enabling stall. The upstream
  series carries the pmdomain fixes, a power-on settle delay and a reset cycle at power-on.
- **Power-on settle**: the NPU power domain needs ~15 us of internal preparation after it
  powers on. An access to its QoS registers inside that window hangs the SoC
  [source-confirmed, Rockchip on the
  [v13 thread](https://lore.kernel.org/all/86d0a09c-f1e1-4310-aa39-6526268bdc76@rock-chips.com/)].
  With the delay at 0, the first power-on of the NPU domain takes an asynchronous SError in the
  QoS restore (`rockchip_pd_power` -> `regmap_write`). It does so whether the domain switches
  the rail or `regulator-always-on` holds it up, so the requirement is the domain's and not the
  rail's. The delay at 15 us survived 536 cold power-ons [their HW sweep, gahingwoo board log
  r420, ROCK 4D, 2026-09-24]. Each failing arm is one boot, and no value between 0 and 15 us was
  tried.

## Memory system, NPU rail, and thermal envelope

This section gives board-level machine parameters, from the Rockchip RK3576 Hardware
Design Guide V1.1 [source-confirmed] plus live readings on the H96 MAX M9. Read an NPU
number on this part against these three platform facts. Each differs from the RK3588 in a
way that matters.

### DRAM bandwidth

The RK3576's DRAM bandwidth is half the RK3588's, at an identical clock.

| | RK3588 | RK3576 |
|---|---|---|
| Bus width | **64-bit**, 4x 16-bit channels | **32-bit**, 2x 16-bit channels |
| Max capacity | 32 GB | 16 GB (8 GB per channel) |
| Standards | LPDDR4/4X/5 | LPDDR4/4X/5 |
| PHY clock | 2112 MHz | 2112 MHz |
| Theoretical peak | ~33.8 GB/s | **~16.9 GB/s** |

The bus width is [source-confirmed] from each part's design guide. The RK3576 guide's
§2.1.7.1 reads "a 32-bit data bus width, composed of 2 16bits DDR channels". The RK3588
guide reads "64bits data bus width, composed of 4 16bits DDR channels".

The clock is identical on both. RK3588 `VDD_DDR_S0` is specified at 2112 MHz, and
`scmi_clk_ddr` reads exactly 2112000000 Hz on the H96 MAX M9 [live 2026-07-29]. So the
ratio is exactly 2x, with no clock difference confounding it. **An RK3576 has half the DRAM
bandwidth of the part every RK3588 number in these notes was measured on.**

Measured, on the H96 MAX M9 (8 GB, 256 MB buffers, `memcpy`, pinned to the A72 cluster,
counting read + write traffic) [live 2026-07-29]:

| Threads | Achieved |
|---|---|
| 1 | 9.0 GB/s |
| 2 | 8.7 GB/s |
| 4 | 8.3 GB/s |

Two things follow. Achieved streaming bandwidth is ~53% of theoretical peak, the usual
figure for a copy benchmark on LPDDR. And **one A72 thread already saturates the bus**:
more threads make it slightly worse, not better. That is the signature of a fully
consumed resource.

This is the mechanism behind three separately measured behaviors on this part:

- **The int32 writers' atom drop tracks DDR traffic.** It rises from 5.8% of row tasks on
  a quiet host to 51.7% under a host streaming `memcpy` to DDR. An integer spin (11.7%) and
  an L1-resident `memcpy` (9.2%) barely move it. A single host thread streaming to DDR
  consumes the whole bus. The NPU's write DMA is then contending for a resource with
  nothing left in it. The load ladder also rules thermal out as the confound: integer spin
  heats the CPU at least as hard and does not move the rate.
- **The DPU write drain is long here.** In `RAW_STATUS`, `DPU_0`/`DPU_1` set 82-343 us
  after `PC_DONE`, scaling with the bytes in flight. The drain reaches 745 us on the int8
  stem at 224×224 k7 s2, which is why `dpu_grace_us` has to default to 500 on this part. A
  bus half as wide predicts about twice the RK3588's drain for the same surface [inferred
  from the width and the clock, not separately isolated].
- The host cube pack runs within about 2x of a straight copy. The pack blocks its scatters
  into `memcpy` runs, which are block moves rather than per-element index calls. A NEON
  16×16 block does the CHW <-> NC1HWC2 transpose. Measured, the conv path's feature scatter
  and output de-scatter run at 2.1-2.4 GB/s. This part's single-thread `memcpy` ceiling is
  4.4 GB/s (`build/membench`, 256 MB).

  So the pack is bulk copying against a bus one thread already saturates. Threading the
  pack is not a lever, and neither is more vectorization. The remaining lever is moving
  fewer bytes. Packing a layer's weights once through a handle collected that lever (98.8 ms
  to 33.6). Keeping tensors in cube layout between consecutive layers would collect it next.

### NPU supply rails

The RK3576's NPU has no separate memory rail [source-confirmed, both design guides].

| | RK3588 | RK3576 |
|---|---|---|
| NPU rails | `VDD_NPU` **+ `VDD_NPU_MEM`** (405 mA, separately regulatable) | `NPU_DVDD` only |
| Peak | 0.850 V / 4.00 A / 3.40 W at 1000 MHz | 0.800 V / 4.00 A / **3.20 W** at 1000 MHz |
| Supply | DC/DC BUCK | RK806S-5 BUCK2 (5 A rating) |
| Main capacitance | > 200 uF | > 90 uF |

The RK3588 gives every major block its own memory rail: `VDD_NPU_MEM`, `VDD_GPU_MEM`,
`VDD_CPU_BIG0/1_MEM`, `VDD_CPU_LIT_MEM`, `VDD_VDENC_MEM` [source-confirmed, both design
guides]. Its guide notes that `VDD_NPU_MEM` "can be combined with `VDD_NPU`" only where
power consumption is not a concern. **The RK3576 has exactly one MEM rail in the whole
part, `LOGIC_MEM_DVDD`, and no NPU equivalent.** The NPU's internal memory arrays, the CBUF
among them, are supplied from `NPU_DVDD` itself.

So on this part no supply can hold NPU array state up while the logic drops. That is
consistent with two findings. The register sweep established independently that the
wide-output poison state lives in an NPU-internal memory array rather than in a register.
The canary already writes both of the implicated bits at their int8 values. And a
runtime-PM power cycle is the only thing that clears that state.

The missing rail does not by itself explain the cycle's ~87% success rate. Runtime PM gates
the on-die power domain, not the external rail. So this is a supporting fact for that
mechanism and not a measurement of it [hypothesis].

Note also that `NPU_DVDD` peaks at ~80% of BUCK2's 5 A rating, so board headroom above
the specified operating point is thin.

### NPU thermal throttling

The RK3576 has no NPU throttle between idle and a critical thermal event.

| | RK3588 | RK3576 |
|---|---|---|
| Package | EHS-FCBGA | EHS-FCCSP698L, 16.1 × 17.2 mm |
| θJA | 8.7 C/W | **15.84 C/W** |

The θJA figures are from both design guides [source-confirmed]. Each is simulated on a
JEDEC 2S2P board with no heat sink, so it is a reference figure rather than a board number.
The smaller package sheds heat about 1.8x worse for the same watts.

The kernel exposes six thermal zones (`package`, `bigcore`, `littlecore`, `gpu`, `npu`,
`ddr`), so the NPU has its own sensor. But `npu-thermal` carries one trip point, type
`critical`, at 115 C, with zero cooling devices bound to it [live 2026-07-29]. The only
three cooling devices on the part are `cpufreq-cpu0`, `cpufreq-cpu4` and
`devfreq-27800000.gpu` [live 2026-07-29]. The design guide says the same thing from the
other side: its SDK "provides temperature control strategies specifically for the CPU and
GPU."

**So nothing throttles the NPU. Between its idle temperature and a 115 C critical event
there is no intermediate response.** Idle is 35-38 C across all six zones on this box.
Sustained gate runs reach 40-43 C with every cooling device at state 0. So there is wide
margin in practice, but the margin is all there is.

A workload that pins the NPU rail and the A72s together loads two rails at once. It does so
on a package that sheds heat 1.8x worse than the RK1. This part's host-bound library calls
do that. The design guide is explicit that per-module peak currents "cannot simply be summed
up as the SoC's peak current" for thermal purposes. Watch die temperature on long sweeps
rather than assuming the RK1's headroom.

## Live-hardware notes (H96 MAX M9, RK3576)

Observed on a real RK3576 box (H96 MAX M9, `rockchip,rk3576`, 8 GB, mainline Debian
forky/sid, kernel 7.1.3-1-arm64 PREEMPT_RT). The device enumerates, and jobs submit and
complete. With this chip's own encoder, the part computes int8 convolutions bit-exactly
[observed 2026-07-24/25].

### Driver and device-tree state

With `CONFIG_DRM_ACCEL=y` + `CONFIG_DRM_ACCEL_ROCKET=m` and the RK3576 NPU device-tree
nodes enabled, `rocket` probes and `/dev/accel/accel0` comes up
(`/sys/devices/platform/rknn/accel/accel0`, `MODALIAS=platform:rknn`). The device
`27700000.npu` is bound, and both NPU power domains are attached and runtime-suspended at
idle.

**The image's in-tree `rocket` carries `patches/rk3576/npu/0001-0012` but none of
`0013`, `0014` or `0020`, so it has three memory-safety defects any `render`-group client
reaches.** One malformed submit oopses it (`0013`). A client that submits and closes
without waiting walks a use-after-free in the IOVA allocator (`0014`). Any submit the
kernel rejects for its task array frees that array twice (`0020`). That corrupts the
kmalloc-16 freelist, so the fault lands in whatever allocates next.

Both, the paths that reach them, and the A/B that verifies each are in
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md), under these sections:

- "A rejected submit oopses the kernel"
- "A BO in an in-flight job must survive the file that made it"
- "The unprivileged double free, and the crashes it was mistaken for"

Until the image is rebuilt, do not measure on a kernel that has taken one Oops. Gates still
run afterwards, but three Oopses take the file-close path into the IOMMU layer, and the box
needs a reboot.

Both patches are in `series/rk3576-npu.toml`. Before 2026-07-28 that file listed the series
only to `0012`, so an image built before that date silently leaves `0013` out. When asking
what an image carries, check the series file, not the directory.

The accel device rocket registers is a synthetic `platform:rknn` aggregate with **no
`of_node`**, so the chip cannot be identified from `/sys/class/accel/accel0/device`. Read
the compatible from the driver-bound per-core device instead
(`/sys/bus/platform/drivers/rocket/27700000.npu/of_node/compatible`), or fall back to the
SoC-level `/proc/device-tree/compatible`. This is what librocketnpu's chip autodetect
does.

DT nodes as instantiated: `npu@27700000` and `npu@27708000`, both
`compatible = "rockchip,rk3576-rknn-core"`, and `iommu@27702000` / `iommu@2770a000`,
`compatible = "rockchip,rk3576-iommu", "rockchip,rk3568-iommu"`. The core 0 and MMU
addresses and the interrupts (GIC_SPI 247 / 248) are confirmed on live silicon by
`rocket` binding and computing there. Core 1's address is confirmed by reading it, not by
the node existing. A DT unit-address is only a claim, and the RFC's was wrong (see "Core
bases"). The NPU regulator `vdd_npu_s0` is PMIC `dcdc-reg2` (750 mV nominal, 550-950 mV).

Two device-tree details are required to get there, and both are easy to get wrong:

- **List both NPU power domains on core 0.** `rocket_core_init()` calls
  `devm_pm_domain_attach_list()`. The driver core auto-attaches a node with a *single*
  `power-domains` entry first. The explicit attach then fails with `-EEXIST`
  (`failed to attach NPU power domains`, probe error -17), and no `/dev/accel` appears.
  Listing two entries (`power-domains = <&power RK3576_PD_NPU0>, <&power RK3576_PD_NPU1>`)
  suppresses the auto-attach, and the explicit attach succeeds.

  This matches the vendor, which powers both NPU domains from one node even when computing
  on a single core. The CBUF->CMAC read path is only fully powered with NPU1 up.
- **Keep core 1 and its IOMMU disabled** for single-core bring-up. Core 0 holds both
  domains.

Two further gaps matter on a bring-up kernel, and the NPU rail has two routes past it:

- **The NPU rail can have no consumer, and `npu-supply` alone will not fix it.** With
  neither `npu-supply` on the NPU node nor `domain-supply` on the power domain, nothing
  claims `vdd_npu_s0`. The regulator core then disables it as unused late in boot
  (`vdd_npu_s0: disabling` at t~33 s). Probe at t~8 s survives only because it runs first.
  The trap is that adding `npu-supply` is inert on a stock kernel: mainline `rocket` in 7.1
  has no regulator support at all. The module `rocket.ko` references no `regulator_*`
  symbol, and the RK3588 `npu-supply` handling is the out-of-tree `082-rocket-drv-npu-volt`
  patch.

  So no consumer ever appears, and the rail still drops. What holds the rail under a stock
  driver is `regulator-always-on` on the PMIC node. Set both: `npu-supply` describes the
  relationship for a regulator-aware driver, and `regulator-always-on` keeps the rail up.
  With both, `vdd_npu_s0` stays `enabled` at 750 mV past boot [live].
- The other route makes the power domain the rail's consumer. The pmdomain driver looks up
  `domain-supply` only on a domain it marks as needing a regulator. The upstream series
  adds that mark for the NPU domain. With that kernel change, `regulator-boot-on` and no
  `regulator-always-on`, the rail follows the domain, and `rocket` still needs no regulator
  support [live 2026-10-02, kernel 7.2.8-1].
- The driver can map fewer register banks than the DT provides. Here the DT supplies five
  banks, but `/proc/iomem` shows only `pc`, `cna` and `core` mapped. The DPU / DPU-RDMA
  mapping support is a separate patch and was absent from this build. This does not block
  compute. The RK3588 `rocket` maps the same three and runs DPU work, because the DPU is
  programmed through the regcmd stream rather than by the CPU. Jobs submit and complete on
  this build with only three banks mapped.

### Block bases

The node `reg-names` are `pc`, `cna`, `core`, `dpu`, `dpu_rdma` at core +0x0000, +0x1000,
+0x3000, +0x4000, +0x5000. So the RK3576 block bases match the RK3588's exactly (PC
`0x0xxx`, CNA `0x1xxx`, CORE `0x3xxx`, DPU `0x4xxx`, DPU-RDMA `0x5xxx`). That is
independent live confirmation that the re-pack is *within* the CNA block, not a relocation
of the blocks [live, device tree].

The binding maps more banks than the RK3588's. The RK3588 `rocket` DT exposes only
`pc`/`cna`/`core` (`fdab0000.npu pc` / `cna` / `core` in `/proc/iomem` on an RK1). That is
a driver-binding difference. The CPU only maps what it touches directly, and DPU and PPU
registers are programmed through the regcmd stream, not MMIO. Neither chip's DT maps a PPU
bank (`0x6xxx`/`0x7xxx`). So the RK3576 node list says nothing about whether the RK3576 has
a PPU pooling engine, and that must be established separately.

### Clocks, resets, power

- **Both cores take the same six clocks** (`aclk hclk npu pclk aclk_cbuf hclk_cbuf`).
  The upstream RFC device tree lists only four on core 1, and that is an omission rather
  than a hardware asymmetry. The CRU has exactly one `ACLK_RKNN_CBUF` and one
  `HCLK_RKNN_CBUF` (there is no `..._CBUF1`). The vendor kernel lists them once on an
  aggregate node covering both cores, and the RFC's own `rknn_mmu_1` and
  `power-domain@RK3576_PD_NPU1` both list them.

  It reads as an oversight nobody upstream would have hit, since the RFC ships both cores
  `status = "disabled"`. It is load-bearing because `rocket_core_init()` hard-gets all six
  with `devm_clk_bulk_get()` for every core. So core 1 fails probe with
  `-ENOENT: Failed to get clk 'aclk_cbuf'` until the node lists them. The patch
  `patches/rk3576/npu/0007` adds them.
- `reset-names` is `srst_a` only. The RK3576 has no `SRST_H_RKNN0/1` analog, and the
  binding carries no `srst_h` slot. The live binding settles what the vendor-kernel
  extraction left unverified.
- The NPU CRU clocks are present and named as expected: `clk_rknn_dsu0` (the SCMI NPU
  clock), `aclk_rknn0`, `aclk_rknn1`, `aclk_rknn_cbuf`, `hclk_rknn_root`, `hclk_rknn_cbuf`,
  `pclk_nputop_root`, plus `scmi_clk_npu`. This confirms the clock symbol names given under
  SoC integration.
- **Power domains**: `npu` -> `nputop` -> `npu0`, `npu1` (hierarchical, two compute
  domains), all `off` at idle. This confirms the 2-core structure.
- **The clock does not park.** `scmi_clk_npu` / `clk_rknn_dsu0` hold one rate with the NPU
  power domains off and with them on and jobs running. With no `assigned-clock-rates`
  that rate is ~786 MHz (786431952 / 786431998 Hz), sampled once per second across a
  200-submit loop and after it drained [live]. With `assigned-clock-rates = <594000000>` it is
  594000000 Hz, at rest and in 91 samples across two running networks
  [live 2026-10-02, kernel 7.2.8-1]. So the RK3588 behavior (boots at 200 MHz, needs the clock
  patch to reach 600 MHz) does not transfer: there is no RK3576 analog of the
  `POWER_DOWN_FREQ`-style park to undo. No clock patch is needed for the RK3576 to run at its
  boot rate.
- **The NPU has no DVFS at all in this kernel.** There is no NPU `devfreq` node and no
  OPP table exposed: `/sys/class/devfreq/` carries only `27800000.gpu` (800 MHz, with a
  300-950 MHz rate list). The NPU sits at one rate for a whole boot, the one the device tree
  assigns or the boot firmware leaves. The rail `vdd_npu_s0` sits at 750 mV against a
  550-950 mV range [live 2026-07-29, and 2026-10-02 on kernel 7.2.8-1].
- **A number's clock is in its tag, and 786 MHz at 750 mV is the cell where an external
  project measures two-core corruption.** Every measurement tagged 786 MHz ran with no
  `assigned-clock-rates` and the rail pinned by `regulator-always-on`: 750 mV, `enabled`,
  `num_users=1`, 786431952 Hz [live 2026-09-25, kernel 7.2.3-1]. That corruption vanishes at
  594 MHz / 750 mV and at 786 MHz / 800 mV. Any single-core result tagged 786 MHz was taken
  at that operating point too, so it bounds more than the two-core work.
- **With `domain-supply` on the NPU power domain and no `regulator-always-on`, the rail
  power-cycles with the workload.** `vdd_npu_s0` reads `disabled`, `num_users=0` at rest, and
  `enabled`, `num_users=1`, 750 mV with a job in flight [live 2026-10-02, kernel 7.2.8-1]. The
  driver's autosuspend delay is 50 ms, so the rail drops in any gap between submits longer
  than that. A snapshot 12 s into a network's all-passes run found the domain off and the rail
  disabled. Across a `bench 100` run, 80 of 81 samples found both on. A job submitted from
  rail-off computes, and the seven jobs that follow it match it byte for byte.
- **Before the first job of a boot, that rail reads `enabled`, `num_users=1` with the device
  runtime-suspended.** `npu`, `nputop` and `npu0` stay powered from boot until the first
  runtime-PM cycle. Their genpd `active_time` equalled a 49-hour uptime, where `npu1`'s was
  143 s [live 2026-10-02, kernel 7.2.8-1]. So read the at-rest state after a job has run.
- **The 786 MHz walls do not describe a board at 594 MHz.** The four one-kick graphs rise
  0.6-1.4 ms, 12-21%. Scaling each graph's execution term (2.25-5.75 ms at 786 MHz) by the
  clock ratio predicts 0.7-1.9 ms, so the clock is the likely cause [hypothesis]. The library
  and the kernel also differ between the two columns, so the clock is not isolated.

  The 786 MHz column is 2026-08-03, and the 594 MHz column is four or five runs a network
  [live 2026-10-02, kernel 7.2.8-1, `rocket` 1.6.0]. The measurement is
  `rk3576_net_gate bench 100`, with resident weights and governor `ondemand`. The 594 MHz
  image boots with `cpuidle.off=1` on the kernel command line, a second condition the
  786 MHz column did not have:

  | Network | 786 MHz | 594 MHz |
  |---|---:|---:|
  | MobileNetV1 | 5.0 ms | 5.6-5.7 ms |
  | MobileNetV2 | 6.5-6.6 ms | 7.5-7.7 ms |
  | ResNet-18 | 6.6 ms | 7.9-8.0 ms |
  | Inception V1 | 7.0-7.1 ms | 8.2 ms |
  | Inception V3 | 33.4-33.7 ms | 37.4-40.5 ms |

- **The vendor's voltage for this clock depends on the chip's bin, and mainline does not read
  the bin.** The vendor kernel picks the bin from a PVTPLL oscillator count at NPU GRF offset
  `0x54`, with the NPU forced to 800 MHz and 750 mV. A higher count selects a higher bin and a
  lower voltage [source-confirmed: BSP `drivers/soc/rockchip/rockchip_opp_select.c`, which logs
  `pvtm-volt-sel=`]. Mainline takes no such reading, so only a vendor-kernel boot log of the same
  board gives its bin. Against the step at or above each clock, 594 MHz at 750 mV is inside the
  table at every bin. At 786 MHz and 750 mV it is inside only at `L5` and above
  [source-confirmed: BSP `rk3576.dtsi` `npu_opp_table`, RK3576 rows only]:

  | Bin | 600 MHz step | 800 MHz step |
  |---|---|---|
  | `L0`-`L1` | 737.5 mV | 800 mV |
  | `L2` | 725 mV | 787.5 mV |
  | `L3` | 725 mV | 775 mV |
  | `L4` | 725 mV | 762.5 mV |
  | `L5` | 725 mV | 750 mV |
  | `L6` | 725 mV | 737.5 mV |
  | `L7`-`L10` | 725 mV | 725 mV |

- The part variants stop lower. That table is the standard part's. The variant the vendor
  table tags `S` tops out at 500 MHz. The J and M variants have their own rows to 850 MHz.
  `rk3576j.dtsi` disables 800 and 850 MHz there as overdrive that shortens the chip's life,
  which leaves 700 MHz [source-confirmed: BSP `rk3576.dtsi` and `rk3576j.dtsi`,
  `develop-6.12`].

- The specified operating point is 1000 MHz at 800 mV, and the CRU's OPP data reaches
  ~1 GHz (`1000000063` Hz) [source-confirmed: RK3576 Hardware Design Guide V1.1
  peak-current table]. So there is roughly 1.27x of clock headroom above 786 MHz and 1.68x
  above 594 MHz. The regulator range covers the voltage it needs [source-confirmed: RK3576
  Hardware Design Guide V1.1 peak-current table]. Reaching that operating point means
  `assigned-clock-rates` in the device tree, which is how 594 MHz is set. The other route is an SCMI `set_rate`
  from inside the driver, as on the RK3588. There is no sysfs interface to it.

  The higher clock is unlikely to be the lever on this part. The submit is 22% of library
  wall time against the host's 78%. Within a submit, the floor is the completion poll and
  the DPU drain rather than MAC throughput. Unmeasured.
- The CBUF is clocked as one unit for both cores. There is exactly one
  `aclk_rknn_cbuf` (the NPU rate, 786 or 594 MHz) and one `hclk_rknn_cbuf` (198 MHz) in the
  tree, with no per-core variant [live]. This agrees with the TRM's shared 16-bank CBUF clock
  gate and with the absence of any per-core CBUF field in the NPU GRF. It is consistent with the
  two-core corruption being contention on one shared CBUF datapath [hypothesis]. But it is
  equally consistent with the undervolt hypothesis above, which this measurement does not
  separate it from.
- Power domains cycle per job. The domains `npu`/`nputop`/`npu0`/`npu1` are all `off` at
  idle and all read `on` while jobs are in flight, then return to `off`. So the driver's
  runtime-PM path works on this SoC [live].

### Host CPU

The host is 4x Cortex-A72 + 4x Cortex-A53 big.Little (`0xd08` / `0xd03`). Pin host fan-out
for NPU offload to the A72 cluster, the analog of the RK3588's A76 recipe.

Both cpufreq policies default to `ondemand`, with a 408 MHz floor and maxima of 2016 and
2208 MHz [live 2026-09-25]. The RK3588 board with the same floor lost 3.2x to an unpinned
governor ([../perf/cpu-governor-and-offload.md](../perf/cpu-governor-and-offload.md)). The loss
on this part is not measured.

**The two clusters have different caches, and reading the wrong one is easy.** The
kernel's sysfs indexes them per CPU, so `cpu0` is the A53, and only `cpu4`-`cpu7` describe
the cluster the pinned host work runs on. Measured from sysfs on the H96 MAX M9 [HW sweep]:

| | L1d | L1i | L2 (unified, cluster-shared) |
|---|---|---|---|
| A72 (`cpu4-7`) | 32 KiB, 2-way, 256 sets | 48 KiB, 3-way | **1024 KiB, 16-way, 1024 sets**, `shared_cpu_list 4-7` |
| A53 (`cpu0-3`) | 32 KiB, 4-way, 128 sets | 32 KiB, 2-way | 512 KiB, 16-way, 512 sets, `shared_cpu_list 0-3` |

Two consequences follow for any locality argument about pinned host work. The A72 L2 way is
64 KiB, so a 4 KiB page has 16 physical colors (physical address bits `[15:12]`), not the 8
the A53's 32 KiB way gives. And that 1 MiB is shared by all four A72s, so a four-thread host
loop contends with itself in it: the per-thread share is 256 KiB.

### Dispatch spine

The dispatch spine is independent of the encoder. The library `librocketnpu` builds
unmodified on the RK3576 (gcc 15.3 / cmake 4.3, `libdrm-dev` plus the kernel's own
`drm/rocket_accel.h`), and its fp16 matmul test submits successfully. With the RK3588
regcmd encoder driving RK3576 silicon, a submission establishes two things:

- The whole dispatch spine works. `DRM_IOCTL_ROCKET_SUBMIT` returns 0, the power domains
  come up, and the job runs. A `PREP_BO` on the output BO returns 0: the completion fence
  signals, so the DPU raised its completion IRQ. There is no `NPU job timed out`, no IOMMU
  fault and no error in `dmesg` across hundreds of submits (M=4/K=32/N=16 and
  M=64/K=128/N=64). BO allocation, IOMMU mapping, regcmd fetch, PC execution, and the
  IRQ/fence path all behave as on the RK3588 [live].
- **The DPU writes nothing.** The output BO comes back untouched (still the 0xAA
  sentinel). It holds no garbage and no partial result: there is no write at all. This is
  the predicted consequence of the re-packed geometry registers. The RK3588 program lands
  its output dimensions in offsets that mean something else on this part, so the output
  surface is mis-sized to nothing [live].

Nothing outside the per-chip geometry encoder is in doubt on the RK3576. Compute is gated on
that encoder alone, not on the driver, the uAPI, the memory path or the interrupt path. That
is what made the int8 conv encoder the whole of the bring-up work. It is also why an
operation that still emits the RK3588 encoding writes nothing at all rather than something
close.

`librocketnpu` reads the NPU's DT compatible at device open, selects this chip's profile,
and warns which paths have no RK3576 encoder yet. That makes the silence diagnosable rather
than mysterious. For bring-up, force a profile with `ROCKET_CHIP=<name>`.

### State and next steps

The device enumerates, and jobs submit and complete. The clock and power behavior are
characterized, and an RK3576 int8 conv encoder exists and runs on the part.
Thermals stay 44-49 C through submit loops (this box is limited to probing, so nothing
here is a performance measurement).

#### Direct int8 convolution

The part computes convolutions. With the RK3576 encoder, a k=3 SAME 32×32 int8 conv at
IC=OC=32, driven by a dense random weight set, reproduces the CPU model bit-exactly. It
matches over the whole surface (32768/32768, reproducible). An unpadded conv over a dense
feature ramp is bit-exact as well (28800/28800). So the encoder programs all of these
correctly:

- The CNA operand DMA
- The CBUF staging
- The CSC
- The CMAC
- The BS bias add
- The OUT_CVT requant
- The output geometry
- The writer

The weight cube is the RK3588's. The envelope reaches:

- IC and OC from 8 to 128
- Planes from 8×8 to 128×64
- Kernels k1/k3/k5
- Stride 1 and 2
- VALID and SAME
- The full signed int8 feature range
- The padded border

The emitter closes three boundaries by construction. Both channel counts are *programmed*
as multiples of 32. The feature plane stays inside the CBUF budget. The resident weight
slice stays inside what the output-channel group count allows. The encoding, the gate, the
corrections to the published map, and what the hardware settles are in
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).

A plane past the CBUF budget still runs. The emitter cuts the conv into row windows. Each
task reads a window of the input rows and writes the output rows it supports. The split
reproduces the CPU model bit-exactly over the whole surface at every shape tried. That
includes 112×112 and 224×224 at ic=32, which have no single-task plan at all. Driving the
row window also determines the three registers that take a second value only on a windowed
program (`0x40b8`, `0x1018`, `0x1038`).

Each window is its own submit, which costs about 1.2 ms. The driver's completion poll sets
that cost, not the silicon. A split therefore scales linearly in windows, and the planner's
job is to use the fewest that fit.

The direct path is swept gap-free, and its boundaries are closed by construction. With a
`rocket` that carries the per-SoC `PC_TASK_CON` width, the whole envelope runs in one NPU
power session. It runs with no inter-shape gap and no wall retries. That removes the doubt
the retry compensation attached to every result taken through it. The sweep passes 102
shapes in 4.6 s, 67 of them direct. It covers:

- The envelope
- The row window as a matrix
- The channel-group jump across group count and kernel size
- The weight-slice boundary, asserted in both directions

Inside the weight-slice boundary the shapes compute bit-exactly, and past it the shapes are
refused rather than run. The sweep sharpens two limits. The first is the resident weight
slice: `ic*kh*kw <= 4608` drives every output channel, and past it the trailing groups are
lost one at a time. The second is an API trap in the row planner, where a single-task plan
can still carry a window shorter than the plane. Both are in
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).

#### Depthwise convolution

Depthwise computes bit-exactly over 35 gate shapes:

- Channel counts 8 to 256, including those that are not multiples of 32
- Kernels 1 through 7
- Both strides, SAME and VALID
- Planes to 112×112, including odd ones
- Shapes across a row window

Its register program comes from manufactured captures at 22 channel counts. Those captures
fix four formulas that the found captures' confounds leave open:

- The channel field in `0x4050`
- Two different channel granules
- The destination surface's round-up to four
- `0x118C`, which is `iw-1` in both halves

A corpus of square captures reads `0x118C` as the plane height.

**Register fidelity is not enough, and depthwise shows why.** A `.rknn` carries a register
program and says nothing about the memory those registers address. Two of this path's
buffers differ from the direct path's, and neither is in any capture. The coefficient group
is 48 bytes for 8 output channels with no weight-zero-point field, where the direct group
is 64 with one. The int8 weight cube groups channels by 64 with two live bytes per channel
per tap. The float cube instead groups by 32, with one 16-bit slot.

Both were read off the part. Both are silent when wrong. A wrong coefficient group writes a
correctly sized, mostly empty surface, the same signature a wrong geometry register gives.
The layouts and the probes that decoded them are in [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).

One consequence reaches every caller: **the depthwise output surface stride is
`round4(ow*oh_full)` elements**, not the plane. Size the output BO and de-scatter with
`rocket_rk3576_out_surf_elems()`. An assumed plane stride is invisible at 16×16 and
112×112 and wrong at 15×18.

#### Documentary sources

Two negatives are worth not re-deriving. First, **the RK3576 TRM carries no NPU register
chapter.** Part 1 and Part 2 hold the memory map, the interrupt table and the CRU clock
tree, and nothing on the CNA/CORE/DPU registers. The memory map places `RKNN TOP` 64 KB at
`0x27700000` and `RKNN_NSP` 64 KB at `0x27710000`. That is worth knowing, since it is
where a mis-addressed core 1 lands. Unlike the RK3588's encoding, this one has no
documentary path, and the captures are its only source.

Second, **the `.rknn` file holds the *stored* register program.** The vendor runtime
patches values into it at load time: the addresses, and at least `0x1064`. A register that
reads 0 in a decoded capture is therefore not known to be 0 on the wire.

#### fp16 convolution

The fp16 program is a transcription, not a sweep. Every `.rknn` on hand was int8, so its
precision fields had no oracle and were inferred from the RK3588's field packing. A float
capture can be manufactured, though: `do_quantization=False` on a float ONNX makes
RKNN-Toolkit2 emit a genuine fp16 conv program for this part
(`tests/data/rk3576-vendor-capture/float/mkfloat.py`).

An fp16 convolution computes bit-faithfully at any input-channel count, delivers every
output channel, and a caller can drive it. The generator `gen_conv2d_fp16_rk3576()` emits
the one task this program computes exactly, which is 16 input channels. The planner
`rocket_rk3576_plan_ic()` lays an arbitrary `ic` out as that many slices, summed on the
