host. The bound is the program's and not the part's: a matmul-form fp16 program contracts
2048 in one job (see "The precision fork"). **Anything outside the envelope is refused
rather than warned about.** The reason is that a wrong `ic` writes a full, correctly sized,
wrong surface with nothing to fault on.

**Three registers carry the float mode, and each is inert while either of the others is
wrong.** They are CNA `0x100C` = `bit21 | proc(fp16)<<7 | in(fp16)<<4`, DPU `0x4038` low
half `0x0092`, and DPU `0x4050` with bit 31 clear. With any one of them at its integer
value, the contraction reads every feature surface twice and skips the odd ones. A
single-register sweep therefore cannot find the mode. Two of the three registers are also
out of reach of a single-*bit* sweep from the integer word. Of the 139-word program,
fourteen registers differ from the integer one, and seven move with `ic`, which is what
makes the diff readable.

The float weight cube groups both channel axes by 16, not by the int8 cube's 32. An fp16
*output* needs the float narrowing at DPU `0x40B0` bit 16, because `0x4010` bits [31:29]
select an output width and not a conversion.

The DPU's output element stride is `16/ic` words, and that one fact accounts for two
output gaps. At `ic = 8` the stride is two words per element: the writer spends four bytes
on a two-byte element, and only `oc/2` channels reach DDR. At `ic = 32` two channels
collide in one slot and the odd ones are lost. At `ic = 16` the stride is exactly one: the
surface is the plain native cube, with every programmed channel present exactly once.
[source-confirmed, HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-26]

The bound is therefore a contraction width, not a writer defect. The split around it
reproduces the model exactly at `ic` 16/32/64/128 crossed with `k` 1/3/5. **A probe that
holds the feature uniform in one axis cannot see this bound.** Under such a probe, this
datapath looks exact and is not. Details, the probe set and the trap list are in
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).
[source-confirmed, HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-26]

The split costs `ic/16` submits at about 1.4-1.5 ms of NPU wall each, flat in the work. At
56×56, `k = 3` does nine times the multiply-accumulates of `k = 1` and takes the same 6 ms.
No compute term is visible at these shapes. The cost is the per-submit dispatch floor, the
same 1.1-1.3 ms the int8 path pays once for its whole `ic`. Against an 8-channel split,
the 16-channel contraction halves both the submit count and the NPU wall.

Host packing and de-scatter add a real share at a full plane, and the split multiplies it
too: `ic/16` weight repacks and `ic/16` de-scatters. The host column varies by a factor of
two run to run on this thermally limited board, so only the NPU column is a measurement.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-26]

#### First conv on a packed image

The first conv also computes in fp16, so a vision stem runs. A convolution whose input
carries four or fewer channels is a different program from the one above. The CNA reads a
packed image straight out of DDR and expands every pixel to four lanes. One task therefore
contracts the whole image, with no input-channel split to make. The entry
`rocket_conv2d_fp16_rk3576()` takes the same row-major CHW tensor as every other entry. It
owns the interleave, the lane cube, the output-channel split and the de-scatter.

It is bit-exact against a CPU model over 24 gate shapes [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]:

- Image channels 1 through 4
- Kernels 1/3/5/7
- Both strides, SAME and VALID
- Planes 16×32 through 224×224
- Output channels 16 through 96

A vision model opens with the 224×224 stride-2 stem. That stem has no single-task plan
(6272 CBUF granules against a 6144 ceiling), so it runs through the row window. Rows and
output channels then split together on the two axes. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

Three things must be right together. The first is the float weight cube: four lanes per
(output channel, tap), with output channels interleaved by sixteen inside a tap. It was
decoded from manufactured captures. The second is the register program, which at fp16 is a
different program, not the int8 one at another precision:

- It has no channel fold.
- The CBUF entry count loses the fold's `+1` lookahead.
- The row stride takes the element size.
- The CVT is bypassed rather than configured.

The third is a bound: **one program delivers thirty-two output channels and no more.** At
`oc` 48, 64 and 96, exactly 32 whole channels come back and the rest of the surface is
never written. That holds even though the program matches the vendor's own captures at
those shapes register for register. The library splits the output channels around it, one
submit per tile of 32.

**The vendor's own float value is not always the one that computes**, and this path shows
it. The BS operand reader `0x501C` reads `0x100` in every vendor float program, against the
integer path's `0x710`. Emitting the vendor's value makes the DPU write nothing at all,
with no fault and nothing in dmesg. Those programs also carry the BS ALU config
`0x4044 = 2`. With both vendor values, the writer runs again, and the arithmetic is wrong
on about a tenth of the surface.

The vendor's float epilogue is a different BS arrangement. This library's coefficient
group is packed for the integer arrangement, which is bit-exact under either ALU mode. A
capture is an oracle for a program, not for a program spliced into a different one.

The int8 first conv computes as well, so a quantized stem runs, and it is the form to use.
It takes 3.9 ms against the fp16 form's 257 ms at the same 224×224 stride-2 shape
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]. The entry `rocket_conv2d_int8_rk3576()` owns these stages:

- The packed image
- The cube
- The row window
- The output-channel split
- The zero-point fold
- The de-scatter

It is bit-exact against a CPU model at twenty shapes, including the stem at `oc` 64 and at
`k` 7 [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]:

- Image channels 2-4
- Kernels 3/5/7, both strides
- Planes 16×32 to 224×224
- `oc` 32 to 96

Its weight cube differs from the float one in every axis but the lanes:

- One byte per weight
- Output channels grouped by 32
- The tap row outside that group
- The tap column folded into the same `round16(4*kw)` row as the four lanes

The layout was read off the part rather than out of a capture. A quantized ARGB capture's
weights do not survive quantization as a findable value set. It is also the one
path here with no per-program output-channel cap: one int8 program delivers 64 channels
where one float program delivers 32.

**Three geometry bounds come with it, and two are silent**, so the entry enforces all three
as refusals:

- A non-zero left pad. `CNA_PAD_CON0` bits[15:8] alone decide whether the DPU writes at
  all, and `pad_top` does not.
- An output width of exactly `iw/stride`
- An output width that is also a multiple of 16

None appears in any capture, because every captured first conv is a 3×3 stride-2 SAME
convolution and satisfies all three. One image channel is not a fourth bound. The emitted
program will not fetch a single channel, so it is programmed as two, a zero lane against
zero weights. That form is bit-exact to 224×224 with the same envelope as two. A TFLite
stem does not satisfy the pad bound directly and is still reachable, as "TFLite stem
through an input shift" below shows. See [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md) for the cube
and the measurements.

The input zero point is a fourth bound, on the border alone, and the entry closes it. At a
non-zero input zero point, this path's interior stays bit-exact and its padded taps do not.
The border falls to about half exact the moment the zero point leaves zero. With the image
widened to eight channels, the direct path is exact everywhere, its border included. That
holds at the same zero points and against the same reference. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

No gate result on this path is wrong. Every first-conv shape in every gate carries a zero
input zero point, and a TFLite MobileNet's does too after the uint8 rebase. Finding the
bound needs a probe that varies the zero point on this path specifically
(`tests/rk3576_argb_pad.c`). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

The bound is an axis: the pad columns, at both ends and at every row. The entry
materializes those columns rather than refusing the shape, so any zero point runs. The cost
is a wider program and a surface that cannot be a consumer's cube. See "Materialized pad
columns" below.

#### The `CNA_PAD_CON1` pad map

`CNA_PAD_CON1` is decoded, and the border residual is not this register. A pass/fail sweep
reads it as "live, but no single centering explains it". Such a sweep asks "is there a
value that works" and returns nothing when there is not.

The probe `tests/rk3576_argb_padcon.c` reads the pad tap instead. It programs one output
channel, one unit weight at kernel tap (0,0), `in_zp` 0 and unit scales. At k3 s1 pad 1,
the outputs at (0,0), (0,1) and (1,0) are then the corner, top and left pad samples the MAC
saw. The output at (1,1) is the image pixel, as a positive control. The same geometry and
weights also run on the direct datapath, whose pad constant is measured correct. That
control places a map read here in the sub-encoding rather than in the instrument.

The readout gives three results [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 8 programmed values × 3 image
channels × both datapaths]:

- **The map is `(int8_t)byte`.** Neither path centers it: `0x00` feeds 0, `0x7F` feeds
  127, `0x80` feeds -128, `0xFF` feeds -1. The emitter writes `(int32_t)in_zp`, which is
  the identity the map wants. It writes `0x1084 = 0` at `in_zp` 0, which is why every gate
  that ever ran this path was exact.
- **It is per image channel on the packed path**: channel `c` reads byte lane `c`. A
  `0x7F` in lane 1 alone moves channel 1's pad and neither of the others. The direct path
  reads one value across its whole MAC group.
- **The pad columns do not read it.** The pad rows track the register. The pad columns feed
  0 whatever is programmed, in all four lanes and at every value tried, the unused fourth
  ARGB lane included. On the direct path both axes track it. The dead columns are the
  "about half the border" reading.

#### The pad-column residual

The residual is an axis, and it is symmetric in both senses. The readout above samples one
position, output (1,0), whose tap sees input (0,-1), the row-0 left pad. In a contiguous
packed image that byte lies before the start of the buffer. There, "the columns feed a
constant 0" and "the columns feed whatever is linearly adjacent" predict the same number.
That readout reads neither the trailing edge nor any other row.

The probe `tests/rk3576_argb_padmap.c` reads all four edges at seven positions each. A
second weight placement at tap (k-1,k-1) reaches the bottom row and the right column. The
probe computes `fed = output + in_zp`, so the readout survives a non-zero zero point
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]:

| arm | pad rows (top, bottom) | pad columns (left, right) | corners |
|---|---|---|---|
| packed, `in_zp` 0 | 0, 0 | 0, 0 | 0, 0 |
| packed, `in_zp` 20 | **20, 20** | **0, 0** | 20, 20 |
| direct, `in_zp` 0 | 0, 0 | 0, 0 | 0, 0 |
| direct, `in_zp` 20 | 20, 20 | 20, 20 | 20, 20 |

The packed path's pad columns therefore feed a constant 0 at both ends and at every row.
Its pad rows and its corners honor the register at both ends. No column sample varies down
its edge, which refutes the buffer-neighbor reading. At `in_zp` 0 a dead column feeds the
right answer, which is why every gate that ever ran this path was exact. The wrong trailing
region, which no leading-column story explains, is the right column.

No other register reaches those columns either. The packed and direct programs differ on 17
non-address registers at one geometry (`ROCKET_RK3576_DUMP=1` on both). A walk sets the
packed program's registers one at a time to the direct program's values. It leaves the
columns at 0 in all 11 arms whose interior still computes, and 6 arms break the program.
The one arm that moves the columns to the zero point, `CNA 0x100C -> 0`, zeroes the ARGB
mode word itself. That stops the program being the packed encoding, and its interior fails.

The walk is a one-at-a-time search and cannot see a condition of two registers.

#### Materialized pad columns

The pad columns are materializable, so the bound does not end the encoding. The error is
`-in_zp` times the sum of the weights over the taps in a pad column, and it is data
independent. It lives in the accumulator, though, and the entry returns a requantized int8.
Repairing an affected output therefore means recomputing it.

That is the expensive route. On a 224×224 k7 s2 stem it costs 9.8 ms of host work against
a ~2 ms saving (`tests/rk3576_argb_border_cost.c`, governor pinned). The work is 3 output
columns × 112 rows × 64 channels at 147 taps.

The cheap route keeps the hardware from synthesizing the columns. Extend the image by

    L = pad - p + stride*d

columns at the input zero point, give the program `pad_left = p`, and take output column
`x + d` as the model's output column `x`. The one column the hardware still synthesizes is
read only by output `x' = 0`, which is discarded. The encoding's own geometry bounds
(`iw % 16`, `ow % 16`, `ow*stride == iw`) then force a wider programmed input and output,
and the entry crops the surplus output columns.

The extension is bit-exact against the direct lowering in all four cells [HW sweep, H96 MAX
M9, `tests/rk3576_argb_extend.c`]. The cells vary everything the formula is a function of.
In each, the naive arm is wrong on pad-column outputs alone, with the interior clean:

| cell | model pad | L | `iw` | `ow` | `in_zp` | naive wrong | extended |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|---:|---:|---|
| ResNet-18 stem `k7 s2` | 3 | 4 | 224 -> 256 | 112 -> 128 | -128 | 7938/21504 | 0/802816 |
| `k5 s2` | 2 | 3 | 224 -> 256 | 112 -> 128 | -128 | 14336/14336 | 0/802816 |
| `k3 s2` | 1 | 2 | 224 -> 256 | 112 -> 128 | -128 | 1086/3584 | 0/401408 |
| `k3 s1` | 1 | 1 | 64 -> 80 | 64 -> 80 | +20 | 214/4096 | 0/131072 |

The library owns the extension. A caller passes its own geometry, and nothing about the
descriptor says an extension happened. The entries `rocket_conv2d_int8_rk3576()` and
`_pack_rk3576()` plan it whenever the packed encoding meets a non-zero input zero point.
They materialize the columns inside the image they already interleave. They crop the
surplus in the de-scatter they already run, so the crop is a narrower read rather than a
pass of its own.

The programmed image grows by one 16-output granule (`iw + 16*stride_x`), which is what the
geometry bounds force. **Two geometries have no extension and still refuse**: a zero left
pad (a different bound, the DPU writes nothing there at all) and `kw < 1 + stride_x`, which
asks for a negative trailing pad. The entry is exact at the caller's own geometry in all
four cells above [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_argb_extend.c`].

The extension changes the join, and the join survives it. The surface is `owx` wide where
the consumer wants `ow`. The caller's tensor starts at column `D = 1` of it, because the
extension discards the leading output column, the one the hardware still synthesizes. The
producer's output is therefore a sub-rectangle of its surface rather than the caller's
plane. Reading it in place needs a row pitch and a column offset the consumer can be told.

#### A plane inside wider rows

Both the PPU and the CNA can read a plane that sits inside wider rows. On the PPU, `0x600C`
carries what the windows consume: `(ow-1)*sx + kw` less the leading pad, clamped to the
plane. The DDR line stride is in `0x7024` and the channel-group jump in `0x7028`, so the
plane appears only in the strides.

The part honors a line stride above the consumed extent, and a source base part way into a
row. The sweep covers 16 cells over four geometries at gaps of +1/+4/+16/+64 elements, plus
12 cells of column offset at 1/3/8 elements. The geometries span max and average pooling,
kernel 2/3, stride 1/2, pad 0/1, square, odd and non-square planes, and 1/2/4 channel
groups. Every cell is bit-identical to a reference over a tight plane whose surplus columns
hold a poison byte. Each geometry also runs against a control that tells the group stride
and leaves the pitch derived. The control differs in all four [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`tests/rk3576_row_pitch.c`].

**The emitter needs the two quantities separated, and conflating them is silent.** If one
`iw` stands for both the plane and the pitch, a pitch passed as the plane lifts the clamp
on `0x600C`. A window meant to read a synthesized pad then reads the producer's surplus
columns instead. The error shows only where the windows overhang the plane, and there it
computes a full, plausible, wrong surface. The field `pool_params_rk3576_t.src_line_elems`
is the pitch, and 0 takes `iw`, so every program that does not set it is byte-identical.

`rocket_rk3576_cube` carries `pitch_w` and `col_off` (0 and 0 = a contiguous plane, which is
every other producer here). A pool consumer takes them. The library's convolution consumer
refuses on the field, for reasons of buffer ownership rather than of the register set
(below). **The column offset is added at the program and not folded into the cube's byte
offset.** The chain's run rule asserts a link by comparing a consumer's feature base against
a producer's surface base for exact equality. Folding the offset in moves that address by
one atom and breaks every run the join exists to create.

The CNA also reads a pitched plane, through three registers, none of which is the pitch on
its own. Its width field is `0x1078` high half (`iw-1`), and its line stride is `0x1090`
(`iw*4` words). A source read of this project's emitter makes them "one quantity in two
units". Both are derived from `iw` there because every capture has a tight plane.

A readout on the part separates them. It is a 1×1 convolution whose two live weights copy a
16-bit position code out of the feature buffer. The output at `(y, x)` then names the atom
the DMA fetched:

| register | what it is |
|---|---|
| `0x1044` low half | the DDR row advance, in 64-byte granules |
| `0x103C` high half | the fetch length, also in granules, and it must stay at the plane's count |
| `0x1094` | the channel-group jump, already a caller-supplied quantity |
| `0x1078` low half | the DMA's row budget, spent at the advance and not at the fetch length |

The CBUF is filled contiguously with the fetch length, and the window walks it at `iw` atoms
a row. Moving `0x103C` to the pitch as well therefore puts the read back at the tight stride
and computes a full, wrong surface. The budget is the register that is not obvious. A
pitched walk consumes `fetch_rows * entries_plane` granules of address at the pitch's rate.
It therefore runs out part way down the plane, and every row past that reads whatever the
DMA had left.

At two geometries, the map departs at exactly the atom where that total is spent. Inflating
that one field to `ceil(rows * entries_pitch / entries_plane)` makes the map affine to the
last row. **`0x1090` is not the row stride, whatever its RK3588 name says.** It advances the
address by its own value once every four rows, while the rows between step by the fetched
width. No value of it expresses a pitch.

The window grid does not follow the budget. The output extent and both derived pads come
from the real geometry. A pitched convolution therefore still synthesizes the trailing pad
that a widened plane would read real bytes for. That is the whole difference from the cheap
route, which programs the pitch as the plane and lets the output extent limit the windows.
The cheap route is exact wherever the window grid stays inside the caller's plane. It is
wrong on the trailing columns wherever a trailing pad is derived.

Measured and scored by region, the cheap route is wrong on 807 elements in column 27 of 28
at 28×28 k3 SAME. At k5 it is wrong on 659 in columns 19-20 of 21. It is wrong on nothing
at k1 or at the stride-2 shape whose grid lands exactly.

The pitched read is bit-exact against a tight-plane reference with the surplus poisoned. The
sweep covers nine geometries at four gaps of +1/+4/+16/+64 elements, plus three column
offsets of 1/3/4 elements [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_conv_pitch.c`]. The
geometries span kernels 1/3/5, both strides, square and non-square planes, 2 and 4 channel
groups, and one row task and up to ten. The feature base is a plain address at atom
granularity, as the PPU's is. Each geometry also runs against a control that leaves the row
stride derived, and the control differs [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_conv_pitch.c`].

The field `conv_params_t.in_pitch_w` is the pitch, and 0 takes `iw`, so every program that
does not set it is byte-identical. Two geometry bounds ride with it, and both are silent:
past either, the surface is written in full and wrong.

**Both the pitch and the plane must fill whole 64-byte DDR granules**
(`iw * ic * elem % 64 == 0`). An odd plane at ic=32 is 10.5 granules a row. It carries a
rounding the budget arithmetic cannot undo, and computes wrong at every pitch.

**A pitched task's row window is the unpitched allowance scaled down by the granule ratio
twice.** A 112-wide plane inside 128-wide rows has an allowance of 48 rows. Windows of 40,
41, 42 and 47 rows all compute wrong, while 16, 32 and 36 are bit-exact.

That is a bound rather than the law. The residue between 36 and 40 is unpinned. The missing
margin has the size of the retained rows a chained window carries, which the arithmetic
does not model. Like the unpitched allowance it scales, the bound is safe in one direction
only. A window too small costs a submit, and one too large writes a silently wrong surface.

**Trap: the arms of a register probe are not independent measurements on this part.** Two
arms of the CNA pitch probe leave the part writing nothing for every submit that follows,
in this process and the next.

A preceding arm can also change a following one's map, even when the two emit
byte-identical streams. Run in sequence, the pitched walk appears to revert to the tight
stride after four rows. Run one arm per process, it is affine to the last row. The sequence
supports a complete, mechanically plausible and wrong story: "the row stride and the group
jump do not compose". Run one arm per process before believing anything a sequence says.

#### The materialized stem on a graph

On a graph, the encoding's program saving lands. The table is ResNet-18 at `bench 100`,
three repeats each, with everything else equal:

| arm | wall | submits | tasks |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| materializing packed stem, joined to the pool | **7.4** | 1 | 33 |
| direct stem (the control) | 9.2-9.3 | 1 | 38 |

The materializing stem saves 1.8-1.9 ms, 20%, at the same 27 joins of 30 adjacent pairs
either way. Every compute layer is bit-exact against the CPU model, and the top-1 is
TFLite's own. The packed stem heads the run and the pool sits inside it, so the graph stays
one hardware kick. The graph's task count falls from 38 to 33, because the packed stem is
one row task where the direct lowering needs six (measured, both arms).

The other three graphs do not move (V1 5.0, V2 6.7, IV1 8.7). Both MobileNets have
TFLite's `pad_left = 0`, which no extension reaches. Inception V1's stem has `in_zp = 0`
and so materializes nothing.

The governor does not carry the saving. The two arms differ in host work, so pinning
`performance` is the control the trap asks for. Pinned, the arms give 6.0 against 7.7 ms
over three repeats each, the same direction at 1.7 ms. The corpus numbers above stay on
`ondemand`, which is what every other wall here is measured on.

Per op, resident and governor-pinned, at that stem geometry, the materializing packed call
takes 1.41 ms against the direct lowering's 3.13. The same packed program at `in_zp` 0
takes 1.26 ms [HW sweep, `tests/rk3576_stem_cost` group E, `ROCKET_SC_ZP=-128`].

The join removes one tensor transposed twice: the same 784 KiB out of a cube and back into
one. Warm over 20 inferences, the stem's readback measures 1.10 ms of its 2.10 (0.10 the
DPU drain). The max pool's scatter measures 0.85 ms of its 1.45.

The alternative route is a host compaction, and the join does not need it. Copying the
128-wide surface into a 112-wide cube moves 4 channel groups × 112 rows of 1792 contiguous
bytes. It takes 0.178 ms over two BOs (4.52 GB/s) and 0.163 in place, against a flat
memcpy of the same bytes at 0.107 (7.47 GB/s). A 0.154 ms PREP/FINI bracket over 784 KiB
comes on top. The compaction therefore costs ~0.33 ms, and as host work between two layers
it breaks the run [`tests/rk3576_row_pitch cost`]. Reading the surface in place costs
nothing.

The frontend chooses the form. The gate `rk3576_net_gate` takes the materializing form
where the next layer is a pool, and its A/B switch is `ROCKET_RK3576_NET_ARGB=0`. A stem
whose consumer is a convolution is a program the part runs: the pitch encoding above is
bit-exact, and the generator emits it. The library's `rocket_conv2d_int8_cube_in_rk3576()`
does not yet carry a pitch through to the handle, though, so no graph reaches it.

No graph in the corpus has a materializing stem that feeds a convolution, either.
ResNet-18's is the only materializing stem, and its consumer is the max pool, which is why
the pool is enough. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-02]

### TFLite stem through an input shift

A TFLite stem is reachable by moving the input rather than the pad. Whether the program
can express a zero left pad is settled at the geometry, not at `CNA_PAD_CON0`. A non-zero
left pad and an output extent of `ow*stride == iw` are jointly inconsistent with a zero
lead at k3 s2. There, a zero lead gives `ow = iw/2 - 1` and never `iw/2`. The entry
refuses before any register is written, and no reading of the pad register would change
that.

The stem is reachable anyway. Padding cannot move a stride-2 sample grid's parity, but the
grid also moves when the input moves. An input shifted one pixel against a pad of one is a
shift of two, a whole stride. Extend the image by TFLite's trailing row and column. Feed
the program that buffer starting at row 1 column 1, and leave both register pads at 1.
Output `(y,x)` then taps image rows `2y..2y+2` and columns `2x..2x+2`, which is TFLite's
grid exactly.

At 224×224 k3 s2 oc=32, 394272 of 394272 interior outputs are bit-exact. The reference is
TFLite's own SAME convolution over the original image. What is left is the output's own top
row and left column, 1.8% of the surface. Their outermost taps read the hardware's pad
constant instead of image row 0 and column 0. That residue is a host fix-up, not a
barrier. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_argb_pad.c`]

Wired into a real graph, the shift is a loss, which inverts the reading above. The
lowering is in `rk3576_net_gate` behind `ROCKET_RK3576_NET_TFLITE_STEM=1`. It is
byte-for-byte identical to the widened direct path over all 401408 elements of the stem's
output. The gate runs that A/B whatever the default is. The border is computed by the same
reference everything else is asserted against, so a comparison against that reference
alone would say nothing about it.

Interleaved at `bench 100` on a resident graph, the shift costs 5.5-5.8 ms against the
widened path's 3.5-3.7. That takes the inference from ~30 ms to ~31.5, even though it
drops the layer from five submits to one.

A "worth about 4 ms" estimate compares the shift with the widened path transient
(10.6-12.2 ms), a saving resident weights already collect. Two costs were measured between
the shift and a win. The first is the border fix-up, 1.7-1.9 ms for 1.8% of the surface.
It is memory-bound and not MAC-bound: 7136 outputs, nine scattered input cache lines each.
Hoisting the loop's bounds into registers moves it by under 0.2 ms. It is the shift
lowering's own cost, and it stands.

The second cost was a per-call BO teardown of 1.0-1.8 ms, paid while the packed path had no
resident handle. The library removes it: `rocket_conv2d_int8_pack_rk3576()` packs the
packed-image cube too, and the host interleave is vectorized. What remains against the
TFLite-stem shift is the border fix-up alone. The lowering is kept, gated and exact, and
off by default. The MobileNets it targets carry `pad_left = 0`, and their whole stem
execution is 0.30 ms of `exec + drain` against that 1.7-1.9 ms.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`, 2026-07-29]

#### Packed-image path traps

**Submit a vendor capture verbatim before calling a datapath dead.** This project's
emitter reproduced twelve int8 ARGB captures register for register and still wrote nothing
at every geometry tried. A leave-one-out over the nineteen-register delta against the
working fp16 program revived none of it, which read as a dead datapath. The captured op
stream from the golden table, submitted with only its five address registers patched
(`tests/rk3576_fc_vendor.c`), writes its whole surface. The mode works, and the
extrapolation away from the captured geometry does not. Walking back from that geometry one
axis at a time yields all four bounds.

**The CNA converter's offset is inert.** The ARGB register table lists `0x1054`-`0x105C`
as the uint8 zero point the CVT subtracts, as the datapath's description says. Every ARGB
capture is zero point 0, though, so no capture exercises those registers. [HW sweep, H96
MAX M9]

Driven on the part, those registers do nothing. An image written at `raw = s + (zp+0x80)`
comes back with the raw byte read as a signed int8 and no subtraction at all. A raw `0x80`
reads -128, and `0xFF` reads -1. The packed image is therefore plain two's complement. The
input zero point folds into the coefficient group's `A` term exactly as the direct path
folds it. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

**The fp16 first conv poisons its own next submit**, and that is most of its wall time.
A second submit of the same program comes back with the surface never written. Against the
inter-submit gap, the write rate is 1/20 at 0 and 20 ms, 8/20 at 60 ms, and 20/20 from
100 ms. The poisoning is not a property of the first-conv sub-encoding. It is the one
hazard the whole part carries on any wide output, and the section below settles it.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

The int8 first conv has no such hazard: 20 of 20 submits write at a zero gap.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

## The poisoning is one hazard, and its cost is a system setting

**Any program from this project's encoders whose DPU output element is wider than one byte
poisons the next submit, of any kind, across processes.** That submit completes normally and
writes nothing, and a one-byte output never carries the hazard. The state belongs to the part and
not to the process. A probe that starts a second process to look for it still finds nothing.
Starting a process takes longer than the idle that clears the state ("An arm that swaps in
another process is an idle arm", below). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

An A-poisons-B matrix over seven program kinds splits them exactly, with each pair measured
against a canary that is known to write. The fp16 first conv, the fp16 direct conv, and both
int32 writers poison every one of the seven. The int8 first conv, the int8 direct conv and the
int8 depthwise poison none of them. Every A wrote its own surface in every cell, so no cell is
a dead program reading as a clean one. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

The instrument is `tests/rk3576_poison_probe.c`, and its design is the point. Asking whether
the program under test wrote cannot tell a dead program from a poisoned one. So the probe
scores the canary instead and lets the program under test compute nonsense.

**It persists on `rocket` 1.6.0**, whose drain fix cures a lost output tail and not this. On
kernel 7.2.3-1, each of three programs leaves the int8 canary empty 20 of 20 at a zero gap.
They are the int32 writer, the wide int32 writer and the fp16 direct conv (`pair i32`,
`pair i32w` and `pair fp16d` against `int8fc`). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-09-24 UTC]

**On 1.6.0 the poisoned submit does not complete.** The driver retires it at the 125 ms
backstop with its surface unwritten. That is 161 of the 161 retirements the gate list's three
poison rows scored, each one matched by a kernel `retiring it` line.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tools/gates-rk3576.sh`, 2026-09-27]

One wide writer poisons nothing. The matmul-form fp16 program writes a flat fp32 surface,
four bytes an element. After it, the int8 direct and first-conv canaries wrote 20 of 20 each in
one process at zero gap, as did the program itself. The controls in the same run poisoned as
usual: `pair i32 int8d` and `pair fp16d int8d` each left the canary empty 20 of 20. [HW sweep,
H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, `rk3576_poison_probe` kind `mmf16`, measured 2026-09-25 UTC]

The matmul-form program sets DPU `0x4038[4]` and `0x4050[17]`, the pair this project's fp16 conv
poisons through, so those two bits are not sufficient. It also raises its own completion: its
pairs logged no retired job, where the other two logged 62.

Nor does a whole output stage poison. The int32 writer stops poisoning with three more DPU
words. The dense int32 writer built on that stage leaves four canary kinds clean (§"A whole
output stage that does not poison").

**A sacrificial submit does not absorb it.** After one int32 job, six consecutive int8
jobs at a zero gap all wrote nothing. The state persists until the power domain cycles,
so the guard has to be an idle rather than a cheap dummy job.

### The register conditions

The two paths poison through different register conditions. Delta debugging
(`rk3576_poison_probe heal`) over the register delta between a poisoning program and its clean
counterpart at the same geometry found both. The method applies the whole delta, confirms that
it stops the poisoning, then bisects. A leave-one-out asks whether any one register is
sufficient. Every negative it returns is equally consistent with a condition of two, so
one-at-a-time sweeps found nothing on the fp16 path. Each path's condition is as follows:

- **The int32 writers: one register.** `gen_conv2d_int8_rk3576_i32out()` differs from the
  int8 direct conv in four registers, and DPU `0x4010` (`out<<29 | in<<26 | proc`) alone
  carries the hazard. Restoring it stops the poisoning with the program still writing. Forced
  onto the int8 direct conv, `out` values 1-3 are clean and 4-5 poison.
- **The fp16 path: exactly two bits, and neither alone.** The fp16 direct conv writes the
  same 96 registers as the int8 one, no register is exclusive to either, and 11 values
  differ. Minimized, the condition is DPU `0x4038` and `0x4050` together. Each restored alone
  leaves the canary at 0/8, and both restored take it to 8/8 with the program still writing.
  [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

An exhaustive truth table over the six differing bits of those two registers narrows the fp16
condition further to `0x4038[4]` and `0x4050[17]`. Every combination that clears both is clean,
every combination that leaves either set poisons, and no other bit matters.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

**Clearing those two bits is not a cure, because they are the float arithmetic.** The first,
`0x4038[4]`, is part of the low half that must read `0x92` rather than the direct path's
`0x80`. The second, `0x4050[17]`, is the high-half field that reads 2 at float and 1 at int8.
With both bits cleared, the fp16 direct conv still writes its surface and stops poisoning.
Every one of the five fp16 direct-conv shapes that reproduces a CPU model exactly then computes
wrong. A whole output stage that keeps both bits set cures the int32 writer (§"A whole output
stage that does not poison"), so they are not the cause alone.

The state does not live in the DPU register file. Neither program writes nine of the DPU
registers (`0x4000`, `0x4008`, `0x4040`, `0x4054`, `0x4064`, `0x4068`, `0x4098`, `0x40a0`,
`0x40c4`). When the next job writes those nine explicitly, it stays poisoned. The canary
already writes `0x4038` and `0x4050` at their int8 values and is poisoned anyway. Whatever
carries the state is internal, and the power domain collapsing resets it, not a register write.

**Nothing absorbs it, on any path.** For each of the four poisoning program kinds, one
poisoning job followed by six submits leaves every position at 0 of 6. The guard has to be an
idle.

**A dead program can poison.** The four truth-table rows that drive `0x4038`'s low half to
`0x80` while the rest of the program stays float write nothing themselves. They still leave
the canary at 0/6. So "it wrote nothing" is not a proxy for "it was harmless". That is the trap
in any register sweep here that scores the program under test.

### The clearing idle

The idle that clears the state tracks `power/autosuspend_delay_ms`, and the law is twice the
delay. A 5×7 sweep of delay against gap gives the gap at which a chain of wide-output submits
first reaches 20 of 20:

| `autosuspend_delay_ms` | Gap to 20 of 20, ms |
|---|---|
| 50 | ~100 |
| 20 | ~40 |
| 10 | ~20 |
| 5 | ~10 |
| 1 | ~5 |

**Lowering that delay system-wide is not free**, and a back-to-back chain cannot show why. A
resume costs about 230 us. Lowering the delay makes every job whose inter-job gap falls
between the new delay and the old one pay that resume. At a 16.7 ms gap, a submit goes from
1124 us to 1372. That gap is one 60 Hz frame, the pipeline that mainline's 50 ms was chosen
for. At a zero gap the two are identical, because the timer never fires.

The library therefore drives the power cycle instead of waiting for it. The function
`rocket_rk3576_power_idle()` writes a zero delay, polls `runtime_status` until it reads
`suspended`, and puts the caller's delay back. The guard costs one real cycle rather than a
worst-case idle. The steady-state policy that every other consumer of the NPU sees is
unchanged. The function needs write access to that sysfs file, and without it falls back to
the plain idle (`ROCKET_RK3576_PM_KICK=0` turns it off).

At the stock 50 ms delay, with every gate still bit-exact, the fp16 first conv's 224×224 k3
s2 runs in 13.75 ms against 226. Its whole gate group runs in 2.3-15.6 ms against 107-334,
and the int32 matmul gate in 9.9 s against 16.2. That beats lowering the global delay to
1 ms, which reached only 21.6 ms and taxed every gapped workload to get there.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

### The LUT job as an eighth victim kind

A LUT job is an eighth victim kind, and like the other seven it writes nothing when poisoned.
The A-poisons-B matrix covers seven convolution and matmul kinds, and a poisoned B in all
seven writes nothing. The library's whole-surface write guard is built on that.

Behind an int32-output job, the table-load-plus-consumer pair in `tests/rk3576_lut_probe.c`
comes back with its surface untouched. Every failing span reports `surface untouched 8192/8192`,
and every passing one reports `0/8192`. The rate is 4 of 20 behind one wide-output
predecessor, interleaved. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

**A stamp read back is not a measurement.** Scored without a stamp count, a poisoned LUT job
reads as a surface written in full, correctly sized and constant. Every one of 8192 sampled
inputs reads `-86` against a ramp, at `max |diff| 213`, which looks like the consumer running
against a table it never loaded. The probe's `SENTINEL` is `0xAA`, which it memsets into the
output BO before every submit, and `(int8)0xAA` is `-86`. So a job that wrote nothing hands the
model back the probe's own fill. A scorer that only knows "expected vs got" reads that fill as a
wrong constant.

Only the first span of a run can show it. The probe's `mode_gate` sleeps 150 ms between
spans, longer than the ~100 ms idle that clears the hazard, so three spans always pass
alongside it. The probe reports how much of the surface is still at the stamp, so the
instrument, not an argument, separates the two readings. **A probe that stamps a buffer must
report how much of the stamp came back.** A sentinel that is a plausible output value makes
the question invisible until it is asked.

The library's whole-surface write guard is intact, and nothing in the corpus needs a
different one on this account.

**The library's activation entry is a victim at the same rate**, and its `0 of 24` was its own
guard firing, not an immunity. In one interleaved run behind the same predecessor, at N=20,
the raw probe fails 4 of 20. The gate `rk3576_act_gate` fails 0 of 20, with its guard firing
in 3 of 20. With no predecessor at all, the gate fails 0 of 20 and the guard fires in 0
of 20. The two counts, 4 of 20 and 3 of 20, are one rate. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

The guard's own line names the poisoned job on the first call of the process:
`rocket_act_int8_rk3576: of 1 row task(s) in this submit the first that wrote nothing is 0, on attempt 1; cycling the power domain and redoing it`.
After that line the gate passes its 180 and 28 cases. The competing reading is that the setup
submits of `r76_conv_int8_run()` stand between the predecessor and the LUT job. Two results
refute it. A sacrificial submit is measured not to absorb this hazard (six after one, every
position 0 of 6), and the guard fires on the LUT job itself. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

**The elementwise probe is a third victim, and it loses its whole run rather than its first
case.** The gate mode `rk3576_add_probe gate` is a DPU-only program like the LUT pair. Behind
`rk3576_pool_probe avg` (the only pooling mode that submits an int32-output matmul), it reports
nothing written at all ten of its shapes, not only at the first one. The ten submits sit
inside one active window, and a sacrificial submit does not absorb this hazard. So once the
hazard starts, nothing writes until the power domain collapses. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
2026-08-04]

The failure rate is 2 of 6 runs behind that predecessor, against 0 of 6 behind an idle and 0
of 6 behind `pool_probe gate` (pooling with no int32 job). That separates the trigger from the
predecessor's kind. Guarded the same way, the probe is 6 of 6 green in the poisoned position,
with 4 of them repaired by a single power cycle. So one cycle clears the state for the rest of
the run, which is what "all ten fail" and "one redo is enough" say together. [HW sweep, H96
MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

The probe runs the same guard the library does. It redoes a wholly untouched surface after
`rocket_rk3576_power_idle()`, up to the library's eight attempts, and prints the redo count
beside the span. It does not retry a partial write, because a retry loop there would paper
over an encoding defect.

The guard turns `rk3576_lut_probe`, intermittently red at its list position without it, into
a deterministic gate without hiding the hazard. Behind the predecessor that puts the unguarded
probe at 4 of 20, the guarded probe fails 0 of 20. Its redo column is non-zero in 7 of 20, and
a single power cycle repairs every one of them (7 redos over 7 runs). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

The hazard is per submit, not the first task of the next submit dying. The pair is one submit
of two tasks, and a dead task 0 with a live task 1 would produce exactly this signature. That
reading predicts a rate of zero with the table load duplicated (`ROCKET_LUT_DOUBLELOAD=1`, job
load/load/consumer). Interleaved against its own control, 40 each, the result is 10 of 40
against 14 of 40, not zero. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

**Read a predecessor arm for what the whole process did, not for the program it is named
after.** Of six `pool_probe` modes, only `rk3576_pool_probe avg` reaches the LUT failure. That
reads as "a padded average pool poisons the next process's first LUT submits". The same mode
also submits an int32-output matmul before half of its pools, and no other mode submits one
at all. So the wide-output writer and the `AVG_NOPAD` divisor mode are confounded across every
arm of that comparison. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

Three knobs separate them (`ROCKET_POOLA_PRE`, `ROCKET_POOLA_EXCL`, `ROCKET_POOLA_PRE_ONLY`).
With no int32 job in the predecessor the rate is 0 of 48, including the padded average pool
alone and both divisor modes. With one it is 24 of 84, across three different binaries. The
int32 job with no pool at all is 4 of 12. The pooling mode does not move the rate (7/12 with
`AVG_NOPAD` against 5/12 with count-include-pad). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

**An arm that swaps in another process is an idle arm.** Putting a pooling or an activation
process between the two takes the LUT failures to 0 of 24. That reads as "a submit absorbs
it", and it is not evidence for that. A process boundary carries an idle, and the idle that
clears the state is twice `autosuspend_delay_ms`, about 100 ms at the stock 50 (above).
Submits alone do nothing to it (six after one poisoning job, every position 0 of 6).

### The `rk3576_lut_probe load` positive control

The probe's `load` mode, which submits the table-load program alone, reports a false negative.
Its positive control for "the program executed" is the scratch BO moving off its sentinel. That
control reads zero: 0 of the scratch's whole 4096 bytes moves, in three consecutive runs. In
the same state `gate` is exact in all four spans, so the table demonstrably loads. The program
runs, and its dummy cube does not write that BO. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

Only `gate` is in the gate list, so nothing asserts on `load`. **A probe mode nothing asserts
on can be reporting a negative about a thing that works.**

### An externally reported hazard on the w4a16 RDMA path

The `charsiu` project reports a second next-submit hazard with a different signature
(gahingwoo/charsiu README, ROCK 4D, their v7-lineage kernel, 2026-08-15, not reproduced here,
see SOURCES.md). A job carrying the vendor's w4a16 values in DPU-RDMA `0x5034` (`0x4000004c`)
or `0x5044` (`0x000280a1`), either register alone, leaves the next job timing out. An
`rk_iommu` reset error appears beside it, and the driver recovers without a power-domain
cycle.

Their bisect has the same shape as this project's. It runs one w4a16 job per register mask,
judged by an int8 job in a separate process that is itself long byte-exact. The two registers
differ from their int8-path values in bit 30, in opposite directions. Setting all four RDMA
registers together, the vendor's own combination, also carries it.

It differs from the hazard above on both observable axes. Its victim times out with an IOMMU
fault rather than completing and writing nothing. Its state clears on driver recovery rather
than on the power domain collapsing. Treat it as a separate mechanism until it is measured on
this stack. No generator here carries a w4a16 stage, so nothing shipped can emit those values.
The trap is for a future int4/w4a16 port taken from vendor streams, which is exactly where
those two values come from.

### A whole output stage that does not poison

Three DPU words together cure the int32 writer's poisoning and keep its width. On
`gen_conv2d_int8_rk3576_i32out()`'s program they are `0x4010 = 0xa0000002`, `0x4038 = 0x53` and
`0x4050 = 0x00023333`. Together they take the int8 direct canary from 0 of 12 to 12 of 12
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, rocket 1.6.0, 2026-09-30]. The gap is zero, the program still writes,
and every pair and every single one of the three leaves the canary at 0 of 12. They are three
members of the `charsiu` project's wide output stage (`src/job.c`), which ships a four-byte
output on every dispatch. Delta debugging over that whole stage found them
(`rk3576_poison_probe heal` under `ROCKET_PP_ASET`).

The whole stage, with `0x40B8` at three surfaces, is a dense int32 writer that poisons
nothing. After it, the int8 direct, int8 first conv, depthwise and fp16 direct canaries each
wrote 20 of 20 at a zero gap. It ran 80 submits back to back with no idle, and every one wrote.
None was retired at the backstop, so it raises its own completion. In the same session the
unmodified writer left the fp16 canary empty 20 of 20, and a chain of it stopped at its first
job. The register program and its map are in [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md) §"The dense
writer".

**The int32 poisoning therefore belongs to the encoder's partial output stage, not to the
width.** This project's int32 writer moves `0x4010`'s `out` field and leaves the rest of the
stage at int8 values. The cure's `0x4038[4]` and `0x4050[17]` are the two bits the fp16 conv
poisons through, so neither that pair nor `0x4010` decides it alone.

The same stage on the fp16 slice program computes an fp16 conv as fp32. Every element
matches a CPU model on random small-integer operands at k 1 and 3 [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
rocket 1.6.0, 2026-09-30]. The operands fill all sixteen input channels, and the planes are
8×4 and 12×10 at 32 output channels and 5×3 at 16 (`tests/rk3576_fp16_fp32out_probe`). So
`0x4038 = 0x53` carries the float mode there as the float word's `0x92` low half does. The
surface is the plain int32 cube: four fp32 lanes to an atom, one atom per pixel, each quad of
channels a plane.

This fp32-out conv poisons nothing. It leaves the int8 canary clean 20 of 20. Eighty of its
own submits ran back to back with no idle, each written and none retired. With the encoder's
float `0x4038` kept in place of `0x53`, the canary stays empty 20 of 20.

The entry `rocket_conv2d_fp16_rk3576()` runs its slices on that stage. It runs no
per-program power cycle and none on exit. At k9 over 32 channels on 120×64, a call takes
9.9 ms against 115-193 ms. Over two slices it takes 15.1 against 227 ms, every element exact
[HW sweep, same board, `tests/rk3576_drain_probe gate`, 6 calls a shape]. At 28×28 k3, 128
input channels into 256 output channels is 64 programs in 47 ms. The packed-image first conv
has not run under the stage and still cycles.

## Cost of the library entries

The table comes from `tests/rk3576_cost_table.c`: three timed repeats after a discarded first,
one process per row, on the driver's default 50 ms autosuspend delay. The NPU column is the
whole public entry, because that is what a frontend pays. It covers host packing into the
native cubes, the submits and the de-scatter back to row-major, and it is not a kernel time.

The CPU column is a single-threaded scalar direct loop over the same tensors, auto-vectorized
at the library's own optimization level. It has no im2col, no packing and no threading, so it
is a reference rather than a tuned kernel. **Read each ratio as an upper bound against that
baseline.** A good NEON GEMM across the four A72s would close much of every one of them.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, kernel 7.1.7 / `rocket` 1.6.0 in-tree, `dpu_grace_us` 0, `ondemand`,
2026-08-10]

| Shape | NPU ms | CPU ref ms | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| stem 3×224×224 k3 s2 oc32, int8 | 3.21 | 41.7 | 13.0x |
| stem 3×224×224 k3 s2 oc64, int8 | 4.62 | 83.1 | 18.0x |
| stem 3×224×224 k7 s2 oc32, int8 | 3.12 | 188.8 | 60.6x |
| stem 3×224×224 k3 s2 oc32, fp16 | 13.98 | n/a | n/a |
| conv 32->64 k1 56×56 | 2.41 | 12.0 | 5.0x |
| conv 64->64 k3 56×56 | 2.68 | 242.8 | 90.6x |
| conv 128->128 k3 28×28 | 2.52 | 186.9 | 74.3x |
| conv 256->256 k3 14×14 | 4.44 | 185.0 | 41.7x |
| conv 256->512 k1 14×14 | 1.87 | 52.3 | 28.0x |
| depthwise 64 k3 56×56 | 2.36 | 7.5 | 3.2x |
| depthwise 128 k3 28×28 | 1.42 | 4.0 | 2.9x |
| depthwise 256 k3 14×14 | 1.07 | 2.2 | 2.1x |
| matmul 32×1024×1024 | 4.32 | 13.3 | 3.1x |
| matmul 32×1024×2560 | 9.78 | 31.1 | 3.2x |
| matmul 128×1024×1024 | 5.20 | 52.8 | 10.2x |
| matmul 256×512×2048 | 7.12 | 106.5 | 15.0x |

**Read no single row's move against the 1.5.0 table as a result.** The CPU column shows why.
The 1.5.0 table carried the same sixteen shapes. Twelve of the sixteen NPU rows read lower
here, the four matmul rows by 9-22% and the three int8 stems by 12-16%. But the CPU reference,
which no module change can touch, moves by up to 25% between the two runs (`conv 256->512 k1`
42.0 -> 52.3, `conv 32->64 k1` 11.4 -> 12.0). That is the table's own run-to-run spread,
measured on a column with no NPU in it.

The spread is wider than most of the NPU deltas, so the two tables are two readings rather
than a change with a direction. The deltas also do not support the drain deadline as their
cause. A job retiring early is a job whose wall is short, so curing it predicts higher numbers
here, not lower ones.

These rows move much less than a graph does under the same change, because of what they
measure. Vectorizing the CHW <-> NC1HWC2 transpose takes a resident MobileNet inference
30.1 -> ~21 ms (1.41x) and moves these rows by 0-30%. Every row here is a transient call,
which also pays the BO allocation and teardown and packs its weight cube from scratch. The
transpose is therefore a much smaller share of it.

The rows with the least other work move most. Under that change, `depthwise 64 k3 56x56` goes
3.11 -> 2.11, `conv 256->512 k1` 2.02 -> 1.70, and the int8 stem 3.40 -> 3.09. Individual
shapes vary 20-40% run to run, so read a single row's change with that in mind.

The table says four things, at this operating point rather than as properties of the
silicon:

- **The submit is a floor of about 0.44 ms, and the submit-bound rows track it.** When the
  completion poll goes from 500 us on `PC_DONE` to 50 us on the DPU bit, the rows with the
  least work per submit move most. They are `conv 32->64 k1 56x56` at 3.12 -> 2.01 and
  `depthwise 256 k3 14x14` at 1.42 -> 0.96. The rows dominated by host packing or by a power
  cycle barely move.
- **Depthwise wins by 2.1-3.7x.** Against a threaded CPU kernel it would be close. That much
  is arithmetic rather than encoding. A depthwise conv has `ic` times fewer MACs than the
  direct conv at the same shape, so the same submit floor buys `ic` times less. Batching
  several depthwise layers into one submit is the lever the encoding would have to supply.
- **The matmul's axis is N, and the planner already spends it.** At M=32 the planner puts
  K=N=1024 in a single submit, the largest N tile the part will take. So the matmul at low M
  is not dispatch-bound. Its bound was the host cube scatter, below.
- **The quantized stem is the stem to run**, but the gap is 3.2 ms against 13.7 rather than
  against 226. Driving the power cycle that the fp16 path's guard waits for closes the gap,
  not arithmetic. The residue is one real cycle per call. The fp16 path also pays the
  `dpu_grace_us` blind settle, because it raises no DPU completion.

### The host cube scatter

Done an element at a time, the host cube scatter is most of the wall, not the submit. The
entries pack row-major tensors into this part's cubes and de-scatter the results back. Through
the index functions, that costs a divide, a modulo and a call per byte. At K=N=1024 that is a
million of them, and twelve of that matmul's thirteen milliseconds. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

Three of those scatters are block copies once the index is written out. At `kh=kw=1`,
`weight_conv_int8()` puts thirty-two consecutive contraction elements at thirty-two
consecutive bytes on both sides, and `feature_data()` at C2=16 does the same for sixteen. So
the matmul's feature pack, weight pack and output de-scatter are blocked memcpys. The
convolution entries stay a real transpose, CHW against NC1HWC2, and keep a strided store.
Their per-element division hoists out to one pair per channel.

Everything above is measured with this change. Without it, the matmul ties the CPU reference
at M=32 (1.0x), depthwise runs 1.4-1.6x, and the 1×1 convolutions 2.2x. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

The convolution entries' weight side has two more host loops of this kind, and only a whole
graph, not a single shape, shows them. The knob `ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1` splits a call into
its operand packs, its submits and its de-scatter. With both loops per-element, the submit is
1-3% of the wall on the layers that cost most, and the two host loops are 90%. Both are
functions of the weights alone, and per-element, both walk a million elements through index
arithmetic.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`]

The first loop is the per-output-channel filter sums, a contiguous scan written as a
triple-nested index expression. The second is the weight cube scatter. Its `taps == 1` case
is thirty-two contiguous source bytes landing on thirty-two contiguous cube bytes, a run of
block moves rather than byte stores. Straightening both takes MobileNetV1's graph from 146 ms
to 115 ms, with every layer still bit-exact. It moves the 1×1 convolution rows of the table
above from 5.6x/15.7x to 5.3x/21.7x. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`]

A locality rewrite of the feature scatter is a measured negative. Packing pixel-outer and
channel-inner fills each C2-byte atom in one go. It touches every destination line once
instead of once per channel, which is the better trade on paper. On the part it is worse,
because it needs C2 concurrent read streams. On the graph it costs the depthwise layers more
than it buys the 1×1 ones (layer 3's feature pack 3.2 -> 5.3 ms). The scatter stays
channel-outer, with one sequential read stream and a strided store.

### The int8 matmul entry

A matmul computes on this part at int8, through `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576()`. The gate
`tests/rk3576_matmul_gate.c` holds it against a CPU model over 33 shapes, which include:

- M with no even divisor
- K and N off the round grid
- Several N tiles
- A row-windowed M
- The two alignment refusals
- Eleven that gate the int32 output exactly

The register program needs nothing new, because a matmul is a 1×1 convolution and this part
computes those bit-exactly. The entry's work is the userspace layer around one:

- The operand scatter into this part's cubes
- The output de-scatter
- The tiling planner
- The per-chip dispatch point

Three of its properties are the RK3588's answers inverted, and the last is why the entry is
separate rather than a routing behind `rocket_matmul_int8()`:

- **int8 is the matmul precision here.** One int8 task takes `ic*kh*kw` up to 4608, so a K of
  4608 lands in a single submit. One fp16 task contracts exactly sixteen input channels, so
  the same K costs 288. Run on one shape at both precisions, fp16 is 30x slower at K=512 and
  300x slower at K=4608. On the RK3588 fp16 wins and int8 buys only RAM.
- **The M axis carries no constraint.** M = 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 17 and 31 are each bit-exact. So is
  every factorization of M into a plane, a single row of pixels, a single column, and
  everything between. On the RK3588, rows are the conv's spatial height and height < 4
  mis-computes. There `M%4` is the real bound, and software pads `M==1` to 4.
- **The output is int8, through the DPU's requant**, where the RK3588's int8 matmul writes
  raw int32 and sums K partials on the host. Inside one task's contraction that is the fast
  path, and the requant is the DPU's own. Past it, the entry splits K through the
  int32-output entry below and the requant moves to the host. The boundary is invisible in
  the result and expensive in the cost.

Throughput is MACs per submit and nothing else. A submit costs about 0.44 ms whatever it
carries, and two of the three axes are already capped. The feature budget caps `M*K` at
262144 int8 elements, and the resident weight slice caps K at 4608. That leaves N as the only
axis worth spending. At the feature-budget cap, throughput rises almost linearly with N:
12 GOP/s at N=32, 47 at 128, 367 at 1024 and about 1.0 TOP/s at N=2560. [HW sweep, H96
MAX M9, measured 2026-07-27]

**Two bounds stop it, and both lose the trailing output channels silently**, with a full,
correctly sized surface and a clean prefix. The output width computes at 2944 channels and is
intermittent at 3072. Independently, a 6 MiB weight cube computes where 6.75 MiB does not.
Both are refusals in the CBUF planner, and the default tile of 2048 sits far enough inside
that they act as guards. No convolution shape reaches either, and they bite only at the
output widths a matmul drives. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-27]

### The int8 GEMM against a real CPU GEMM, at LLM prefill shapes

The cost table above prices the entry against a scalar reference. Against a tuned CPU GEMM at
the shapes an LLM prefill issues, with `M` at the ubatch and never 1, the entry is 3.3-6.8x.
The median is 5.4x over the six shapes that compute correctly. The harness is
`ggml-rocket/test-rk3576-sgemm-ab.cpp`. Its NPU column is the whole public entry (host pack of
A and B into the cubes, the submits, the de-scatter). Its CPU column is the fastest of ggml's
own F32 `MUL_MAT`, ggml's Q8_0 `MUL_MAT` and OpenBLAS `cblas_sgemm`, all four threads on the
A72s, arms interleaved.

| M / K / N | NPU ms | best CPU ms | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 512 / 2048 / 512 | 10.29 | 34.39 | 3.34x |
| 512 / 2048 / 2048 | 28.20 | 133.22 | 4.72x |
| 1024 / 2048 / 2048 | 48.50 | 267.79 | 5.52x |
| 2048 / 2048 / 2048 | 78.70 | 532.24 | 6.76x |
| 512 / 2048 / 8192 | 91.07 | 533.27 | 5.86x |
| 512 / 4096 / 4096 | 100.13 | 531.80 | 5.31x |

The ggml Q8_0 kernel is the fastest CPU arm at every shape, 3.2x its own F32 and a few percent
under OpenBLAS. The denominator is therefore not a strawman. The ISA does not help it either.
This part is ARMv8.0-A (`/proc/cpuinfo` has no `asimddp` and no `asimdhp`), so it has no SDOT
and no fp16 arithmetic. A part with dot-product would close some of this gap.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-08-07]

The ratio rises with M because a fixed cost amortizes. At K=N=2048 over four values of M, the
entry fits `T(M) = 10.9 + 0.0337*M` ms. The intercept is M-independent work: B's cube pack
plus the per-call BO allocation and teardown. It is 39% of the M=512 wall and 14% of the
M=2048 one. This part has no resident-weight matmul path, so the entry re-packs B on every
call. Building one is worth up to that intercept, which at M=512 would take 28.2 ms to ~17.3
and the ratio from 4.7x to ~7.7x.

**A median over a shape set is not the model-weighted answer.** A Llama-3.2-1B-shaped layer at
ubatch 512 has its own MAC shares: attention QKVO 17%, gate/up 55% and down 28%. Weighted by
those shares, with the down-projection left on the CPU because it refuses (below), one layer
is 2.39x, not 5.4x.

### The entry at M = 1, and what decode would cost

The transient entry is not a decode engine, and at M = 1 the reason is arithmetic: the call is
its M-independent intercept. The decode-shaped rows of `rk3576_cost_table` below are ten timed
repeats after a discarded first, in one invocation:

| shape | NPU ms | CPU ms | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| matmul 1×1024×1024 | 3.88 | 0.43 | 0.11x |
| matmul 1×2048×1024 | 8.72 | 0.78 | 0.09x |
| matmul 1×1536×1536 | 8.25 | 0.94 | 0.11x |
| matmul 32×2048×1024 | 7.85 | 24.63 | 3.14x |

At the same K and N, M = 1 tracks M = 32 within 3-11% (two runs), which measures the
intercept a second way. On single-row cells the profiler `ROCKET_MM_PROFILE` splits the whole
call. It buckets the entry's setup, its BO teardown and its own entry-spanning wall, and wall
minus accounted reads 0 over 11 calls. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 7.1.7 / `rocket` 1.6.0
in-tree, `ondemand`, 2026-08-18/19]

At 1×2048×1024 the buckets read:

| Bucket | ms/call |
|---|---:|
| `packB` | 3.27 |
| `alloc` | 2.36 |
| `bofree` | 1.18 |
| submit plus fence wait | ~0.27 |
| everything else | ~0 |

So ~94% of an M = 1 call is machinery a resident-weight path would not run. That machinery is
the pack and the five-BO create/mmap/munmap/GEM_CLOSE round trip (the packB cache and the BO
pool named at the end of the section above). The teardown term is M-independent (1.18 ms/call
at M = 32 too, 0.91 at 1×1024×1024). It accounts for the ~1.3 ms that a profile without the
teardown bucket leaves unattributed. The write guard's read and every PREP/FINI pair are
inside other buckets. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 7.1.7 / `rocket` 1.6.0 in-tree, `ondemand`,
2026-08-18/19]

The device-side residue sits on a weight-fetch line. The fence-wait marginal between the
1.05 MB and 2.10 MB rows is 86 us/MB. The charsiu project fits 84.3 us/MB on a ROCK 4D through
its own kernel (`github.com/gahingwoo/charsiu`, chained tasks, 2026-08-15). The agreement is
closer than the instrument. The profiler dumps integer milliseconds over eleven calls, so this
marginal carries about +-60 us/MB. Read it as the same regime, not the same number.

What decode would cost with the transient machinery gone is a projection [inference,
end-to-end untested]. A resident, chained M = 1 path pays the bytes term: an int8 model's
weights once per token at ~86 us/MB. That is the same DRAM wall a CPU GEMV pays on a part
where one A72 thread saturates memory. So int8 NPU decode projects to parity with the CPU
rather than a win. Halving the bytes (int4) is the lever that would move it.

The charsiu project reaches the same reading from the vendor side. The vendor runtime ships
M = 1 NPU decode at ~13 tokens/s on Llama-3.2-1B (their measurement, their board, their
kernel).

### The same port on a real model: a tie with the best CPU arm, and the denominator is half the headline

The W8A8 route runs under stock llama.cpp on Qwen2.5-1.5B, measured as `pp512` at ubatch 512.
Four threads are pinned to the A72s, with governor `performance` and one job in flight. The
GGUF is an F16 conversion of the same HF snapshot the accuracy corpus is scored on. The DL
backend is `ggml-rocket`, and the entry is `ROCKET_INT8=1`. Two CPU denominators run in the
same boot, interleaved, two processes each:

| arm | GGUF | pp512 t/s (two processes) | ratio to the NPU arm |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| CPU only | F16 | 5.92, 5.77 | **2.01x** |
| CPU only | Q8_0 | 11.58, 11.51 | **1.02x** |
| NPU, W8A8 | F16 | 11.78 +- 1.80, 11.78 +- 1.84 | n/a |

The port ties the strongest CPU arm end to end and doubles the weakest one, on the same
silicon and the same model. The 2.01x is the number a drop-in user reads. The port consumes
an F16 GGUF, and ggml's F16 `MUL_MAT` is what that GGUF runs on the CPU. The 1.02x is
the number against ggml's Q8_0 kernel, which is what the port's 2.37x model-weighted cap was
measured against. **Quoting the first without the second credits the port for its own input
format.** [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 7.1.7 / `rocket` 1.6.0, llama.cpp b10356, measured
2026-08-11]

The 2.37x cap therefore overstates the end-to-end port by 2.3x. It is a cap over projection
MACs on shapes this model does not have. What the cap does not carry:

- Every offloaded call here is `K`=1536, and none of the six shapes behind the cap is.
- The down-projection's 29.4% of projection MACs runs on the CPU, by the library's own
  `K >= 6176` refusal. The cap does charge that part.
- The wall also holds attention, the norms, rope, softmax and the scheduler's copies.

Six of the seven projections per layer offload, 168 calls per ubatch over 28 layers. The
down-projection and the `lm_head` (computed at `M`=1 for the last token only) stay on the CPU.

A third `ROCKET_MM_PROFILE` accumulator prices where the tie comes from, because the first two
do not fit this route. The RK3588 accumulators `g_prof` and `g_prof_i8` in `rocket_matmul.c`
split an RK3588 job into pack/gen/sync/submit/wait/read. Three of this route's terms have no
bucket there: the O(K*N) `sum_abs_w` pass, the per-tile per-column ramp plan, and the
poisoning stamp-and-check.

The route's own buckets in `rocket_matmul_rk3576.c` are
`sumabs / packA / packB / coeff / gen / alloc / stamp / submit / wait / read`, over calls,
tiles and submits. On the same knob, `ggml-rocket.cpp` carries the terms outside the entry
(`valloc / quant+rot / calibrate / entry / dequant`). Both sum to the offloaded path, not to
the prefill wall. Attention, the norms, rope and the scheduler are in neither.

The arm also gives two operating notes. The governor is worth 16% on this arm: the NPU column
reads 9.87 and 9.91 t/s on `ondemand` against 11.78 pinned. **The NPU arm's run-to-run spread
is +-1.8 t/s (15%)** against +-0.01 on both CPU arms, so a single process is not a measurement
of it.

### Flash attention past a 512-token prompt

Whenever the prompt is longer than 512 tokens, the NPU arm returns
`failed to decode prompt batch, res = -3` and llama.cpp stops. **The refusing op is
`GGML_OP_FLASH_ATTN_EXT`, and the trigger is `n_kv >= 1024`.** The micro-batch does not
move it, so the axis is the context, not the ubatch. The cells ran one process at a time on
an otherwise idle board:

| arm | `-b`/`-ub` | prompt | result |
|---|---|---|---|
| NPU, FA on | 512 / 512 | 512 | **rc=0**, 10.51 t/s |
| NPU, FA on | 512 / 512 | 2048 | `res = -3` after 79 s |
| NPU, FA on | 1024 / 1024 | 2048 | `res = -3` after 2 s |
| NPU, FA on | 2048 / 2048 | 2048 | `res = -3` after 4 s |
| NPU, FA on | 1024 / 1024 | 1024 | `res = -3` after 5 s |
| NPU, FA off | 512 / 512 | 2048 | **rc=0**, 12.12 t/s |
| NPU, FA off | 1024 / 1024 | 2048 | **rc=0**, 12.10 t/s |

The deciding cell is a ubatch of 512 failing at a 2048-token prompt. The micro-batch is a
shape the route computes 168 times per ubatch in the passing control. The context is what
separates the passing row from the failing ones, and the elapsed times locate the failure
inside the run. At ubatch 512 the failure comes after 79 s, in the second micro-batch, where
the KV length first crosses 1024. When the first micro-batch is already that long, it comes
after 2-5 s.

The source gives the chain, and it was read before the arm ran. A `-3` from `llama_decode`
is `GGML_STATUS_FAILED`. An allocation failure is a distinct `-2` (`ALLOC_FAILED`), so the
board's RAM is not the cause. In `ggml-rocket`, `supports_op` claims `FLASH_ATTN_EXT`
subject only to shape, type, `n_tokens >= 16` and `n_kv >= rocket_flash_attn_min_kv()`
(default 1024). That case has **no `rocket_rk3576_selected()` branch**, unlike its
`MUL_MAT` case, whose comment states this exact failure mode.

The handler's only compute call is `rocket_flash_attn_fp16*`, and every path in
`rocket_attn.c` goes through `rocket_matmul_fp16`. That function begins with
`mm_wrong_encoding()` and returns `ROCKET_E_UNSUPPORTED` on any profile that is not
`rk3588`. The FA gate therefore reaches this part unadapted. At a prompt of 512 the `n_kv`
floor kept it out of reach, which is why the pillar's whole corpus ran.

**The refusal names its clause, and the bench harness discards the message.** Both the
`ROCKET_LOGE` in `mm_wrong_encoding()` and the `GGML_LOG_ERROR` in `graph_compute` fire.
The `ggml-rocket` backend forwards the rocket channel into ggml's, and `llama-bench`
installs a no-op ggml log callback that swallows everything sent through it. Setting
`ROCKET_DEBUG=1` raises the level and does not change the sink, so it cannot help. The knob
is `ROCKET_LOG_STDERR=1`, which the convert, int8 and MoE profiler dumps already use for
exactly this reason. Under a bench harness, read `res = -3` as "a message you are not being
shown".

This is a coverage question, not a bound. The backend claims the op and then declines it,
and the scheduler has nowhere to put it. The fix is therefore at the gate, not in the
handler: decline `FLASH_ATTN_EXT` on this part and the CPU takes it. Declining at the gate
or porting the handler is the maintainer's choice. Declining is what makes the port usable
past a 512-token prompt today, and `ROCKET_FLASH_ATTN=0` is the knob that already does it.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, Qwen2.5-1.5B F16, llama.cpp b10356, measured 2026-08-11]

The `-ub 2048` lever is open on this part. The reading that closes it comes from runs that
vary only the ubatch axis, where the context moves with the ubatch. With FA declined, the
port reads 12.12 t/s at ubatch 512 and 12.10 at ubatch 1024, so doubling `M` per offloaded
call moved the wall 0.2%. That reading is a hint, not a measurement: it is one process per
cell against this arm's own +-15% run-to-run spread. The profile buckets below settle what
the lever is worth. Two wall-clock points do not, and the wall cannot resolve it at all.

### Time and wall share of the offloaded W8A8 path

The profile runs Qwen2.5-1.5B F16 on llama.cpp b10356 under
`ROCKET_MM_PROFILE=1 ROCKET_LOG_STDERR=1` and `ROCKET_FLASH_ATTN=0`. It sweeps `-ub` over
{512, 1024, 2048} at a fixed prompt of `-p 2048`. Every cell runs the same total tokens and
the same total MACs, with the per-call count quartered. Each cell is one process, with the
governor pinned to `performance` and `ondemand` restored after.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, rocket 1.6.0, measured 2026-08-11]

The model offloads 166 matmuls per micro-batch, with `K` = 1536 or 8960. Across the whole
axis the entry's buckets are constant at 2.301 tiles per call. Submits per call double with
`M`: 9.20 at `M`=512, 18.41 at 1024 and 36.82 at 2048.

Per library call, in ms:

| bucket | M=512 | M=1024 | M=2048 | scaling |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| `sumabs` | 3.62 | 3.61 | 3.61 | M-independent |
| `packB` | 7.51 | 7.58 | 7.60 | M-independent |
| `coeff` | 0.219 | 0.218 | 0.215 | M-independent |
| `gen` | 0.044 | 0.068 | 0.116 | ~M-independent |
| `packA` | 1.87 | 5.35 | 11.32 | M |
| `alloc` | 10.63 | 13.33 | 18.74 | mixed |
| `stamp` | 2.13 | 5.29 | 14.51 | M |
| `submit` | 0.148 | 0.246 | 0.449 | M |
| `wait` | 4.75 | 10.50 | 24.88 | M |
| `read` | 3.65 | 6.96 | 15.60 | M |
| **accounted** | **34.57** | **53.16** | **97.05** | |

The entry is host-bound. The `wait` bucket, the share that runs programs, is 13.7% of it at
`M`=512, 19.7% at 1024 and 25.6% at 2048. The two largest buckets are `alloc` (30.7% at
`M`=512) and `packB` (21.7%). The `alloc` bucket is large because the weight, coefficient
and output BOs are allocated and freed per tile per call. The attribution order held: the
host is the cost, and the part is a quarter of it at best.

#### The M-independent intercept

On the per-call path, where the entry recomputes per call, the M-independent intercept is
11.4 ms per call. It is measured, not fitted: `sumabs+packB+coeff+gen` reads 11.39, 11.48
and 11.54 ms across a 4x sweep of `M`, constant to 1.3%. That is three passes over `K*N`
every call, and 31.3% of the converged per-call cost at `M`=512. The shipped frontend path
does not pay them. Its caller hands the entry the sums (`_sa`) and the weight resident in a
device cube (`_wbo`, below). This intercept therefore prices the calibration forwards and
any caller still on the per-call path.

The intercept is `sumabs` plus `packB` and essentially nothing else. The `coeff` bucket is
0.216 ms a call and `gen` is 0.045, together 0.8% of the 11.4. So `sumabs` and `packB`
together are 11.136 ms = 32.35% of the accounted total. A cap over a weight cache does not
need to discount the intercept for the other `M`-independent buckets, because there is
nothing in them. The `alloc` bucket is large (10.640 ms, 30.91%), but it belongs to a
different lever and sits outside this intercept.

The `M`=512 column reproduces between 2026-08-11 and 2026-08-13 on an unchanged binary,
which is tighter than this route's usual bucket drift. The re-measurement ran three
processes at `-ub 512`, 2988 library calls each, and reads per call, in ms:

| bucket | 2026-08-11 | 2026-08-13 |
|---|---:|---:|
| `sumabs` | 3.62 | 3.629 |
| `packB` | 7.51 | 7.500 |
| `coeff` | 0.219 | 0.216 |
| `gen` | 0.044 | 0.0454 |
| `alloc` | 10.63 | 10.640 |
| `stamp` | 2.13 | 2.121 |
| `submit` | 0.148 | 0.147 |
| `wait` | 4.75 | 4.745 |
| `read` | 3.65 | 3.601 |
| `packA` | 1.87 | 1.771 |
| accounted | 34.57 | **34.42** |

Every bucket sits inside 5.3%, and all but `packA` and `read` inside 1.5%. Process-to-process
spread within the 2026-08-13 run is 0.04-0.6% on every bucket except `packA` (9.8%) and
`read` (3.4%). The `pp2048` wall reads 12.33, 12.37 and 12.35 t/s. That spread is the
resolution any A/B on these buckets has to clear. It does not license quoting a level from
another session, only the observation that these particular levels held.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-13]

#### Supplied sums, the resident weight cube and the BO pool

The `sumabs` bucket does not run on the shipped frontend path. The per-column sum of |B|
that caps the C ramp is a quantity `ggml-rocket` already computes once per weight (it is
where `cbound` comes from). The entry `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_perc_sa()` takes that sum
instead of recomputing it. Setting `ROCKET_RK3576_WSA=0` restores the recomputing form for
pricing.

The two forms are bit-identical by construction, and measured so. With the knob either
way, `llama-perplexity --chunks 4` returns PPL 8.7433 +/- 0.76052 to every printed digit,
on the same binary and the same text. The supplied-vs-computed arm of
`tests/rk3576_mm_requant` reads 0 of 344064 elements over six shapes at two column-scale
spreads.

The `packB` bucket does not run on the shipped frontend path either. The weight is resident
in a device cube, and a BO pool takes the transient allocations. The function
`rocket_rk3576_wbo_create()` packs a weight once into a device BO. The whole-`N` cube is
byte-for-byte the concatenation of the per-tile cubes (`nt` is a multiple of 32). One BO
therefore serves every `nt` and every `M`, and a tile's program offsets `weights_dma` by
`(n0/32)*nK1*1024`.

The entry `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_perc_wbo()` is `_sa` with the object in place of `B`.
It requires `sum_abs_w`, refuses a K/N mismatch, and refuses a no-plan `K` outright. The
frontend creates the object at a weight's first converged call and drops its host `qB`.
Device memory therefore goes up by what host memory comes down. The knob is
`ROCKET_RK3576_WDEV`, default on, and `=0` restores per-call packing. Calibration still
reads `qB` and still takes the per-call path.

Setting `ROCKET_RK3576_BO_POOL=1` (default off, read per allocation) keeps the path's
transient BOs on a per-fd free list capped by `ROCKET_RK3576_BO_POOL_MB`. The three arms
were scored over 3x3 interleaved processes at `-ub 512` on one binary (2988 calls), per call
in ms:

| bucket | control | resident | resident+pool |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `packB` | 7.49 | 1.66 | 0.95 |
| `alloc` | 10.60 | 5.18 | 0.015 |
| `bofree` | 1.32 | 0.84 | 0.004 |
| `packA` | 1.84 | 1.59 | 0.98 |
| accounted total | 32.13 | 19.89 | **13.06 (-59%)** |

The `packB` residue is the 332 calibration forwards, still on the host path.
A fresh CREATE_BO's demand-zero page faults spread across the first-touch buckets, so the
pool removes more than its own `alloc`. It also cuts `calibrate` 21.2 -> 11.4 s a process,
because the bootstrap's create faults lived there. With the host stalls gone, the `wait`
bucket rises 14.1 -> 15.9 s, and the NPU becomes the pacing element [hypothesis: queuing].
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, cells `w{0,1}p{0,1}_p{1,2,3}`, 2026-08-19]

Bit-identity holds: `--chunks 4` prints PPL 8.7433 to every digit in both WDEV modes. The
requant gate's resident, pooled(x2) and forced-16-tile arms read 0 differing bytes, and the
gate list reads 60/60. The interleaved wall reads `pp2048` 12.30 -> 13.41 (resident) ->
13.53 t/s (both, +10.0%), from n=3 pairs resolving ~3.3%.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, cells `w{0,1}p{0,1}_p{1,2,3}`, 2026-08-19]

A green result does not show three things. They are the IOMMU-churn exposure on kernels
without the BO-lifetime fixes, behavior under real memory pressure, and decode at M=1.
Decode is unmeasured, and the cache removes that call's 77% packB+alloc share in principle.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, cells `w{0,1}p{0,1}_p{1,2,3}`, 2026-08-19]

Against a pinned layout, the pool's marginal wall gain is +1.86%, about twice the +0.9% the
3x3 read above gives it. That 3x3 figure comes from n=3 pairs against a ~3.3% instrument
resolution. Collapsing the per-process layout draw with `setarch -R` on both arms brings the
resolution to 0.358% (re-derived from the arm's own cells), which makes the level directly
