Do not measure the floor with a gate's total wall time. Host packing and the gate's own CPU
model dominate the conv gate, which reads flat at every period.

The DPU completion interrupt reaches the GIC, so the poll is a driver choice, not a silicon
bound [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-13]. The pre-`0008` premise "not routable to the GIC"
is a measurement of `PC_DONE`, and it never covered the DPU pair.

A diagnostic module (a diagnostic patch on the 1.6.0 OOT reconstruction)
masked `DPU_0`/`DPU_1` (bits 8-9) in at submit. The mask holds them (wrote `0x3300`, read
`0x3300`). The NPU's shared line (GIC 279, with `27702000.iommu`) counted 1 -> 311 across a
300-submit floor run. That is one interrupt per submit, with `raw=0x30000155` at each
arrival. The module left retirement to the poll, so nothing else moved.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-13]

Taking the interrupt as the completion is therefore open to the series (it is what
gahingwoo's upstream v7 does). An interrupt-only path cannot retire the two classes that
raise no DPU completion. One is pooling, whose `PC_OPERATION_ENABLE` bitmap never enables
the DPU. The other is any output element wider than one byte. The poll, or a grace, must
survive for them.

Under the armed module, the floor itself reads 279.4 us median against the same-base
control's 351.2 [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-13]. Retirement there is still the poll's.
The mechanism is unmeasured, and plausibly the interrupt holds the waiting CPU out of deep
idle [hypothesis]. In that case the interrupt is worth more than removing the poll's
latency alone.

#### Retirement on the DPU completion

Retiring on the DPU's own completion is what makes a short poll period safe. A `PC_DONE`
status means the program counter finished issuing, not that the DPU's write DMA has drained.
The driver both tears the IOMMU mapping down and signals the fence as soon as it sees
`PC_DONE`.

On the IOMMU side, the per-job detach faults writes still in flight. It leaves a write page
fault (`RK_MMU_STATUS 0x2b`) that blocks the next `enable_stall`. The patch
`patches/rk3576/npu/0011` fixes that, and takes stall timeouts and NPU DMA errors to zero
at every period.

On the visibility side, the fix is `0012`. The `RAW_STATUS` bits `DPU_0`/`DPU_1`, which the
RK3588 takes as its completion interrupt, set **82-343 us after `PC_DONE`** on this part,
scaling with the bytes in flight. Requiring them retires the job when the writes have
landed. Retiring on `PC_DONE`, the int8 matmul gate passes 42/43 at a 250 us period and
36/43 at 50 us. Retiring on the DPU bit, it passes 43/43 at both, and the whole gate set is
clean.

**The int32 writers raise no DPU completion at all.** They stop at `RAW_STATUS =
0x30000055`, CNA and CORE done and nothing further for milliseconds. So the driver bounds
the wait by a deadline, and they retire on `PC_DONE`. The deadline is load-bearing: they
fail at 100 us and pass from 250 up [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-28]. On
`rocket` 1.6.0 the wait for the writing block has no deadline. There an int32 job without
`ROCKET_JOB_NO_DPU_DONE` waits out the 125 ms backstop, 5 of 5 per writer [HW sweep,
2026-09-27].

These programs also carry the poisoning, but the two are separate hazards: the poison
probe's `scope` map is unchanged under `0012`.

**The fp16 convolution programs raise it, and a poisoned fp16 job does not.** Without the
flag on 1.6.0, the input-channel slice program and the packed-image first conv each fence
0.38-2.2 ms after the submit. Every element is written and nothing is retired. At k9 over
32 channels on 120×64 that is 2.07 ms, 10 of 10 [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, rocket 1.6.0,
`tests/rk3576_drain_probe raw`, 2026-09-27]. An fp16 job submitted into a poisoned part
writes nothing and waits out the backstop, 5 of 5.

At the 2026-07-28 sweep's own shape, the slice program raises it too. The input-channel
slice at `oc` 32, 28×28 k3 fences a median 343-350 us after the submit, at `dpu_grace_us` 0,
100 and 500. No rep left a tail at the fence or logged a retirement [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
rocket 1.6.0, 2026-09-30]. The probe is `tests/rk3576_drain_probe raw fp16d:16:32:28:28:3`,
10 reps an arm. A deadline counted from the submit cannot bill that program a whole grace.
So the 2026-07-28 reading does not come from a deadline outrunning it.

A poisoned slice does pay one. At grace 500 it fences at 665 us having written nothing, and at
grace 0 it waits the 127 ms backstop, 6 of 6 each. So the no-completion reading is not a
property of the program. Whether that sweep's module or its submits produced it is not
established. Two of the 50 unpoisoned reps wrote nothing after the 300 ms idle. One retired at
the grace-100 deadline and one at the backstop.

**`ROCKET_JOB_NO_DPU_DONE` fences a job at the blind settle whatever its program is
doing.** Under the flag an fp16 program fences 0.73-0.94 ms after the submit. Every one
still running then returns rc 0 with the last rows of each 8-channel plane unwritten. Those
rows land about 1.2 ms later [HW sweep, same probe]. That is 10 of 10 at k9 over 32 channels
on 120×64. It is 6 of 6 at k5 and k7 on that plane, and at k9 on 64×64.

Programs that finish by 0.62 ms come back whole: k1 and k3 on 120×64, and k9 on 32×32. So
the threshold lies between 0.62 and 0.96 ms of program time. The write guard asks whether a
task wrote anything, so it cannot see a partial tail. The flag belongs only on a program
that raises no completion, with a check of every element behind it. The int32 writers'
per-atom scan is that check.

The vendor `rknpu` driver has no special case for either class. It takes each task's
interrupt mask from userspace (`rknpu_task.int_mask`) and writes the last task's into
`INT_MASK`. A job completes only when `INT_STATUS`, each bit pair folded to one, equals that
mask exactly. The clear value, `0x1ffff`, never touches the `PC_DONE` pair at bits 28-29
[source-confirmed: BSP `rknpu_job.c`, 0.9.8, unchanged in `develop-6.12`]. So what the vendor
stack waits on for a pool or a wide output is `librknnrt`'s choice of mask. Only a capture of
the submitted tasks on a vendor kernel shows it.

#### The settle wait on the fp16 path

Without `0017`, every wide-output submit pays that grace in full, so it is the fp16 path's
largest single cost. An fp16 convolution is `ic/16` such submits and takes no poisoning
retries at all. So a slice costs the poll floor plus the whole blind settle. At 28×28 k3,
`ic=128` runs a median 15.89 ms over 8 slices at the 500 us default and 13.29 at 250. Each
arm is five runs with disjoint ranges, and the difference is 300 us of a 250 us cut,
landing once per slice.
[HW sweep, measured 2026-07-28, `tests/rk3576_fp16_split_cost.c`]

Three consecutive full gate suites pass at 250 (each 102 conv, 156+7 conv-library, 43
matmul on each int32 writer, 15 refusals, the host regcmd gate). That covers the
wide-output programs the grace exists for. The default stays where it is, because 250 is
the measured cliff edge rather than a margin above it. The value to ship lies between 250
and 500. Picking it needs the int32 sweeps run at that value rather than at 250.
[HW sweep, measured 2026-07-28, `tests/rk3576_fp16_split_cost.c`]

The patch `0017` splits the two waits, and with it the fp16 path pays `dpu_blind_us`, not
`dpu_grace_us`. A job that raises no DPU completion names itself with
`ROCKET_JOB_NO_DPU_DONE` and waits `dpu_blind_us`, default 250. The sweep below drove each
dial with the other held, five interleaved pairs a dial, governor pinned. It ran `ic=128`
at 28×28 k3 on the 1.5.0 module, with both fp16 entries submitting under that flag:

| dial | held | low | high | effect |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|
| `dpu_blind_us` | grace 500 | 250: **9.85 ms** (9.25-12.17) | 500: **12.90 ms** (12.09-14.63) | **3.05 ms, ranges disjoint once the cold first pair is dropped** |
| `dpu_grace_us` | blind 250 | 250: 9.83 ms (9.23-10.39) | 500: 9.95 ms (9.25-11.62) | **0.12 ms, ranges fully overlapping** |

The 2.6 ms that the 2026-07-28 sweep priced for lowering `dpu_grace_us` is already
collected at the shipped `dpu_blind_us`. Moving the grace buys this path nothing. The
measured step is 1.5-2.1x the 8 x 250 us = 2.0 ms that one blind interval a slice predicts.
The likely cause is more than one blind wait per slice [hypothesis], and the step is not
decoded.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-06, `tests/rk3576_fp16_split_cost.c`]

**Do not read these absolutes against the 15.89 and 13.29 above.** Those were taken on the
shipped governor, and these with it pinned. One arm's spread here (9.25-12.17) is wider
than the 2.6 ms the comparison is about.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-06, `tests/rk3576_fp16_split_cost.c`]

#### Per-job core reset and the half-started job

**A per-job `rocket_core_reset()` does not clear the int32 poisoning, and it takes the
IOMMU down.** The IOMMU reports `MMU_DTE_ADDR is not functioning`, the matmul gate drops
from 43 shapes to 2, and the part stays wedged. The driver's own timeout path resets inside
a scheduler stop and an IOMMU detach/re-attach. A bare call is missing that surrounding
re-init. The vendor `rknpu` driver never resets per job either.

**A half-started job pins the NPU `active`, which then breaks the int32 path and nothing
else.** The fault is present in mainline `rocket` on the RK3588 as well, and
`patches/rk3576/npu/0010` fixes it. The two early returns in `rocket_job_run()` leave a
runtime-PM reference that nothing will drop and a fence that nothing will signal. Each pins
the device on its own. The usage counter never returns to zero, and the scheduler never
takes back the job's credit, which `rocket_device_runtime_suspend()` waits on. The
usage-counter half lives on the platform device, so `rmmod`/`insmod` does not clear it
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-27].

The part then comes back computing convolutions bit-exactly, 102/102 and 111/1, while every
i32 matmul shape writes nothing. The poisoning's clear is the power domain cycling, and the
pinned domain does not cycle. It reads as a bug in the int32 writer, and it is a machine
state.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-27]

Without 0010 the state is also self-sustaining. One failed attach times out, the timeout
path's core reset takes the IOMMU down, and the next attach fails for real. One injected
failure produced seven failures and leaked seven references. **Check
`power/runtime_status` before believing any int32 result.**
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-07-27]

#### Kernel module build on the board

The module builds on the board. The board's `linux-headers` package is a full kbuild tree
with `Makefile`, `Module.symvers`, `arch`, `include` and `scripts`. To build it:

1. Take pristine v7.1 `drivers/accel/rocket/`.
2. Apply 0006 and 0008. They are the only two in the series that touch that directory. The
   rest patch pmdomain, the IOMMU and the DT, which are built in.
3. Give it an `obj-m` Makefile.
4. Run `make -C /lib/modules/$(uname -r)/build M=$PWD modules`.

The compiler differs from the kernel's and warns. The module loads, and both conv gates and
the matmul gate pass on it unchanged.

### The third thing that stops a GEMM frontend is what the entry writes, and the fix is two changes

The speed question is answered, and the datapath blockers above are refusals a caller can
see. This one is silent, and it is measured on real models rather than on synthetic
operands. **One per-tensor output scale writing int8 costs a real LLM's prefill between
1.11x and 2.26x in perplexity, depending on the model.** The pair of changes that takes
both models to 1.00x is known.

The arm is the entry's own expression, `sat8(round_even((A*B^T + bias) * scale))`,
simulated on the host from a real model's own prefill activations. The models are
SmolLM2-1.7B (`d`=2048, ffn=8192, 24 layers, 144 offloadable projections) and Qwen2.5-1.5B
(`d`=1536, ffn=8960, 28 layers, 168), over 512 real wikitext-2 tokens. Every scale is an
oracle taken from the tensor it quantizes, so these are the entry's best case. A projection
counts as offloadable exactly where the entry accepts it (`K`%32, `N`%32, `K` < 6176). That
puts every `d_model`-input projection on the NPU and leaves the FFN down-projection on the
CPU.

Per GEMM, relative Frobenius error against a float64 reference, over 36 offloadable cells:

| what quantizes how | SmolLM2-1.7B | Qwen2.5-1.5B |
|---|---:|---:|
| fp16, the floor the CPU path already delivers | **0.0003** | 0.0003 |
| per-tensor A, per-tensor B, per-tensor int8 out | 0.0965 | 0.0712 |
| per-row A, per-channel B, per-tensor int8 out | 0.0439 | 0.0408 |
| Hadamard-rotated A and B, per-tensor, int8 out | 0.0202 | - (`K`=1536 is not 2^k) |

**Read the per-GEMM table and you will pick the wrong one.** The end-to-end table below
replaces every offloadable projection in every layer. It scores wikitext-2 perplexity over
8 windows of 512 tokens, and top-1 agreement against the fp32 run. It covers two models,
because one of these rows differs between them by a factor of eighty:

| arm | SmolLM2 ppl | x fp32 | top-1 | Qwen ppl | x fp32 | top-1 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| fp32 / fp16 | 11.1937 / 11.1924 | 1.000 | 99.73% | 12.8403 / 12.8385 | 1.000 | 99.63% |
| the entry as specified (per-tensor everywhere) | 25.2618 | **2.26x** | 61.99% | 14.2294 | **1.11x** | 83.18% |
| per-row A + per-channel B through it | 964.3787 | **86x** | 60.69% | 13.2236 | **1.03x** | 91.19% |
| per-channel output requant, no rotation | 17.2697 | 1.54x | 72.36% | 13.6248 | 1.06x | 85.86% |
| Hadamard rotation, per-tensor output requant | 14.6771 | 1.31x | 76.78% | 13.1144 | 1.02x | 90.48% |
| per-axis inputs, no output requant at all | 15.1168 | 1.35x | 77.22% | 13.1369 | 1.02x | 93.51% |
| **Hadamard + per-channel output requant** | **11.2366** | **1.004x** | **97.29%** | **12.8645** | **1.002x** | **96.85%** |

Four things follow, and the first is what the second model bought:

- **The cost is model-dependent by more than an order of magnitude, and per-axis input scales
  are a coin flip.** The caller quantizes A and B, and the entry's `scale` is the output's
  alone. So per-row activation and per-channel weight scales are free at this interface, and
  they lower the per-GEMM error on both models. End to end they take Qwen from 1.11x to
  1.03x and SmolLM2 from 2.26x to 86x. **So a port must not assume they help, and must
  measure a candidate model end to end rather than reading a norm.**
- **The obvious mechanism for that is refuted.** A per-tensor requant's error is uniform in
  accumulator units, so per-axis input scales make it rank-1 in real ones. On SmolLM2 the
  arm does concentrate: up to 20.6% of a call's error energy in one row of 512, against
  0.62% for per-tensor inputs. But Qwen's numbers have the same shape, 14.84% against 0.64%,
  and Qwen does not blow up. Its per-row scale spread is 4.4x median and 24x max, against
  SmolLM2's 3.7x and 22x.

  The concentration statistic does not separate the two models, so it is not the
  explanation. What SmolLM2's arm does show is that its damage is calibration rather than
  ranking. Its top-1 agreement is 60.69% against the per-tensor arm's 61.99%, essentially
  unchanged, while perplexity goes 25 -> 964. The mechanism is open.
- **The route is a rotation and a per-output-channel requant, it holds on both models, and
  neither half is enough alone.** Alone, the rotation scores 1.31x on SmolLM2 and 1.02x on
  Qwen, and the requant 1.54x and 1.06x. Together they score 1.004x and 1.002x at 97% top-1.
  The rotation is host-side and needs no library change. It is an orthonormal Hadamard
  along `K` before quantization, which `ggml-rocket/test-hadamard-quant.cpp` already builds
  for non-power-of-two `K`. Qwen's `K`=1536 takes the weaker block-diagonal form over
  512-element blocks and still lands.

  The per-channel requant is a library change whose machinery is already on the part. This
  DPU's epilogue is `(acc + A[oc]) * C[oc]`, then one `(v*MUL)>>SHIFT` per task. The file
  `rocket_conv2d_rk3576.c` programs that C ramp today for per-axis convolution.

  The matmul entry pins `in_scale`/`w_scale` to 1, puts the caller's factor in `out_scale`,
  and emits one row task per N tile. The conv path solves the same situation by sorting
  channels by scale. The matmul entry needs that planner, not a new capability.
- **1.004x is a ceiling, not the route's number.** The arm gives every output column an
  exact per-column scale. The hardware gives an integer C ramp at a resolution of
  `0.5/C[oc]`, with one shift per task. That is why the per-axis ResNet-18 scores 89 of 100
  against a per-tensor control's 93. What the ramp itself costs a GEMM is unmeasured.

This does not show that the on-NPU path agrees with this host model of it. Every number
here is host arithmetic, and the tiled, requantized device path is a second gate.
[host-computed, arithmetic over two real models, `M`=512, 2026-08-10]

The port's cap survives the host work it adds, because the down-projection dominates. A
W8A8 path pays, per call, an activation quantization and a rotation the sgemm A/B never
ran. A run on the board's own A72s at `M=512 K=N=2048` measures both
(a standalone host micro, best of 5 after a discarded first, `taskset -c 4-7`).
Quantizing takes 1.08 ms, the FWHT 2.06 ms and dequantizing 0.57 ms, in all 3.70 ms on four
cores and 12.22 on one. That is 13% of the entry's 28.2 ms at that shape, and it takes the
shape's ratio from 4.72x to 4.18x.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-10]

Re-weighting the A/B's shapes by a Llama-3.2-1B layer's MAC shares moves the model-weighted
ratio only 2.44x -> 2.37x. The refused down-projection is 65% of the NPU path's wall and
pays none of the host work. The rotation is also per input, not per call. The q/k/v
projections share one and gate/up share one, so a real port rotates twice a layer rather
than six times. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-10] The down-projection's CPU wall is taken
as gate/up's at equal MACs [expected].

### Sources of the per-column output scale

Every perplexity figure above uses an oracle per-column scale, taken from the accumulator it
is about to quantize. The entry `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_perc()` takes `scale_n` from its
caller. A caller holds A and B and no accumulator, so the oracle figures are the route's
ceiling and not a frontend's. The table below scores what a caller can supply. It uses the
same eight windows of 512 wikitext-2 tokens and the same 168 offloaded projections of
Qwen2.5-1.5B:

| per-column scale source | ppl | x fp32 | top-1 vs fp32 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| oracle, exact per-column (`hadamard_chanout`) | 12.8645 | 1.0019 | 96.85% |
| oracle, through the shift-0 ramp | 12.8724 | 1.0025 | 96.63% |
| **frozen over 2 disjoint calibration windows** | **13.4451** | **1.047** | **87.28%** |
| the analytic bound `128*sum_k\|Bq[n][k]\|` | 389.5999 | 30.342 | 19.63% |
| the frozen scale's perturbations, dealt to the wrong columns | 12.9315 | 1.007 | 92.70% |

The scale source is the largest single term on this route. Its excess over the exact-scale
ceiling is 0.045 of ratio, against the integer ramp's 0.0006 on this same model. That is a
factor of seventy-five. A port that budgets for the ramp and not for the scale has
mis-scoped the accuracy by an order of magnitude.

The calibrated arm's damage is a tail, not an average. On median, the frozen scale is
1.083x looser than the call's own column maximum. The surface saturates less than the
oracle's (0.180% against 0.212%), so "the frozen scale clips" is the wrong reading. Over
1344 forwards, the tightest single column sits at 0.228x, 4.4x too tight, and saturates
hard. A max over the calibration set is therefore the wrong estimator on the tail. A
percentile or a per-column safety factor is the untested lever, and so is a larger
calibration set.

The last row is the control that earns the reading. It applies exactly the calibrated arm's
multiset of per-column perturbations to a fixed random permutation of the columns. The
magnitude and the distribution are the same, and nothing carries over about which column
needed the headroom. Its profile is, if anything, harsher (median 1.078x, tightest column
0.209x, saturating 0.326% against the calibrated arm's 0.180%). It costs 1.007x against
1.047x. So the metric is stable under a same-size information-free perturbation, and the
calibrated arm's cost is attributable.

Scrambling which columns get the bad scale makes the model six times better, so the harm is
the correlation rather than the magnitude. A column the calibration set under-estimated is
a column whose activations grew. Clipping that column costs, where the same clip on a random
column is nearly free.

The analytic bound is not a substitute, and its failure is pure aim. It overshoots a real
column's accumulator by 57.7x median (28-266x), spending about six bits of the int8 output
on headroom nothing reaches. It saturates 0.000% of the surface. Its C ramp is meanwhile
nearly exact, with `worst_rel_err` 0.15% against the oracle's 0.59%. Under this source
`cs/cmax` is one constant in every unclipped column, so `C` lands exactly on its cap. Only
the integer rounding of `C` is left.

The ramp's resolution and the scale's aim are independent failure modes, and on this route
the aim is the one that costs.

The arms are the `_cal`, `_bnd` and `_calshuf` suffixes of
`rocket-userspace/tools/rk3576-w8a8-ppl.py`.
[host-computed, arithmetic, Qwen2.5-1.5B, 2026-08-11]

The second model, SmolLM2-1.7B, costs nearly three times as much, and its control splits
differently. It runs on its own eight windows and 144 offloaded projections:

| per-column scale source | ppl | x fp32 | top-1 vs fp32 | tightest column | saturating |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| oracle, exact per-column | 11.2366 | 1.0038 | 97.29% | 1.000x | 0.221% |
| oracle, through the shift-0 ramp | 11.2855 | 1.008 | 96.14% | 1.000x | 0.213% |
| **frozen over 2 calibration windows** | **12.6872** | **1.133** | 84.57% | **0.161x** | 0.176% |
| the analytic bound | 1.043e9 | 9.3e7 | 3.22% | 2.352x | 0.000% |
| the same perturbations, wrong columns | 12.0254 | 1.074 | 90.06% | 0.127x | 0.360% |

Its excess over its own exact-scale ceiling is 0.129 of ratio, against Qwen's 0.045. So the
scale source is the largest term on both models, but it is 2.9x worse on one of them. A port
that budgets from one model budgets low. Its tail is deeper too (0.161x, 6.2x too tight,
against 0.228x).

The control's share also differs by model. The information-free perturbation costs 0.070
of the 0.129 here, 54% of the damage, against 12% of Qwen's 0.045. So "the harm is the
correlation, not the magnitude" holds in direction on both models and in magnitude on
neither. On SmolLM2, about half of it is what any perturbation of that size costs. The
analytic bound is unusable on both, and its SmolLM2 number (9.3e7x, 65.9x median overshoot)
is a destroyed model rather than a measurement of a lever.

### A global safety factor on the frozen scale

A global safety factor on the frozen scale recovers 87% of the scale-source term, on both
models. The factor, `CALSAFE`, multiplies the frozen per-column colmax before it is
inverted. It trades output resolution everywhere for clipping headroom in the tail. The
windows and the calibration set are the same, with `NCAL`=2 throughout. The ratio and
saturation columns are over 1344 forwards on Qwen and 1152 on SmolLM2. The sweep runs one process per point.

| CALSAFE | Qwen ppl | x fp32 | ratio median | tightest | saturating | SmolLM2 ppl | x fp32 |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1.0 | 13.4451 | 1.047 | 1.083x | 0.228x | 0.180% | 12.6872 | 1.133 |
| 1.25 | 13.0510 | 1.016 | 1.349x | 0.273x | 0.021% | 11.8534 | 1.059 |
| **1.5** | **12.9395** | **1.008** | 1.614x | 0.308x | 0.004% | - | - |
| 2.0 | 12.9510 | 1.009 | 2.156x | 0.410x | 0.000% | **11.4165** | **1.020** |
| 3.0 | 12.9452 | 1.008 | 3.233x | 0.588x | 0.000% | - | - |
| 4.5 | 13.0487 | 1.016 | 4.846x | 0.889x | 0.000% | - | - |

The curve is a U with a broad flat floor, and one scalar takes most of the term. Qwen's best
point leaves 0.0058 of ratio over the exact-scale ceiling against 0.045 unfactored, 87%
recovered. SmolLM2's `CALSAFE`=2.0 leaves 0.016 against 0.129, 87.5%. The two models recover
the same fraction while differing 2.9x in the term's size. That is the strongest evidence
available that the lever acts on the mechanism rather than on a model's quirk.

The floor is flat from 1.5 to 3.0 and the walls are not steep, so this dial does not need
tuning per model. On Qwen it reads 1.008 at 1.5, 1.009 at 2.0 and 1.008 at 3.0, and 4.5 is
worse than the floor by 0.0085 of ratio. Below the floor, the cost is clipping the tail.
Above it, the cost is resolution spent on headroom nothing reaches. That is the same
failure the analytic bound takes to its limit at 57.7x median overshoot.

**Only Qwen's floor is mapped.** SmolLM2 ran 1.25 and 2.0 alone, so that 2 sits inside a
floor there too is [expected]. What is measured there is that 1.25 recovers 58% against
2.0's 87.5%, so err high rather than low.

The residual after the factor is roughly what the information-free control costs. Qwen's
0.0058 sits just above `_calshuf`'s 0.0052. So on that model, the safety factor removes
essentially all of the correlated clipping damage and leaves the size-of-perturbation term.
That residual is what a per-column or per-layer estimator would have to attack, and neither
is built.

### The calibration window count, `NCAL`

More calibration windows buy the same thing more slowly, and the two levers fall on one
curve. The other dial, `NCAL`, is how many disjoint windows the colmax is frozen over. The
table holds `CALSAFE` at 1.0:

| NCAL | Qwen ppl | x fp32 | ratio median | tightest | saturating | SmolLM2 ppl | x fp32 |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2 | 13.4451 | 1.047 | 1.083x | 0.228x | 0.180% | 12.6872 | 1.133 |
| 4 | 13.1409 | 1.023 | 1.156x | 0.250x | 0.088% | - | - |
| 8 | 13.0340 | 1.015 | 1.224x | 0.281x | 0.043% | 11.7297 | 1.048 |

Four times the calibration tokens recovers 71% of Qwen's gap and 66% of SmolLM2's, against
the free scalar's 87%. So the colmax has not converged at two windows, and it is still
climbing at eight. But sampling is the expensive way to buy what a multiplier gives.

Both dials act through the same quantity, and on Qwen the points fall on one curve in it.
Order every point by the median ratio of supplied colmax to the call's own. The perplexity
is monotone down to a floor and back up, whichever dial produced the point: 1.083x/1.047,
1.156x/1.023, 1.224x/1.015, 1.349x/1.016, 1.614x/1.008, 2.156x/1.009, 3.233x/1.008,
4.846x/1.016. The points `NCAL`=8 (median 1.224x, 1.015) and `CALSAFE`=1.25 (median
1.349x, 1.016) are indistinguishable in outcome.

The aimed dial does buy a better tail per unit of median looseness: 0.281x at median 1.224x
against 0.273x at 1.349x. The difference does not reach the model's score, though. **Treat
the two as interchangeable at equal looseness, and pick the one that costs nothing.** Their
composition was not run and is not in any number here.

The sweep has four blind spots:

- `CALSAFE` is one scalar for every column of every layer, so nothing here says whether a
  per-layer or per-column factor beats it.
- Both models are ~1.5B on one activation regime.
- A percentile over calibration rows, which moves the scale tighter, the wrong way for this
  tail, has never been run.
- The `NCAL` axis stops at eight windows, so where the colmax converges is unmeasured.

[host-computed, arithmetic, two models, 2026-08-10]

### The frozen colmax from two device passes

Every arm above freezes the exact per-column max of the calibration accumulator. No caller
of `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_perc()` can see it, because the entry writes int8 and the
accumulator never leaves the part. Computing it on the host means a host int32 GEMM per
calibration window per projection, which is the cost the offload exists to avoid.

Two device calls give it instead, from the part's own int8 output. Pass one runs the entry
at the analytic accumulator bound `127/(128*sum_k|B[n][k]|)`, which overshoots a real column
by ~60x median and so cannot saturate. It reads each column's largest `|C8|` back as a lower
bound on that column's magnitude. Pass two runs at that readback times a margin (1.5), so
the codes are spent, and reads it again.

A column that saturates in pass two keeps pass one's margined value. Both passes go through
the shift-0 integer ramp. They are de-quantized by the scale asked for rather than the gain
delivered, so the ramp's own error is inside the estimate.

The estimator is accurate, and its cost is not the estimator's size. Its `|est/true - 1|`
median over columns is 0.30% on Qwen2.5-1.5B and 0.31% on SmolLM2-1.7B, flat across every
shape. The worst single column is 2.4% / 8.3%, with 0.14% / 0.37% of columns falling back
from pass two. The table gives what that costs the model at `CALSAFE`=2.0, with both arms
in one process against one `fp32`:

| model | `_cal` (exact frozen colmax) | `_calboot` (two device passes) | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| Qwen2.5-1.5B | 12.9510, **1.0086x**, 95.41% top-1 | 12.9517, **1.0087x**, 96.02% | **0.000055** |
| SmolLM2-1.7B | 11.4165, **1.0199x**, 94.80% top-1 | 11.6192, **1.0380x**, 95.12% | **0.0181** |

A 0.3% estimator error is free on one model and costs 0.018 of ratio on the other. On
SmolLM2 it takes the recovered fraction from 90.5% to 76.0%. These two models show the same
split three times. The control `_calshuf` costs SmolLM2 54% of its damage against Qwen's
12%. The scale-source term itself is 2.9x larger there, and a sub-percent perturbation is
330x more expensive there.

The size of a perturbation does not price its cost on this route. Its correlation with the
columns that matter does, and SmolLM2 is where that correlation is expensive.

A dial that is flat for an exactly-frozen scale is not flat for an estimated one, and that
sets the shipped constant. Raising `CALSAFE` from 2.0 to 3.0 on SmolLM2 moves the exact arm
0.0017 and the bootstrapped arm 0.016:

| SmolLM2, `CALSAFE` | `_cal` | `_calboot` | gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2.0 | 11.4165, 1.0199x | 11.6192, 1.0380x | **0.0181** |
| 3.0 | 11.4356, 1.0216x | 11.4591, **1.0237x** | **0.0021** |

The gap falls 88%, taking the bootstrapped arm's recovery of that model's 0.129 term from
76.0% to 87.4%. That is the same fraction the exact freeze gets on both models. So the
estimator's 0.3% tightening lands on columns that were already near their clip, and margin
applied to all of them absorbs it. The default of `ROCKET_RK3576_CALSAFE` is therefore
**3.0**, the top of the flat floor, rather than its middle. Qwen's exact arm is 1.008x at
both 2.0 and 3.0, and its bootstrapped gap at 2.0 is 0.000055. So 3.0 being safe there too
is [expected], not measured.

Pass two is load-bearing, and the readout that says so is the absolute error. Pass one's
signed median ratio is 1.499x / 1.523x against a 1.5 margin, which looks like a perfect
estimator. Its own `|est/true - 1|` is 49.9% / 52.3% median and 563% / 230% at worst. At
the analytic bound a column reads back a couple of codes, so pass one is unbiased in the
median and enormous per column. **A signed median ratio that matches the margin exactly is
the statistic that hides this** [host-computed, arithmetic, two models, 2026-08-11].

### The two passes composed on the part, and what M does to them

The route above runs on the device end to end in `tests/rk3576_w8a8_route`. The operands are
pseudorandom, with a deliberate ~30x spread of `sum|w|` across the columns of one tile. The test
applies no rotation (the Hadamard's job is to make a real activation Gaussian, and these operands
already are). Its reference is the exact int32 accumulator on the host, which no frontend has.

| `M` | `K` | `N` | pass-1 `SHIFT` | pass-1 median &#124;err&#124; | est median | est worst | composed RMS rel | `worst_rel_err` | saturating |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1536 | 2048 | 39 | 53.1% | **0.455%** | 22268% | **15.3%** | 33.1% | 0.0000% |
| 512 | 1536 | 2048 | 39 | 6.9% | **0.283%** | 0.98% | **2.23%** | 0.417% | 0.0000% |
| 1 | 2048 | 2048 | 39 | 61.8% | **0.480%** | 611% | **18.1%** | 39.3% | 0.0000% |
| 256 | 2048 | 1024 | 39 | 12.2% | **0.296%** | 1.11% | **2.11%** | 0.655% | 0.0000% |

The estimator holds on the part. Its median per-column error is 0.28-0.48% at every `M`, against
the host simulation's 0.30% / 0.31%. The C ramp's integer gain, which the host arm does not
model at all, therefore does not dominate it. Pass one's signed median again lands on the margin
(0.97-1.63), while its absolute error is 6.9-61.8%.

This route reaches no shift bound, and cannot reach one. Pass one's scale is ~6e-8, which raises
the question of whether it drives the OUT_CVT shift out of DPU 0x40B4's six-bit field. The
planner, `rocket_rk3576_plan_perchannel()`, divides the scale it is handed by a `cmax` that
carries the same accumulator bound, `128*sum|w|+1`. With a NULL bias the two cancel exactly, so
`best_base` is the constant `127/INT32_MAX` whatever the weights are. The device therefore
programs `SHIFT`=39 at every shape, 24 values inside the field, and the per-column `C` comes out
at its `cmax` ceiling. The frozen call programs 27-34.

A host readout prints the same 39 flat over `sum|w|` from 128 to 1.7e7. Nothing
refuses an overflow either: `rocket_rk3576_requant_params()` carries no bound check. The RK3576
emitter writes `out_cvt_shift` unmasked, where the RK3588 path masks `& 0x3F`.

On this route `M` carries no shape constraint and a large accuracy one. A call at `M`=1
computes (`rc`=0, no row rule, the RK3588's height<4 is the opposite part), and its composed
error is 6.9-8.6x the prefill figure. The mechanism is the tile, not the estimator. A column's
frozen colmax is a max over `M` accumulator rows: tight at a prefill `M`, and one Gaussian draw
at `M`=1. The entry plans one `(MUL, SHIFT)` per output-channel tile, so the spread of `scale_n`
inside a tile sets every column's `C` resolution. The `ggml-rocket` frontend does not route `M`
below `rocket_min_m()` (128), so this is the library entry's envelope rather than a frontend
path.

At `M`=1, freezing from the exact column maximum is 13x worse than the bootstrap. The crossed
arm varies the colmax source and the `N` tile:

| `M` | `K` | colmax source | `NT` 2048 | `NT` 256 | `NT` 32 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1536 | bootstrap | 15.3% | 15.0% | **10.7%** |
| 1 | 1536 | exact | 201% | 148% | **70.7%** |
| 1 | 2048 | bootstrap | 18.1% | 18.1% | **14.5%** |
| 1 | 2048 | exact | 181% | 121% | **63.4%** |

The bootstrap's readback floors at one code: a column that reads back zero is credited one. That
floor bounds its colmax from below, and so bounds `scale_n` from above. The exact maximum has no
floor. At `M`=1, a column whose single accumulator draw is near zero is handed an enormous
scale. The entry takes `best_base = max_j(cs_j/cmax_j)` over the whole tile, so one such column
costs every other column in that tile its `C`.
[HW sweep, gate run, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_w8a8_route`, 2026-08-11]

The over-estimate that reads as the estimator's worst per-column error (22268%) is the same
floor that keeps the shared ramp usable. The code's stated reason for the floor is that a
too-tight scale costs a model, and at low `M` its larger effect is this one. A smaller tile
moves the error monotonically and does not close it. That prices the sort-then-tile lever on
this route at ~1.43x of the `M`=1 composed error at 64 tiles.
[HW sweep, gate run, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_w8a8_route`, 2026-08-11]

### The `rk_hadamard_ok()` shape gate

The RK3576 entry's rotation gate refuses one of the two models the accuracy numbers came from.
The gate is `ggml-rocket`'s `rk_hadamard_ok()`, which admits a power-of-two `K` and
`K = 60*2^k`, and nothing else. Qwen2.5-1.5B contracts every projection over `K`=1536, which is
`3*2^9`, not a power of two, and `1536 % 60 = 36`. That gate therefore refuses the whole model,
including the one whose 1.047x set the scale-source term's size.

Wherever `K` was not a power of two, the accuracy simulation used the block-diagonal H over the
largest power of two that divides `K`. That H is still orthonormal, so the product reconstructs
exactly. It smears an outlier over its block rather than over the whole contraction. That makes
it the weaker of the constructions, and the right fallback rather than a replacement.

**A shape gate written from one construction's availability is a different object from the
arithmetic a number was measured in.** Here the two disagreed on a whole model.

### The matmul entry's requant and per-column ramp

The matmul entry's requant is the shared host model, and the per-column fix runs on the device.
Every accuracy statement above is host arithmetic. The test `tests/rk3576_mm_requant` compares
the on-NPU requant against a host model of it, at the entry.

The device computes the shared model exactly. The model is
`sat8(round_half_to_even((acc + bias) * MUL >> shift))`, with `MUL` and `shift` from the
emitter's own QNNPACK derivation. It matches the part on 0 wrong of 1,396,736 elements. The
sweep covers five cells from `32x256x64` to `512x2048x1024` and four distinct `(MUL, SHIFT)`
pairs, and leaves nothing unwritten. Each cell runs at a scale filling the int8 range and at one
4x larger, which clips a quarter to a third of the surface. So `tests/requant_model.h` is
the matmul path's arithmetic as well as the convolution's.

The exact-float model's cost as an instrument is two to three orders of magnitude below what it
was used to decide. The accuracy simulations used an exact-float requant with round-to-even, and
it disagrees with the integer requant on 0.014% to 0.21% of elements. Every disagreement is
exactly 1 LSB. The perplexity numbers above stand as written.

A per-output-column output scale works on this datapath, expressed on the coefficient group's C
ramp, and the entry is `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_perc()`. The convolution path's per-axis
planner assumes an epilogue of `(acc + bias[n]) * C[n]` in saturating int32, then one shared
`(v*MUL)>>SHIFT` per task. The part does the same on the matmul plane: 0 wrong of 698,368
elements over twelve arms, six cells at two column-scale spreads each. One of the arms is
forced to eight N tiles, so the per-tile `(MUL, SHIFT)` re-plan is exercised. The planner is the
convolution's, not a copy.

**At GEMM depths, the int32 product binds the ramp, not C's int16 field.** A caller must read
this number rather than assume it. C is capped at `INT32_MAX / (128*sum_k|B[n][k]|)`, so the
operands set it, not the shape alone.

That cap belongs to the shift word at zero. Under a nonzero shift the BS product is held wide
and rounded after the multiply. It is exact to 2^45.95 on both signs [HW sweep, 2026-09-23 and
2026-09-27]. So the entry carries its gain on the shift. The planner sets each tile's largest
scale near C = 32767, with the smallest shift that keeps every column inside int32 on both
signs.

The shift ramp is device-exact against an independent host model [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, rocket
1.6.0, 2026-09-27]. It read 0 wrong of 1380352 elements over 42 arms. On Qwen2.5-1.5B's and
SmolLM2-1.7B's own projections, with rotated operands, its median column gain error is
0.0013-0.0035% [HW sweep, `tests/rk3576_mm_percol_err`]. Setting
`ROCKET_RK3576_MM_PC_SHIFT=0` selects the shift-0 ramp, whose median there is 0.10-0.33%. The
figures below describe that shift-0 ramp. The shift word is in [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).

The delivered worst-case relative gain error is roughly `0.5 * spread / C_max` and grows
linearly in both the contraction depth and the spread of the column scales.

The ramp's `C_max` differs by an order of magnitude between the probe and a real model, and the
probe is the pessimistic end. The gate's probe draws B from a full-range generator, so
`sum_k|B[n][k]|` ~ 64*K and `C_max` ~ `262144/K` = 128 at K=2048. In a real per-channel
quantized weight row, a few outlier weights set the channel's scale, so most weights land on
small integers. Such a row has mean `|Bq|` near 5.5, and the same K=2048 projection gets a
`C_max` of 424 to 1496.
[host-computed, arithmetic over the shipped planner, two models, 2026-08-10 and 2026-08-11]

Over SmolLM2-1.7B's 144 offloadable projections, a Hadamard rotation along K takes the median
`C_max` from 1496 unrotated to 424. The rotation raises `sum|Bq|` by spreading each row's mass,
and so costs 3.5x of the ramp's resolution. That is an interaction between the two levers a
port would compose. The same models' within-tile column-scale spread is 7.6-9.5x median (range
3.5-160.9). That gives a delivered `worst_rel_err` of 0.69-0.89% median and 3.4-7.3% max,
rather than the probe table's 3.0%.
[host-computed, arithmetic over the shipped planner, two models, 2026-08-10 and 2026-08-11]

Qwen2.5-1.5B's 168 projections agree on every input and reproduce the rotation's cost. Their
spread is 10.1x unrotated and 6.3x rotated, their `C_max` median goes 1848 -> 555 (3.3x), and
their `worst_rel_err` median is 0.45-0.56%.
[host-computed, arithmetic over the shipped planner, two models, 2026-08-10 and 2026-08-11]

The probe table below is therefore the instrument's own figure. The host model of the planner
reproduces it within 0.92-1.19x on the discriminating (spread) arms:

| K | flat columns | 8x column spread |
|---:|---:|---:|
| 256 | 0.042% | 0.341% |
| 512 | 0.080% | 0.611% |
| 1024 | 0.162% | 1.533% |
| 2048 | 0.220% | 2.96-3.01% |

Against an exact per-column scale, which the 1.004x / 1.002x simulation used, that ramp moves
2.2% of elements by 1 LSB at K=2048 flat. At K=2048 with an 8x spread it moves 12.7%, with a
few at 2. **So 1.004x is the route's ceiling and not this entry's number.** The term the
simulation did not include is this one.

The ramp's end-to-end cost to a model is small. A re-run of the perplexity arm models the ramp
in place of an exact per-column scale, as `chanout_ramp` and `hadamard_chanout_ramp` in
`tools/rk3576-w8a8-ppl.py`. It covers SmolLM2-1.7B over 8 windows of 512 wikitext-2 tokens,
144 offloadable projections:

| route | exact per-column scale | the shift-0 integer ramp |
|---|---:|---:|
| per-column output requant alone, SmolLM2 | 1.543x | **1.505x** |
| per-column output requant alone, Qwen2.5 | 1.061x | **1.080x** |
| Hadamard + per-column requant, SmolLM2 | 1.004x (97.29% top-1) | **1.008x (96.14%)** |
| Hadamard + per-column requant, Qwen2.5 | 1.002x (96.85%) | **1.0025x (96.63%)** |

On the composed route, the ramp therefore costs 0.4% of ratio on SmolLM2-1.7B and 0.06% on
Qwen2.5-1.5B. That is an eightfold difference between two models, both negligible. Both are two
orders of magnitude away from the per-tensor output scale the ramp replaces (1.11-2.26x). The
models disagree on the unrotated route: 1.543x against 1.061x for the same exact-scale arm.
[host-computed, arithmetic, 8 windows x 512 wikitext-2 tokens, 2026-08-11]

**Eight windows under-read SmolLM2's ramp cost 2.8x.** Over 32 windows its composed route reads
1.0165 through the shift-0 ramp and 1.0055 through the shift-word ramp. The exact scale reads
1.0048 [host-computed, arithmetic, 32 windows x 512 wikitext-2 tokens, 2026-09-27].
Qwen2.5-1.5B's arms sit in one noise band there, and a 4-chunk device perplexity cannot resolve
them either. Its two ramps differ by 0.012-0.095 PPL, and the sign flips under a perturbation
that carries no information. Those perturbations were the N tile and a 0.1% scale change
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, the W8A8 route, 2026-09-27].

**A model's unrotated int8 route can be chaotic, and SmolLM2-1.7B's is.** On SmolLM2-1.7B,
de-quantizing by the gain the ramp achieved reads 22.209x, against the requested scale's
1.505x. The achieved gain is `C[n] * MUL/2^SHIFT`, at most 2% from the requested scale. The
same substitution on Qwen2.5-1.5B reads 1.083x against 1.080x, a non-event. The blow-up is not
a property of the reconstruction:

- A per-element map covers the model's own layers, on either arm's own trajectory. In every
  band of every mapped projection, the achieved-gain reconstruction is equal or smaller in
  error. No `C` sits at either clip, and no column is floored.
- A per-layer magnitude trace localizes the divergence entirely to the last decoder layer, and
  that localization is false. Restoring the requested divisor in that one layer leaves
  2.24e10. The achieved divisor in that layer alone costs nothing (10.578 against a 10.577
  baseline).
- The depth sweep is non-monotone. The achieved divisor in the first L decoder layers gives
  3.6e10 at L=1, 10.68 at L=2 and 1.3e10 at L=3.
- The control settles it. An arbitrary +-0.5% per-column divisor jitter that carries no
  information, in layer 0 alone, gives 2.3e10, the same catastrophe. The same jitter over
  layers 0-1 gives 10.33.

The Hadamard rotation buys stability as well as accuracy. Under the identical perturbations,
the rotated route moves between 8.24 and 8.35 over four configurations, including the achieved
divisor everywhere. So the route the port would ship is not on the knife edge. The SmolLM2
numbers quoted for the unrotated route are samples from an unstable regime rather than
measurements of a lever.
[host-computed, arithmetic, 2026-08-11]

The achieved-gain question is settled and closed by a wash: on Qwen, 1.0828 against 1.0800
unrotated and 0.9989 against 1.0025 rotated. A signature change that returns the achieved gains
is therefore neither dangerous nor worth building.
[host-computed, arithmetic, 2026-08-11]

Two levers follow, and neither is built.

The first is the N tile, which sets the ramp's resolution as well as throughput. Forcing eight
tiles at K=1024 took an 8x-spread cell from 1.53% to 0.265%, because each tile then spans only
`8^(1/8)` = 1.30x. That measurement is the sorted case: the probe's column scales are monotone
in the column index, so consecutive tiles are narrow bands by construction. A real model's
unsorted scales would not see that effect. The convolution path already sorts channels by scale
(`r76_sort_by_scale`) and passes a permutation, where the matmul entry passes NULL.
[HW sweep, gate run, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, 2026-08-10]

The second lever is the accumulator bound itself. The bound `128*sum|w|` is the int8 worst
case, and a real GEMM's accumulator is ~90x below it at K=2048. That gap gives away about six
bits of C resolution. Tightening the bound needs a statistical bound, and therefore evidence
about whether `(acc + A)*C` saturates or wraps, which is unmeasured.
[HW sweep, gate run, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, 2026-08-10]

## Quantization

The RK3576 vendor path uses per-output-channel weight scales, and Mesa's single global scale
saturates most channels [source-confirmed]. The per-channel requant is the same as on the
RK3588, so the on-chip requant work (per-OC `OUT_CVT` MUL with a single global shift)
transfers. See [../encodings/out-cvt-converter.md](../encodings/out-cvt-converter.md).

## The DPU LUT and its input map

A nonlinear activation on this part is two programs. The first is a DPU-only program that
loads two 513-entry tables through an auto-incrementing window, and `gen_lut_load_rk3576()`
emits it. The second is an ordinary convolution with the LUT enabled: the direct int8
convolution plus a short register set. The load and the use belong in one job. The table lives
in the LUT RAM, and a separate submit can take a runtime-PM cycle.

The input map is `index = (value - LE_START) / 2^sel`, with the `0x00020000` table covering
`[LE_START, 0]` and the `0x00030000` table `[0, LO_END)`, at 512 intervals each. All
three parameters are live and programmable: `LE_START` (`0x4110`-`0x411C`, four lanes),
`LO_END` (`0x4140`-`0x414C`) and the index step (`0x410C`). The hardware interpolates linearly
between entries at the full resolution of the step. The domain is half-open at the top:
`value == LO_END` takes the overflow clamp. The map is bit-explained against a CPU model at
four spans of 8192 samples each, to at most one output count (`tests/rk3576_lut_probe.c`).
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

The value the LUT sees is the BS stage's output, `(acc + A[oc]) * C[oc]`. A caller therefore
places each output channel's range inside the window with the same per-channel `C` that a
per-axis quantization already uses. The vendor does this, which is why it never moves the
window. At fp16 the coefficient buffer carries that scale, not a register. That is why sigmoid
and tanh there emit register-identical programs, with tables spanning 6.25 and 3.02 input
units.

**BN_CFG `0x4060` is the trap.** Every vendor activation carries `0x20` there, against `0x903`
in its own bare-conv control. Transcribing that `0x20` pins the LUT at the table join for
every input, across twenty-two binades. The readout still tracks the table faithfully as the
table changes, so it reads as a working LUT whose input map is in no register. The BN stage is
then multiplying by an operand register no vendor program writes, on a part whose
register file is not cleared between jobs. Left at the generator's own `0x903`, the stage is
bypassed and the map appears.

The LUT itself is the EW stage: `EW_LUT_BYPASS` is bit 7 of `0x407C`, the RK3588's field
layout at a moved offset.

### The int8 activation op

The entry point over that map is `rocket_act_int8_rk3576()`. It computes sigmoid, tanh, swish,
hardswish, hardsigmoid and elu over a row-major `[C][H][W]` int8 tensor, with a per-tensor
quantization on both sides. The op deliberately omits `exp`, `gelu` and `sqrt`. No vendor
capture emits a 513-entry table for any of the three, so they lower some other way. A table for
them would be an invention.

The op is exact for every int8 input, not an interpolation of one. It lowers onto a depthwise
1×1 identity convolution whose weight is the LUT's own index step, `2^sel`. That weight puts
input value `q` at table index `q - in_zp + 512` exactly. Each of the 256 possible inputs
therefore reads its own entry, and the hardware's interpolation never runs. An identity weight
of 1 would have put all 256 inputs inside eight index intervals.

The per-output-channel `C` can express the same scaling, and this op does not need it. The
coefficient `A` fold already carries `-in_zp*sum_w`, so the input zero point comes out right
with no special case. That leaves `C` free for a fused conv->activation, where the accumulator
range differs per channel.

The identity must be depthwise. A 1×1 direct identity needs an `oc x ic` weight matrix, which
is quadratic in the channel count. At any real width that matrix is past the emitter's
`ic*kh*kw <= 4608` weight slice rule. Depthwise binds output channel `c` to input channel `c`.
The identity is then one weight per channel, and the layer is one task whatever the width.

The table has two scales. A datapath value `v` means the real number `in_scale * v / 2^sel`.
An entry is read in units of `out_scale * gain`, where `gain` is the OUT_CVT pair that the
emitter programs. The op reads that pair back from `rocket_rk3576_requant_params()` rather than
re-deriving it, since a table built against a slightly different gain is quietly off
everywhere. The op holds the conv scale at 1/128. That is the finest gain whose int16 entry
still spans the whole int8 output range a zero point can ask for (128 * 255 = 32640).

The two programs go out as one job, a multi-descriptor job. That is a third submit layout in
the conv driver, beside "one submit per row task" and "one chained kick". The conv driver needs
it because the chain rewrite links programs inside one BO, and the table load is a separate
one. A LUT call declines chaining and says so. That costs the single-kick saving, not
correctness.

The gate (`tests/rk3576_act_gate.c`) sweeps 6 kinds × 5 quantizations × 6 shapes, dense over
all 256 byte values. The result is 180 of 180 bit-exact against a CPU model of the chip's own
arithmetic. No output is more than one count from an exact `f()`. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

### The activation fused into a convolution epilogue

The activation fuses into a convolution's epilogue. The standalone op invents a convolution to
reach the LUT. A real convolution layer needs no invented one, and the LUT sits in the EW stage
of the same program. So `rocket_conv2d_int8_act_rk3576()` costs only the table-load task: the
activation is free.

The fused form is different arithmetic from conv-then-activation, and the better one. The
standalone op reads the convolution's requantized int8 output, an alphabet of 256 values, and
the fused form reads the accumulator before any rounding. What the fused form gives up is
exactness. The standalone form puts each of its 256 inputs on its own table entry. A real
accumulator lands between entries, so the hardware's linear interpolation runs.

Over 28 cases (six kinds, direct and depthwise, three quantizations), the fused form is at most
one count from an exact `f()`. The cases put the pre-activation inside the curved region rather
than on a saturating tail. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_act_gate.c fused`]

The conv entries' contract needs no change, even though the standalone op pins its scale at
1/128. Three quantities follow from the three scales a caller already passes:

- **The value unit.** With a per-tensor requant, the BS multiplier `C` is unity. The value the
  LUT sees is then the accumulator itself, `A` included, and `A` is where the bias and the
  zero-point fold already are. One count of it means `in_scale*w_scale`.
- **The entry unit.** OUT_CVT scales the table's output by the gain the emitter derives from
  `in_scale*w_scale/out_scale`, so an entry is read in units of `out_scale*gain`. The
  standalone op must pin its conv scale to make that well defined. A real conv has the model's
  own three.
- **The step.** The window is the symmetric `[-512*2^sel, 512*2^sel)`, so `sel` is the smallest
  whose real half-span covers the domain the activation still varies over. Past it, the LUT
  returns its endpoint clamp, which is the right answer for a saturating kind. For a kind that
  keeps growing, the bound is where the int8 output saturates instead, since `f(x)` is `x` by
  then.

**Two bounds are refused rather than computed wrong**, and both refusals name
`in_scale*w_scale` as the quantity to change. The first is a value unit too fine for the
table's peak to fit an int16. An unbounded kind over a fine unit reaches it. Swish and elu at
`in_scale*w_scale = 1.6e-4` need 49151 entries, and the table saturates at 32767, which would
give a flat-topped curve. The second is a domain that no `sel` reaches.

Per-output-channel `C` is the lever this leaves open. It divides the value unit per channel.
That is how a fused conv whose channels have very different accumulator ranges puts each inside
`[LE_START, LO_END)`. The product `(acc + A)*C` saturates at int32, a bound to plan inside. The
per-tensor form above needs none of it.

Two reference traps both read as hardware faults. **A liveness check against the same conv
without the LUT must allow a rounding.** A one-count difference between the two surfaces is the
requant's tie-to-even against a reference that rounds half away from zero, not the activation
doing something. **ELU under an output quantization with no negative range is the identity
clamped at zero**, which the DPU's int8 saturation already computes. On that quantization the
fused and plain surfaces are legitimately identical, so a differs-from-plain assertion fires on
a working LUT.

## The precision fork: what the op library should be founded on here

The RK3588's composed op library is built on the fp16 matmul. It comprises:

- The norms
- Softmax
- The gated FFN
- Attention
- The ViT/Whisper encoder blocks

That foundation does not port to this part, and the choice of what to put in its place is a
decision rather than a port.

The fp16 convolution program in `librocketnpu` is not a matmul precision here. One int8 task
contracts `ic*kh*kw <= 4608`, and one task of that fp16 program contracts exactly sixteen input
channels. So the same K costs one submit against 288, and the measured ratio runs 30x to 299x
over ordinary shapes. Anything that lowers to that program inherits it.

The sixteen channels are the program's bound, not the part's. The charsiu fp16 matmul program,
emitted by its own `charsiu_emit_job` at `524e10a`, contracts K 32, 64, 256, 1024 and 2048
exactly in one job. That holds at N 64 and M 1, and at K 256 with M 4. Its fp32 output matched
an fp16-rounded CPU reference bit for bit. The fills are period-free, and their sums are exact
in any order [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 7.2.3-1, `rocket` 1.6.0, 2026-09-24]. The same job with the
wrong weight layout came back wrong on 64 of 64 outputs, so the check can fail.

The charsiu program differs from this project's fp16 program where this record puts the bound.
It reads the activation row major, `[m][k]`, and sets CNA `0x1090` to `ic/8` for that layout.
It also sets bit 29 of CNA `0x100C`, which reads `0x20200120` in full. Its output stage is the
w4a16 one: CORE `0x3018` at `0x10000200`, and three DPU words (`0x4028`, `0x4030`, `0x40b8`)
in closed forms of the channels and rows. The output is a flat `m` by `n` fp32 surface, and the
weight buffer is 16 output channels by 32 input channels of two-byte elements.

**Its fp32 output poisons neither its own kind nor this project's int8 programs.** Four jobs
back to back in one process were exact on every rep [HW sweep, same board, 2026-09-24]. After
it, this project's int8 direct program wrote 20 of 20 in one process at a zero gap. Controls in
the same run poisoned 20 of 20 [HW sweep, same board, 2026-09-25]. The section on the poisoning
has the detail.

This project's own emitter for the program, `gen_matmul_fp16_rk3576()`, computes exactly. It
carries the stream word for word, with 23 of its 148 words as closed forms of the shape
(M, K, N). They reproduce 441 of charsiu's streams, swept over M 1-32, K 32-4096 and N 16-1024.
On the H96, `tests/rk3576_mm_fp16_gate` runs 23 one-task shapes exact to the bounds below, and 6
shapes of 2 to 25 tasks. Its row-major weight control comes back wrong on 64 of 64
[HW sweep, gate run, `rocket` 1.6.0, 2026-09-25]. The gate's fills make every sum exact in fp32,
so it cannot see rounding.

The part sums each group of 32 products along K without rounding, then accumulates the groups
in fp32. Each group sum is rounded to fp32, and the group sums are added in K order, both
roundings to nearest even. That model matches every output bit for bit at M 4, N 64 and K from
32 to 2048. The fills carry full fp16 mantissas, with products spread over 2^-28 to 2^-6.

Groups of 16 or 64, or a rounding per product, miss by up to hundreds of ulps at K 2048. The group
is the weight cube's 32 input channels, and two runs are identical. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0,
`tests/rk3576_mm_fp16_round`, 2026-09-25]

The same program contracts bf16 operands when CORE `0x3018` names them. Set the word's
operand type, bits [10:8], to 3 and fill both buffers with bf16 bytes. One task is then
bit-exact at 16×256×64 and 64×1024×128. That holds on the gate's integer fill and on a fill
scaled to 2^38 and 2^-51, which fp16 cannot represent. So the datapath carries bf16's 8-bit
exponent rather than narrowing to fp16.

It accumulates as the fp16 program does. On full 8-bit mantissas the 32-product group model
matches every output bit for bit. That holds at M 4, N 64 and K 32 to 2048, and two runs are
identical.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, `tests/rk3576_mm_bf16_probe`, 2026-09-26]

Only the CORE word selects the type. The probe left CNA `0x100C`'s precision fields, DPU
`0x4010`'s processing precision and the scale table at fp16's values in several combinations.
Every one returned the same exact surface. bf16 bytes under the unmodified fp16 program come
back wrong on every element, so the check can fail. An fp16 control after the bf16 runs stays
exact, so a bf16 task leaves no poisoning behind it.

No library entry emits bf16. The probe runs one task a shape, so M tiling across tasks and
the envelope's K and N bounds are untested for this type. The program differs from fp16's in
one field, so the bounds are predicted to match [expected].

On the device, the fp16 program costs what int8 costs at N 64, and more at K 2048 N 256.
From submit to fence, with its buffers packed once, the program takes 0.076-0.084 ms to K 2048
at N 64. That is the int8 entry's per-submit cost there. At K 2048 N 256 it takes 0.12-0.19 ms.

In one process at those two shapes, the fp16 program's device time is 0.122 ms at 1×2048×256
and 0.173 ms at 16×2048×256. The whole pooled int8 resident call is 0.102 ms and 0.136 ms, device time
included. The fp16 weight is twice the int8 weight's bytes, which is the likely cause
[hypothesis]. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, 786 MHz, both clusters `performance`,
`rk3576_mm_fp16_cost`, 2026-09-25 UTC]

Per call, a resident weight closes the host gap. The per-call entry is 2.9-3.3x the int8
entry at K up to 256 and 1.9-2.3x beyond, 0.96-4.19 ms against 0.30-2.16 ms. It allocates
five buffers a call, one a 257 KiB coefficient buffer that depends on N alone.
`rocket_matmul_fp16_rk3576_wbo()` holds the weight and the coefficient buffer in a handle,
and is bit-identical to the per-call entry at all 18 gated shapes. Paired in one process
against `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_perc_wbo()`, the resident int8 entry an LLM's GEMMs call:

| Shape (M×K×N) | fp16 resident | int8 resident | Ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 14 shapes to K 2048 at N 64, or K 512 at N 256 | 0.23-0.26 ms | 0.26-0.29 ms | 0.86-0.94x |
| 1×2048×256 | 0.32 ms | 0.30 ms | 1.07x |
| 16×2048×64 | 0.43 ms | 0.38 ms | 1.15x |
| 16×2048×256 | 0.59 ms | 0.40 ms | 1.50x |

Two passes agree within 2%. The corners are the device term above, plus larger per-call
buffers: the fp16 activation is twice the bytes and its output four times. What is left per
call is buffer allocation, which fits as about 0.03 ms a BO plus 9 us a 4 KiB page. With
`ROCKET_RK3576_BO_POOL=1` and one entry per process, the resident call sits 0.014-0.043 ms
above its device time at every shape. [HW sweep, same board and conditions, 2026-09-25 UTC]

**A pooled cost depends on the pool's history.** A pooled BO keeps the size it was
allocated at, and a request takes the smallest free BO that covers it. After the mix of
entries or shapes changes, a small request can hold a far larger buffer. `PREP_BO` and
`FINI_BO` sync the whole BO every call.

Five entries sharing one pool put the fp16 resident call 0.09-0.16 ms above its device time
at 9 of 18 shapes, not monotone in any size. The resident entry alone, whose three buffers
are all small, sat 0.014-0.043 ms above at all 18. The per-call entry alone still steps, from
0.147 to 0.185 ms between its first two shapes. By the matching arithmetic, its 8 KiB weight
request settles onto a 257 KiB BO the coefficients left [hypothesis]. [HW sweep, same board,
2026-09-25 UTC]

### One task's envelope

Run as one task past the gated shapes, the program has three bounds, each found on the part
(`tests/rk3576_mm_fp16_envelope`, 49 shapes):

- **The input must fit the CBUF data window.** A row of the activation takes `K/32` CBUF
  entries, and `(K/32)·M` must be at most 4096, the data window CNA `0x1040` sets. M is not
  rounded: 85×1536, 1365×96, 21×6144 and 512×256 are exact. One row more at each comes back
  wrong on every row, not short by the rows past the window, and wrong the same way twice.
  The vendor's 4,940 fp16 streams all keep this surface under 4096, so that pattern is the
  part's bound and not a compiler habit. The charsiu int8 surface runs to 8192 entries, and
  why that wider window does not carry to fp16 is open.
- **K is at most 6144.** K 6144 is exact, and K 6176 never reports completion, the same
  boundary the int8 program has.
- **N is at most 8192.** At N 8960 the job writes exactly the first 768 columns and hangs.
  That count is 8959 mod 8192 plus one, so CNA `0x1024`'s kernel count is 13 bits wide
  [hypothesis].

Nothing else the sweep reached bounds one task. A 96 MiB weight at K 6144 N 8192 is exact, and
so is M×N to 4096×2048, where `0x40B8`'s closed form wraps. At every one of these shapes the
charsiu emitter writes the same 148 words as this project's. So nothing in the stream changes
at the window.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, 2026-09-25]

A row tile is an offset into the row-major activation and output. So past the window
`rocket_matmul_fp16_rk3576()` cuts M into equal tasks and submits them as one job of that many
task descriptors, one fence a call. Six shapes of 2 to 25 tasks are exact, to 2048×1536×256.
[HW sweep, gate run, same board]

### The price at a prefill's M

Past one task the fp16 call costs about twice the int8 call, and the gap does not shrink as M
grows. Both entries are resident and paired within a round, over two processes with the pool off:

| Shape (M×K×N) | fp16 resident | int8 resident | Ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 16×1536×1536 | 1.06 ms | 0.67-0.71 ms | 1.5-1.6x |
| 64×1536×1536 | 2.44-2.46 ms | 1.25-1.26 ms | 2.0x |
| 128×1536×1536 and 256×1536×1536 | 4.7 and 9.1 ms | 2.2 and 4.1-4.2 ms | 2.2x |
| 512×1536×1536 | 17.5-17.8 ms | 8.5-8.7 ms | 2.0-2.1x |
| 512×1536×256 | 7.4-7.6 ms | 4.1 ms | 1.8-1.9x |
| 512×1536×4096 | 37.6-38.2 ms | 18.0-19.3 ms | 2.0-2.1x |
| 512×1536×8192 | 84.4-85.5 ms | 32.3-32.6 ms | 2.6x |
| 512×256×1536 | 11.7-11.8 ms | 4.3 ms | 2.7x |
| 512×4096×1536 | 39.0-39.6 ms | 20.5-21.1 ms | 1.9-2.0x |
| 512×6144×1536 | 86.2-86.4 ms | 87.1-90.3 ms | 1.0x |
| 512×2048×2048 | 24.6-24.7 ms | 12.5-13.0 ms | 1.9-2.0x |

The device's share decides it. At 512×1536×1536 the fp16 job takes 4.5 ms from submit to
fence, and the int8 entry's four submits take 2.1 ms by its own profile. Each fp16 task
re-reads the whole weight, and at that shape the program contracts at about half the int8
program's rate. K 6144 ties because the int8 entry slows there, not because the fp16 program
speeds up.

The rest of the fp16 call is its per-call buffers. Allocating and freeing them is 7.2 ms of
the 17.5-17.8. With `ROCKET_RK3576_BO_POOL=1`, one entry a process, the fp16 call is 6.9 ms
against the int8 call's 4.8, 1.4x. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, 786 MHz, governor
`performance`, A72s, `rk3576_mm_fp16_cost resident`, 2026-09-25 UTC]

### The int8 route's host terms

An fp16 route would skip three host passes the int8 route makes around every GEMM: rotating and
quantizing the activation, and dequantizing the output. It would add one, narrowing the
activation to fp16. The frontend profiler prices each per GEMM shape, inside a model.

Qwen2.5-1.5B F16 on llama.cpp b10356, `pp2048 -ub 512`, four A72 threads, governor
`performance`, BO pool off. Four processes, 540-560 converged calls a shape each, two of them
also timing an FCVTN narrowing of the same activation:

| M 512, K 1536, N | Projections | Calls reusing the previous activation | `quant+rot` | `dequant` | fp16 narrowing |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 1536 | q, o | none | 7.48-7.56 ms | 0.90-0.91 ms | 0.50 ms |
| 256 | k, v | all | 7.48-7.56 ms | 0.152 ms | 0.50 ms |
| 8960 | gate, up | half | 7.39-7.60 ms | 5.32-5.41 ms | 0.50 ms |

`quant+rot` depends on M·K alone, about 9.5 ns an element on the one thread that dispatches.
The entry reads 1-10% above its resident price from the library probe, and the processes read
12.70-12.83 t/s. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, 786 MHz,
2026-09-25]

Route against route, the int8 route is those host terms plus the resident int8 call. The fp16
route is the narrowing plus the resident fp16 call. The fp16 entry takes N up to 8192, so N 8960
is priced as two calls of N 4480. The fp16 device time grows faster than N: 11.3 ms at N 4480
and 35.7 ms at N 8192. Resident prices are one entry a process, 30 rounds.

| Shape | int8 route, pool off / on | fp16 route, pool off / on | fp16/int8, off / on |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 512×1536×1536 | 17.0 / 13.2 ms | 18.4 / 7.4 ms | 1.08x / 0.56x |
| 512×1536×256 | 11.7 / 9.5 ms | 8.1 / 3.0 ms | 0.69x / 0.32x |
| 512×1536×8960 | 50.9 / 41.9 ms | 81.5 / 34.5 ms | 1.60x / 0.82x |
| One layer, two of each | 159 / 129 ms | 216 / 90 ms | 1.36x / 0.70x |

[HW sweep, same board and date, `rk3576_mm_fp16_cost resident`]

The host terms decide the pooled comparison and not the unpooled one. At 512×1536×1536 they
come to 7.9 ms net of the narrowing. That is above the pooled fp16 excess of 2.1 ms and below
the unpooled one of 9.3 ms. Unpooled, the fp16 call is its buffers: 29 of its 40.5 ms at N 4480.

This is what that comparison is worth in the prefill wall. The offloaded GEMMs are about 17.4 s
of the 160 s a `pp2048` takes here, 11%, with the pool off. Pooled, an fp16 route takes 4.4 s
off, 2.7% of the wall. Unpooled it adds 6.1 s. Those two figures are arithmetic over measured
per-call terms, since the route is not built.

Half of the pooled 4.4 s is repeat work. The k and v projections rotate and quantize q's
activation again, and up does gate's. Quantizing each activation once removes 2.5 s by the same
arithmetic. The rest is single-threaded host work beside three idle A72s [expected].

This comparison does not price:

- Accuracy (the int8 route is 1.011x perplexity)
- An fp16 K split for `ffn_down` (K 8960 is past the fp16 entry's 6144)
- Another model or `-ub`
- Calibration

The int8 route's first two forwards calibrate, 21.9-22.1 s a process here. A `llama-bench`
warm-up hides that, and a process's first prompt pays it. An fp16 route has no calibration.

### Outcome of the precision fork

The fork does not turn on speed. The int8 route's host terms buy back the fp16 GEMM's
excess only with the BO pool on. Even then they buy 2.7% of this model's prefill wall, and half
of that is removable on the int8 side. At M 16 the matmul form costs 0.86-1.15x the int8
resident entry and needs no power-cycle guard. At a prefill's M its GEMM costs about twice the
int8 one.

int8 carries the GEMMs but not the reductions. The entry `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576()` is
fast and bit-exact, but its output goes through the DPU's requant and is int8. The RK3588
builds RMSNorm, softmax's row sums and every ReduceMean as a matmul by a ones vector. That
reduction needs an accumulator wider than its operands, and an int8 output saturates on the
first long row. The 32-bit entry, `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_i32()`, runs on the dense writer
(§"A whole output stage that does not poison"). It poisons nothing, idles only on a redo, and
one task writes a 38 KiB surface exactly.

The dense writer removes two of the three reasons the 32-bit entry on the older writers is
the wrong instrument for a per-layer norm. The older writers poison the next submit and pay a
power cycle for it, and their surface is bounded at 8 KiB a task. The matmul gate's 21 int32
shapes are exact on the dense writer in 0.26 s, against 6.3 s on the older ones
(`ROCKET_RK3576_I32_DENSE=0`). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, rocket 1.6.0, 2026-09-30]

On the narrow writer the shape M 512 K 8192 N 2048 takes the part down until a reboot. On the
dense writer it runs in 345.7 ms and takes 48 submits with no redo. Its 4096 sampled elements
are exact [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, rocket 1.6.0, 2026-09-30]. The third reason is unmeasured on
it: the older writers drop atoms at a rate the memory system sets, so the sentinel scan stays.

On this part the transformer composition stays on the CPU, and the NPU runs four kinds of op:

- Convolution
- int8 GEMM
- Pooling
- LUT activations

That call rests in part on the fp16 cost above. At a prefill's M the fp16 GEMM costs about
twice the int8 one, which the int8 host terms do not repay, so the call stands.

The three pieces that argue for the call are all on the convolution/DPU side rather than the
matmul side. The direct and depthwise int8 convolutions and the int8 first conv are bit-exact
and 10-76x a scalar CPU reference. The PPU gives pooling as an independent 31-write program in
the cube the conv path already packs, and the DPU LUT gives the nonlinear activations. That is
a CNN/detection/classification stack, and it is the one this silicon is shaped for.

All four pieces that decision named are built and gated:

- Per-output-channel weight scales on the direct and depthwise paths and through a graph
- The PPU pooling emitter
- The DPU LUT and its activation entry
- A whole network

Revisit the transformer question only if a use for it appears. Then the first question is
whether an int32 accumulation path can be made cheap enough to sit inside a norm. Making the
fp16 path faster is not that question.

## A whole network

MobileNetV1-224 at int8 runs end to end on this part and returns TFLite's own label. The
graph is 27 convolutions, a PPU pooling layer and a classifier. Every one of the 29 compute
layers is bit-exact against a CPU model of the part's own arithmetic. The whole graph's wall
per inference depends on which levers are in place:

| Configuration | Wall per inference |
|---|---:|
| Resident weights, tensors kept in cube layout between layers, each run of cube-linked layers as one hardware kick, and the pooling layer retiring on the PPU's own completion | **5.1 ms** |
| The same without the PPU completion | 6.2 ms |
| One submit per layer | 10.4-10.5 ms |
| The transposes at every layer boundary | 21 ms |
| No resident weights at all | 115 ms |

The sections below take that difference apart and measure each part of it.

The gate is `tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`. Its input is a blob that
`tests/data/rk3576-net/mknet.py` flattens from a per-tensor-quantized `.tflite`. The blob
carries TFLite's output for every intermediate tensor. A wrong layer is therefore named by its
own index, not by a wrong label 27 layers later.

### The graph planner

The planner is the product, separate from the gate. The placement and linking passes live in
`graph/rocket_graph_rk3576.c`:

- One buffer per add and per concatenation, with a slice per operand
- The declared tails
- The shared surfaces
- The adjacent-pair join, with its refusal buckets
- The caller of the run finder

The passes run over a frontend-neutral layer description:

- Kind
- Geometry
- The operand graph, by producer
- The resident handles
- One flag for the thing no rule there can derive: whether the caller prepares that layer's
  input on the host

The gate is the planner's first caller and owns what a caller owns:

- The weights
- The tensors
- The ping-pong
- The skip lifetimes
- The de-scatter points

The planner is a separate target that the frontends link. It is not part of `librocketnpu`,
which holds no graph-execution object. The run finder (`rocket_chain_plan_rk3576()`) is in
the library because it is pure, and it is the one thing a frontend cannot own.

Twelve refusal classes have been closed so far, and **every one was a host-buffer rule rather
than the part's**. That is why the planner is shared rather than copied: a second copy would
fork where being wrong is silent. A frontend that uses the planner gains the whole ladder on
MobileNetV1-224. Per-op entries with transient weights take ~115 ms, resident weights ~21,
cube layout 10.4-10.5 and one kick 5.0. A frontend that calls only the op entries gets about
a twentieth of the demonstrated throughput. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

The move into `graph/` preserves behavior. Every join, refusal bucket, run boundary, submit
and task count and TFLite distance is identical on four graphs. Inception V3 differs only in
two pooling layers' submit counts, which vary run to run on one binary because they are the
divisor lag's redos. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

### The TFLite delegate

A second caller runs the planner: the TFLite delegate, on real `.tflite` files. The delegate,
`tflite-rocket`, lowers a delegated partition onto the same neutral description and runs the
plan. The part is therefore reachable from an unmodified `.tflite` through the ordinary
external-delegate API, rather than only from a hand-built blob.

Each row is warm on the H96 MAX M9 (kernel 7.1.6, 786 MHz), on a real image, median of 19. The
CPU column is the same interpreter with no delegate. The last three columns are the nodes
claimed, the adjacent-pair joins and the hardware kicks that the plan produced:

| model | delegate | CPU | claimed | joins | kicks |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1-224 | 5.2 ms | 71.7 | 29/31 | 28/28 | 1 |
| MobileNetV2-224 | 6.8 ms | 45.3 | 64/66 | 63/63 | 1 |
| ResNet-18-224 | 8.8 ms | 167.1 | 36/38 | 27/27 | 1 |
| Inception V1-224 | 7.4 ms | 180.5 | 81/83 | 53/53 | 1 |
| Inception V3-299 | 34.5 ms | 669.3 | 130/132 | 90/94 | 16 |
| SSD-MobileNetV2-COCO | 17.0 ms | 109.0 | 87/111 | 70/82 | 6 |
| EfficientDet-Lite0-320 | 120.3 ms | 122.7 | 231/267 | 85/89 | 10 |

Every classifier returns the interpreter's own top-1. The plans are identical to the blob's,
join for join and run for run. That is the check that two independent lowerings of the same
five networks are the same lowering.

On the four graphs where both take the same stem, the delegate's wall runs 0.3-0.7 ms above
that of `rk3576_net_gate`. That difference holds the interpreter's own invoke, the two
boundary transposes and the harness difference. None of the three is measured apart, so no
cap is quoted for it. ResNet-18 is 8.8 against the gate's 6.6, because the gate takes the
packed-image stem there and the delegate does not. The delegate attempts that encoding only at
a zero input zero point, where the surface stays a cube.

### Claim-time refusals

The detectors carry two node kinds that no hand-built graph produces. A `DEPTHWISE_CONV_2D` of
four or fewer channels **has no form on this part**. The packed-image first conv folds the
kernel's columns into the channel axis, so nothing is left to be depthwise over. The library
refuses `direct_datapath` on a depthwise descriptor rather than ignoring it. [HW sweep, H96 MAX
M9, 2026-08-04]

A `QUANTIZE` on a narrow tensor hits the same wall through its lowering, since the
requantization edge's natural form is a depthwise 1×1 identity. On four or fewer channels it
takes the direct identity instead, which is the same arithmetic and sixteen weights. An SSD
box-encoding branch is exactly both shapes. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

**Both must be answered at claim time.** A layer the library will not pack is a Prepare
failure, and a Prepare failure fails the whole model. An unclaimed node, by contrast, is one
the framework runs itself. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

A claim-time refusal is cheaper than a late one by more than a prepare. The delegate asks every
bound that the library owns and that is decidable from the shape before it claims a node:

- The pooling plan
- The per-axis gain ramp
- Through `rocket_conv2d_int8_plan_rk3576()`, the convolution geometry, the resident weight
  slice and the row window

The row window shows why this matters. The pack never reaches it, only the run's per-task plan
does. **A model that lands past it builds cleanly, passes `AllocateTensors`, and throws at
every `Invoke`.** That failure comes later than the one the check was written for, and past
any load-time validation a caller can do.

A probe model of three layers shows both halves. Its middle convolution is ic=256 oc=32 8×8
k5, a 200 KiB resident slice that is past the CBUF pool at a single output-channel group.
Without the claim-time check, `rocket_conv2d_int8_prepacked_rk3576` returns -5 from inside the
partition and the whole model dies. With it, 2 of 3 nodes are claimed, and the refused
