convolution runs on the CPU between two delegated partitions. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
2026-08-04]

Nothing in the corpus reaches any of these bounds, so **a test of them must be written rather
than found**. This one is `tflite-rocket/tools/mk_claim_probe.py`. The anti-drift control is
`rk3576_conv_lib_gate claimplan`, which needs no device. Over all 160 shapes of the envelope
table, it asserts the pure plan's verdict against `lib_refuse`, the run's own. It reads 0
accept-a-refusal and 0 over-refusal. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

### Detector accuracy

Both detectors score at CPU parity once two per-axis fixes are in. The metric is mAP@[.5:.95]
over 500 COCO val2017 images, from the same interpreter with and without the delegate
(`tflite-rocket/tools/coco_map.py`):

| model | delegate | CPU | delta |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| SSD-MobileNetV2 | 0.2617 | 0.2631 | -0.0014 |
| EfficientDet-Lite0 | 0.2826 | 0.2823 | +0.0003 |

That is the comparison a detector admits. Its output is an NMS-ordered list, so one count of
arithmetic drift reorders or drops a box. An element-wise diff against the CPU therefore says
nothing. On SSD it disagrees on 4 of 20 class ids and 75 of 80 box coordinates, while 19 of
20 scores agree to 0.02.

EfficientDet-Lite0 is the first model with per-axis weights to reach the part through a
frontend. Without the two fixes it scores 0.0000. It emits exactly 25 boxes an image, almost
all class 1, with scores pinned in [0.3125, 0.5]. That is a degenerate head, not drift.

The discriminator for what a per-axis defect costs is a per-axis classifier, whose label is an
assertion a detector cannot carry. The script `mkresnet18.py --per-axis` builds one from the
same weights and the same calibration set as the per-tensor ResNet-18. The quantization
granularity is then the only variable. The scorer, `rk3576_net_ab.py --images DIR`, measures
top-1 agreement against the CPU as a rate over 100 real photographs rather than on one image.
The per-axis build reads 0 of 100 against the per-tensor build's 93 of 100. Two independent
defects lie under that reading, and neither is the arithmetic `rk3576_perchannel_gate` asserts.

### The per-channel C ramp floor

**The per-channel C ramp has a floor, and below it a channel computes at the tile's gain
instead of its own.** A tile is one task, and a task carries one `OUT_CVT` shift. A channel
therefore reaches its own scale through the int16 `C` in its coefficient group and nothing
else. The base gain is already the smallest the tile admits, and every channel's `C` must fit
that channel's own accumulator bound. A channel whose scale sits below half the base
therefore wants a `C` under one, and the field's floor is one.

Such a channel is then wrong by a factor. The packer's own log reads 311% and 8150% on two
per-axis ResNet-18 layers, and past 100% on 7 of EfficientDet's 182 per-channel layers. The
surfaces are still full, correctly sized and entirely plausible. The clamp is the boundary,
and it is the ramp's own rather than a chosen threshold. Above it, `C` is an integer and the
resolution is `0.5/C`, which is what the tile search spends submits to improve. Below it there
is no `C` to choose.

Equivalently, a clamped channel is exactly one whose relative gain error exceeds 100%. So the
layer is refused. The pure call `rocket_conv2d_int8_perchannel_plan_rk3576()` asks that
question. The delegate asks it at claim time, where a refusal costs one node that the
framework runs itself. The median EfficientDet layer is 0.021%.

The depthwise path is where it bites. A depthwise layer is one task whatever its channel
count, so it has no tile lever at all. Of the 7 clamped layers, 6 are depthwise (1152, 1152,
1152, 672, 240, 32) and 1 is direct: the stem, already at the emitter's floor of 32. With
`ROCKET_RK3576_PC_ALLOW_CLAMP=1` the layer computes anyway, at a cost of 0.0428 mAP on
EfficientDet: 0.2395 against the CPU's 0.2823, where refusing scores 0.2809.

### Inert clamped channels

What clamps is a pruned channel, and a clamp is only a defect where it reaches the surface.
Every one of the 111 clamped channels across those six depthwise layers, and the stem's four,
has an all-zero filter. An all-zero tensor is quantized against the quantizer's own floor
(TFLite's 1e-6). Its scale therefore sits four to five orders below its neighbors', while its
int32 bias still carries the layer's magnitude. That is exactly the shape that puts a channel
below half the tile's base gain.

A channel with no live weight reaches exactly one accumulator, the zero-point fold `A`, at
every output position. That is because the MAC term is zero whatever the feature cube holds.
For such a channel the question is whether the byte differs, not how wrong the gain is. That
question is decidable exactly, with no bound and no sampling.

The byte usually does not differ. A large negative fold saturates to -128 under either gain,
and that covers 105 of those 111. The library's rule is written as saturation rather than as
equality. Both gains must drive the fold past the same rail by more than a count. The answer
then cannot depend on the DPU's rounding, which is modeled here rather than run.

A live channel is refused without asking, since its accumulator ranges over `+-128*sum|w|`
around `A`. A gain wrong by a factor is wrong somewhere in that range.

The rule takes EfficientDet-Lite0 from 7 refusals to 4. The three layers it accepts are the
stem, the first depthwise layer and the 240-channel one, and every per-op instrument says
those three are exact. In `rk3576_perchannel_gate`, the `pruned` and `dw-pruned` cells measure
0.0 counts of distance from an exact per-axis requant on the pruned channels, on the part.
The `pruned-live` cell, which differs only in the fold those channels carry, refuses. The
three layers' non-clamped channels are all within 0.077-0.097% of their own gain, so the
ramp's resolution is not in question either.

Before the CBUF rung fix, accepting the three layers loses 0.043 mAP. The loss is the CBUF
rung, not the per-axis arithmetic and not the graph. Accepting all three scored 0.2380 against
the refusal's 0.2809, and accepting only the two depthwise ones scored 0.2076.

One of that pair is `blocks_0`: 160×160, ic = oc = 32, k3 s1. Its three row windows all
selected F=512, a rung the depthwise path does not honor. The layer came back wrong on 28 of
its 32 live channels, in exactly the output rows past each window's 51st staged input row. A
forced small window closes the whole gap and more, which localizes the cause to the rung:

| arm | mAP | vs CPU's 0.2823 |
|---|---:|---:|
| the refusal | 0.2809 | -0.0014 |
| `PC_INERT=dw`, before the rung fix | 0.2076 | -0.0747 |
| `PC_INERT=dw` + `MAX_ROWS=48`, before the fix | 0.2832 | +0.0009 |
| `PC_INERT=1` + `MAX_ROWS=48`, before the fix | 0.2826 | +0.0003 |
| `MAX_ROWS=48` alone | 0.2809 | -0.0014 |
| **`PC_INERT=dw`, after the rung fix, no forced cap** | **0.2832** | **+0.0009** |

The per-axis acceptance is therefore a small win, and the clamped-channel arithmetic owes none
of the loss. Two arms separate the causes. With `MAX_ROWS=48` alone the score reproduces the
refusal's 0.2809, so the cap moves nothing by itself. With the rung fixed, the acceptance
reaches the same 0.2832 with no cap forced at all. See "On the DEPTHWISE path the two low CBUF
rungs do not deliver". [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 500 val2017 images per arm, 2026-08-04]

The acceptance ships on, and the wall is what decides it. The knob `ROCKET_RK3576_PC_INERT`
defaults to full acceptance. Setting it to `0` refuses every clamped channel, and `dw`
restricts the acceptance to the depthwise path.

The mAP is the smaller half of the case. The larger half is that **a refused layer splits the
partition it sits in**, so its cost is a graph property and not a node's. Refusing leaves
EfficientDet-Lite0's head as a 12-layer and a 28-layer partition, carrying 5394 KiB of cube
joins between them. Accepting makes it one 43-layer partition carrying 7594. The claimed-node
counts barely move (231, 233, 234 of 267) and the wall moves 17%:

| arm | claimed | head partition | wall | vs CPU TFLite |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|
| `PC_INERT=0` | 231 | 12 + 28 layers, 5394 KiB joined | 99.3 ms | 1.23x |
| `PC_INERT=dw` | 233 | 42 layers, 6794 KiB | 90.3 ms | 1.35x |
| **default (full)** | **234** | **43 layers, 7594 KiB** | **82.8 ms** | **1.47x** |

The protocol is five interleaved rounds of 20 iterations, with the governor pinned to
`performance` and the first process discarded. Each arm's four rounds sit inside 0.6 ms, and
the CPU arm is constant at 121.9. On `ondemand` the same ordering reads 120.2 ms for
`PC_INERT=0`, 110.9 for `dw` and ~99 for the default, with more spread. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
2026-08-04]

The wall separates the two acceptance modes, and the mAP cannot. Their 7.5 ms difference is
twelve times the run-to-run spread. Their 0.0006 mAP difference is inside what one count of
drift does to an NMS-ordered detection list. Under the shipped default the model scores 0.2826
against the CPU's 0.2823. SSD carries no clamped channel and is the control: it is unmoved at
0.2617 against 0.2631, and at 17.0 ms. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

### Channel-range splits

A channel-range split does not clear the rest. A depthwise conv is one task because output
channel `c` is bound to input channel `c`. The obvious recourse gives a channel range its own
task and its own `OUT_CVT` shift. A host readout tried it on all six layers, over every
reachable tile (a multiple of the surface's 16-channel atom). It asks the shipped planner, with
the range sliced as a layer of its own.

The split clears 3 of the 6: op#15 at a uniform tile of 96, and ops #44 and #52 at 32 and 16.
A non-uniform partition clears them at 3, 6 and 3 programs (greedy-longest from the left,
which a planner can emit). The other three each carry an irreducible 16-channel atom: one
whose base-setting channel and smallest-scale channel sit inside the finest slice the surface
admits. No 16-aligned partition clears them at all. One of them (op#48) gets worse
as the tile shrinks, with 1 clamped range at 1152 against 4 at 32. [host-computed, readout,
2026-08-04]

The mechanism is that a depthwise layer cannot sort its channels. A consecutive range narrows
the scale spread only where the model's channel order happens to be locally scale-ordered.
That is a property of the model rather than of the tiling. [host-computed, readout, 2026-08-04]

### Channel order of a per-axis surface

**A sorted per-axis handle's surface is not in the model's channel order.** The direct
per-axis path sorts its output channels by scale, so that each tile spans as little of the
layer's range as the tiling allows. A tile's channels occupy consecutive slots of the surface,
so the surface comes out sorted. The de-scatter puts a materialized tensor back in model order
and hides the sort completely. That is why every per-op gate is exact. It is also why the
graph gate's per-axis pass is green, since that pass runs each layer as a materializing per-op
call.

But the cube path hands a surface to the next layer as its feature cube, and there the channel
order is the layout. The consumer reads sorted channels as model order and computes a full,
correctly sized, entirely plausible surface from the wrong data.

With one output-channel tile the sort buys nothing, because that tile spans every channel
whatever their order. The base gain and each channel's `C` are the same values in different
slots. So the single-tile path drops the sort, and its surface comes out in model order.
Multi-tile handles keep the sort, where it is the whole accuracy lever. Their cube is refused
anyway, because each tile owns its own surface. The invariant also sits in
`r76_cube_shape_ok()`, at the place that depends on it.

With both per-axis defects handled, per-axis ResNet-18 scores 89 of 100 against the
per-tensor control's 93 of 100, and EfficientDet is at CPU parity. The remaining gap is the
per-axis requant's own accuracy on near-ties, which is the expected regime. [HW sweep, H96 MAX
M9, 2026-08-04]

### Per-axis gain on the BS shift word

The per-axis gain rides the BS shift word, and a channel that cannot vary is its byte. Each
task's largest live scale takes `C` = 32767. The BS shift is the smallest at which
`((acc + A)*C) >> s` fits int32 at every live channel's accumulator bound. The word carries
the shift in both sign fields, since the stage splits it by sign here as on the RK3588
(`rk3576_coeff_c shiftsign`: 32 of 32 channels under the sign-split model, 16-17 under either
field alone). A channel whose exact output is one byte over its whole reachable accumulator is
programmed as that byte through its own `A` and `C`.

On EfficientDet-Lite0 those byte channels number 345 over 28 layers: all-zero filters, and dead
channels driven past the rail. They are every channel the shift-0 ramp clamps. Only a live
channel below 1/65534 of its task's largest live scale is refused. The per-axis gate is 34 of 34
bit-exact against the extended host model [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, 2026-09-27, `tests/rk3576_perchannel_gate`].

The shift-0 ramp's larger cost is the output-channel split, not the refusals. It refuses 4 of
EfficientDet-Lite0's 182 per-axis layers and cuts 11 direct layers into 133 tiles. A multi-tile
layer cannot produce a cube, so 6 joins are refused too. Priced by knob over 8 rotated passes,
the refusals cost 6.3 ms and the tiles 20.1 ms of an 83.1 ms wall.

With the shift carrying the gain, nothing is refused or split. The model runs 55.5 ms against
83.6 (0.661x paired), 2.22x the CPU's 123.1 ms [HW sweep, both clusters pinned `performance`, 0
retirements]. Its mAP@[.5:.95] is 0.2818 against the CPU's 0.2823 over 500 val2017 images.
Setting `ROCKET_RK3576_PC_SHIFT=0` restores the shift-0 ramp. Its detections are then
byte-identical to those of a build without the shift word.

### Distance from TFLite

Against TFLite the graph differs on 0.095% of elements, every one of them by a single count.
The pooling layer and the classifier match exactly. That residue is the requant, not a defect.
This DPU's `OUT_CVT` carries a 15-bit multiplier and rounds ties to even, where TFLite carries
31 bits and rounds half away from zero. Two fixed-point approximations of one real-valued
scale therefore disagree on the elements near a boundary.

Fed its own previous output rather than TFLite's, a layer's disagreements compound down the
graph. By the classifier only 76% of the logits are bit-identical, and the top-1 and the whole
top-5 still agree. That is the useful statement about a quantized classifier: the ranking
survives what the last bit does not.

The fused RELU6 is free. Every intermediate tensor is quantized at scale 6/255 with its zero
point at the bottom of the range. The activation's clamp is therefore exactly the int8
saturation the DPU already applies. The gate asserts that rather than assuming it.

### Blind spots of a per-op gate

Running the graph exposes three defects that a per-op gate structurally cannot see:

- **The two dead CBUF rungs** above. Every shape in the correctness envelope sits at F=0, and
  the first plane large enough to need a rung is this network's second depthwise block. That
  measurement reads the axis off as the kernel. On the direct path the axis is the resident
  weight footprint instead, and a square-kernel sweep cannot separate the two. On the
  depthwise path no footprint threshold fits at all, and the rungs are declined. See the
  symmetry audit below, and "On the DEPTHWISE path the two low CBUF rungs do not deliver".
- **TFLite's SAME padding is asymmetric, and it is an output extent rather than a materialized
  border.** A stride-2 SAME convolution over an even plane pads one row and one column at the
  end only. The CNA takes no configured trailing pad. It derives the pad its last window
  consumes from the output extent and the leading pad. That layer is therefore a leading pad
  of zero against an extent one larger than the symmetric formula gives, and nothing has to be
  copied. Materializing the border instead puts a buffer in the cube chain and breaks it.
- **The int8 first conv's bounds are jointly the ONNX symmetric-SAME convention.** A TFLite
  SAME stem has `pad_left = 0`, so it does not satisfy them directly. The network runs its stem
  on the direct path. It runs it at the model's own three channels, because the int8 direct
  cube is a 32-channel MAC group at every count (see below). Widening to eight channels is
  exact too: the added channels hold the input zero point against zero weights. That
  contributes nothing to either half of the coefficient `A` fold and is exact for any zero
  point.

The SAME-padding detail is written up below. The stem has a second route: shifting the input by
one pixel against a pad of one moves the sample grid by a whole stride. That reproduces
TFLite's grid bit-exactly in the interior. That lowering is wired and gated, but it is a loss
against a resident widened stem, and it is written up above.

### The graph's wall

Every layer of the graph runs on paths with no wide-output hazard. The graph is int8
throughout, so it pays no poison guard and no power cycle. The atom drop that the int32 writers
show did not appear. That makes this wall a wall, not a floor with retries in it. [HW sweep,
H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`]

At this operating point the graph's wall is host arithmetic over the weights, not dispatch,
and the levers below remove that term. The whole inference is 40 submits carrying 40 tasks.
The three layers that cost most, the stem and the two 1024-channel 1×1 convolutions, take 5, 1
and 1 of them. Output-channel tiling is the obvious suspect when a layer is slow on a
submit-floored part. It is not what those layers spend: they are already one submit each.

The profiler knob `ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1` splits a call into seven buckets:

- Sums
- Feature pack
- Weight pack
- Coefficients
- Plan+gen
- Submit
- De-scatter

Across the graph the submit is 1-16% of a layer and 3% of the two most expensive, against a
host share of 84-99%. Measure the submit count before believing a submit-floor story. Then
measure the submit time before believing the count. The ~340 us floor is a floor on a round
trip that waits for a full surface to drain. These jobs are well under it, at 0.13-0.25 ms
each.

**That ratio is a measurement of this graph, not a property of the part**, and the levers in
the sections below change it. Residency removes the weight arithmetic, the cube layout removes
the transposes, and the kick removes most of the submits. On the graph with those levers in
place, the host share is 6-12%, and the two live terms are the submit and the write guard. See
"What the wall is made of, once the graph work is collected".

The host work is two loops over the weight tensor, and neither depends on the input. The
per-output-channel filter sums fold the input zero point, and the cube scatter is a pure
re-layout. Straightening them in place takes the graph from 146 ms to 115 ms.

### Resident weight handles

Hoisted out of the inference entirely, the two loops and the BO churn they carry are 65% of
the wall. The wall goes 98.8 ms -> 33.6 ms, a 2.94x speedup on the same 40 submits. That is
`rocket_conv2d_int8_pack_rk3576()`, a handle a caller packs once and passes per inference, and
its answer is bit-identical to the per-call entry's. The graph's TFLite distance is the same
4792 of 5044713 elements. Each of the 29 compute layers stays bit-exact against a CPU model of
the part's own arithmetic, and the whole top-5 is unchanged. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`, `ROCKET_RK3576_NET_RESIDENT=1`]

It is one code path, not a second implementation. A transient call builds the same handle and
packs each output-channel tile as its tile loop reaches it. There is therefore no second
arithmetic to keep in agreement. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`,
`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_RESIDENT=1`]

Only 29 ms of the 65 is the weight arithmetic the profile predicts. The profiler stops at the
last bucket, and the BO teardown runs after it. An account that is not closed against the wall
therefore attributes 16 ms per inference to nothing at all. The table is per inference, over
20 iterations:

| bucket | per-call | resident | removed |
|---|---|---|---|
| filter sums | 4.0 | 0.0 | 4.0 |
| feature pack | 27.8 | 11.6 | **16.2** |
| weight cube | 24.0 | 0.0 | 24.0 |
| coefficients | 0.6 | 0.0 | 0.6 |
| plan + gen | 6.7 | 2.2 | 4.5 |
| submit | 7.6 | 7.6 | 0.0 |
| de-scatter | 6.6 | 6.8 | 0.0 |
| **BO teardown** | **16.1** | 0.0 | **16.1** |
| library total | 93.3 | 28.3 | 65.0 |

Three entries in that table are buffer costs. **Unmapping the BOs a layer touched is 16 ms of
a 99 ms graph.** It is a megabyte-sized dirty mapping per tile, once per layer per inference,
and it is invisible to any profile that ends at the de-scatter.

The feature bucket more than halves without its scatter loop changing at all. Of its 28 ms,
16 are the allocation, the zeroing memset and the two cache-maintenance ioctls. Most of the
stem's feature pack is therefore the buffer, not the scatter. The `plan+gen` bucket is mostly
an allocation too: the regcmd BO. The scatter that remains, 11.6 ms, is the transpose itself,
vectorized below.

With resident handles the graph is 33.6 ms, and the submit is 22% of the library time rather
than 8%. The resident path is therefore much closer to dispatch-bound. What is left on the
host is the feature scatter (11.6), the de-scatter (6.8) and the 2.2 ms the table calls
`plan + gen`.

That 2.2 ms is not the register program. A resident handle's DMA addresses do not move between
calls. The whole per-row-task stream is therefore a pure function of frozen state, and it can
be generated once at pack time. Pack-time generation is built and gated bit-identical over the
173-shape resident A/B and the whole network. It is worth 0.13 ms of 33.6. [HW sweep, H96 MAX
M9, 2026-07-29]

What the bucket holds is the output surface's sentinel stamp. The same bucket is 2.06 ms per
inference with the stamp on and 0.02 ms with it off. Neither the row plan nor the emitter is
therefore measurable at all. Cutting the stamp's `memset` from the whole surface to 64 bytes
moves it 1.53 -> 1.45. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29]

The stamp itself is load-bearing: it makes "this task never wrote" a property of the surface.
A conv inherits the wide-output poisoning from whatever ran before it, so the lever is where
the stamp is written, not whether. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29]

The next call's stamp rides the de-scatter's own bracket. The surface is already inside a
`PREP_BO`/`FINI_BO` pair there, so a resident tile pays one round trip per call instead of two.
Interleaved bench-100 runs of two binaries over five pairs read 30.2 ms against 29.7. Four
sequential blocks of 100 each way put 29.9-30.6 against 29.8 and below, with no overlap. The
gain is about 0.5 ms of 30, and never negative.

Halving the stamp's brackets does not halve its cost. Attributing the stamp to two cache
ioctls predicts that removing one of the two brackets removes half the cost, and it does not.
With the sentinel off entirely the same bench reads 28.5 ms. The whole stamp therefore costs
1.7 ms with two brackets and 1.2 with the fold.

The fill's own cache maintenance follows it into whichever bracket it lands in, because
`dma_sync_sgtable_for_device` walks the buffer whatever is dirty in it. That is also why the
64-byte memset buys almost nothing. What the fold removes is one ioctl pair per tile per call,
and that is the 0.5 ms.

**The bucket profile is the wrong instrument for a lever this size.** Under
`ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1` the two buckets are not additive with the wall. The sentinel-off
run reads worse in `plan+gen` plus `de-scatter` than either stamped arrangement, while being
1.2 ms faster in the wall. The stamp also warms the output surface for the de-scatter that
follows it, and that saving lands in a different bucket than its cost. Use long interleaved
runs of the whole graph to separate the two. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29]

### The CHW <-> NC1HWC2 transpose

The transpose runs as a 16×16 block, and the point is to keep only eight vectors live. The cube
interleaves sixteen channels into every sixteen-byte atom, so a row-major tensor and a cube are
a transpose and not a copy. Written an element at a time, the transpose moves one useful byte
per destination cache line. The sixteen channels of a group each walk the same lines again,
four line touches per byte moved. A 16×16 block does the same work in whole vectors and
touches every line once. It makes sixteen 16-byte loads and four levels of `TRN1`/`TRN2`, and
stores four full cache lines.

**The block must keep eight vectors live, not sixteen.** A 16×16 written as one array of
sixteen `uint8x16_t` needs thirty-two registers with its scratch. The compiler then puts the
array on the stack, and the whole point is lost. On the part that form measures 3x slower than
the same transpose done as two 8×8 halves plus a swap of the off-diagonal quadrants. The
quadrant form never has more than eight vectors live. Against the scalar loop, at
`ic=128 px=3136`:

| operation | scalar loop | array of sixteen | quadrants |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| pack | 0.42 | 0.23 | **0.078** |
| unpack | 0.35 | 0.21 | **0.095** |

The full-group predicate is a constant argument to one `always_inline` helper. The compiler
therefore emits the unpredicated sixteen-channel loop and the partial one from one source, not
from two hand-written kernels. A per-lane runtime predicate costs 10-25%. Dead lanes are still
written on the way in. They carry the cube's channel padding, which must be zero, and a whole
atom is stored either way.

On the graph the vectorized transpose is 1.41x. The result is bit-identical, with the same
4792-element TFLite distance, the same top-5 and the same 173-shape resident A/B:

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| feature scatter | 10.91 | 4.94 |
| de-scatter (readback) | 7.90 | 5.30 |
| submit | 7.17 | 7.22 |
| library total | 26.26 | 17.70 |
| **inference** | **29.5-30.3** | **20.3-21.3** |

With the vectorized transpose, the submit is the largest single bucket, at 41% of the library
time.

What is left in those two buckets is the bytes, not the loop. The de-scatter's `PREP_BO`, the
DPU write drain, measures only 0.53 ms of the 5.3. The transpose is therefore the rest.

At ~5 MB each way the two buckets run at 2.1-2.4 GB/s. This part's single-thread `memcpy`
ceiling is 4.4 GB/s (`build/membench`, 256 MB). The buckets are therefore within about 2x of
the cost of moving the bytes at all, against 6-9x before the block transpose. Further
vectorization is worth little.

### Cube layout between consecutive layers

Between two NPU layers, the transposes above can be skipped entirely. A direct conv's output
surface stride is `ow*oh` exactly, and `rocket_rk3576_out_surf_elems` pads only the
depthwise case. A feature cube's channel-group stride is `ih*iw`. So whenever the plane and
the channel rounding agree, layer n's output surface is layer n+1's feature cube, byte for
byte.

Keeping the cube is an API question and not a hardware one. Both ends are properties of the
resident handle, which already owns the input and output BOs and already freezes the fd.
Three calls carry the join:

- `rocket_conv2d_int8_cube_of_rk3576()` describes a handle's output surface.
- `_cube_in_rk3576()` makes another handle read from that surface instead of scattering.
- `_cube_out_rk3576()` leaves the output where the DPU wrote it.

`in` and `out` can be NULL for an end the handle does not use. A cube-in call does no cache
maintenance either. Nothing on the CPU touches those lines, so the scatter's
`PREP_BO`/`FINI_BO` pair goes with the scatter.

The channel-group stride is not one of the bounds. A direct convolution writes `ow*oh`
elements per group and a depthwise one writes `round4(ow*oh)`, 52 against a 7×7 plane's 49.
The consumer's DDR group jump is not a constant, though. It is a register the emitter fills
(`R76_CNA_SURF_FULL`, `0x1094`, in 16-byte atoms).

The part honors any value of that register at or above the plane. The sweep is bit-exact at
+3, +16 and +64 elements over five geometries, at one row task and at ten. The task-window
stride (`0x1098`) stays derived throughout. The control lays the same padded buffer out
without telling the register, and it differs every time
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_surf_stride.c`].

The descriptor `rocket_rk3576_cube` therefore carries its producer's stride, and
`_cube_in_rk3576()` passes it to the program. A depthwise producer at a plane whose element
count is not a multiple of four joins like any other. The library refuses a stride shorter
than the plane. Such a stride makes the groups overlap, and no producer in the library makes
one.

**Three bounds are refusals rather than fix-ups.** Each names a shape where the two buffers
are not the same object:

- More than one output-channel tile.
- An input channel count that is not a multiple of 32, from a producer that does not say
  what its tail holds.
- A mismatched fd or plane.

Each output-channel tile owns its own surface BO, and several BOs are not one cube. Nothing
in a MobileNet reaches the tile bound. A 1×1 layer's weight slice is 32 KiB, against the
144 KiB that leaves the tile at the whole layer.

For the channel-count bound, a handle's own cube fills the channels past `ic` with its
border constant. A producer's surface holds whatever the DPU wrote into its own padded
output channels. Their weights are zero, so nothing reaches the MAC either way. What reads
them is the coefficient group's B term, which sums every tap of the whole programmed group.

Two cases are past the B term. A symmetric weight quantization has no B term at all. A
direct producer's partial output group carries its output zero point, and at a graph join
that is the consumer's own border constant. The descriptor `rocket_rk3576_cube` carries
`pad_from`/`pad_value` so that the tail is stated rather than assumed, and the check is
against the consumer's `in_zp`. A depthwise producer's surface carries the deeper tail the
host fills at pack time. See "What a producer leaves past its live output channels" below.

A cube can also be a slice of a larger buffer, which is what a residual topology needs. See
"A cube's base is a plain address" below.

#### What a producer leaves past its live output channels

A consumer whose `ic` is not a multiple of 32 walks the round-32 count. The join therefore
turns on the channels between the producer's `oc` and that boundary, and their content is
known.

A direct producer's partial output group carries its output zero point. The channels past
`oc` are packed with zero weights and a zero A term, so their accumulator is zero. The
OUT_CVT then sends them to the output zero point. At a graph join that is the consumer's
input zero point, because the two are one tensor's quantization. It is therefore exactly the
border constant the consumer's own cube would have filled. This holds for 12544 of 12544
padding elements at `oc` 16, 24 and 144 and at `k` 1 and 3 (`tests/rk3576_pad_channels.c`).

**This holds only once the tail is given no B term, and that is the trap.** The term `B` is
per output channel. The obvious packer loop gives every channel of the programmed count the
same one, and a padding channel then carries `requant(B*sum(x))`, which is data-dependent.
At `oc=16 w_zp=12` the probe measured 5776 of 12544 padding elements away from the output
zero point. Those channels are not a live output, so the correction they lose is one nobody
applies. The live channels are unchanged.

**A gate whose producers are all symmetric cannot see this axis.** Such a gate accepted
MobileNetV2's layer 2 -> 3 join, which took that layer to 0.5% agreement with the row-major
path. Every isolated per-op case stayed byte-identical.

A depthwise producer is programmed with the raw output count and writes nothing past it:
12544 of 12544 padding elements still hold the sentinel. Its surface is therefore a group
short of what a consumer reads. At `oc=144` it holds 9 groups, against the 10 that a direct
consumer of 144 channels walks.

That tail is a host fact, not a hardware one. A depthwise producer's tile allocates the
deeper surface and fills the group past its own with the output zero point once, at pack
time. Nothing overwrites that fill. The program is told the raw count and does not address
those bytes, and the sentinel covers the written groups alone. Read back after an
inference, the fill still stands: 12544 of 12544 at the output zero point, at `oc` 16, 48
and 144. A depthwise producer therefore declares the same tail a direct one does, and the
join is available.

The join is available only when the raw count fills its last written group. Below that, at
`oc=24` (two groups holding 32 channels), the tail lies inside a group the program does
write. Whether the DPU leaves those channels alone has not been measured. The surface stays
short there, and the join is refused.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_pad_channels.c`, 2026-07-31]

#### Linking pass

**The linking must run as a pre-pass over already-packed handles**, not be decided as the
graph runs. The producer's "leave it in the cube" and the consumer's "read it from there"
are one decision. A producer that skipped its de-scatter for a consumer that then refused
the cube leaves no row-major tensor at all.

On MobileNetV1-224 the pass makes 24 joins of 30 layers. It runs with the asymmetric-pad
lowering below, which closes four of the joins. Together they take the graph
20.9-21.4 -> 11.9-12.3 ms, 1.73x, on the same 40 submits and 40 tasks. The two compose superadditively,
measured interleaved at `bench 100`:

| asymmetric pad | cube chain | ms per inference |
|---|---|---|
| materialized | off | 20.9-21.4 |
| extent | off | 20.1-20.5 |
| materialized | on | 15.7-16.4 |
| **extent** | **on** | **11.9-12.3** |

| bucket | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| submit | 8.4 | 7.5 |
| feature scatter | 6.5 | 3.0 |
| de-scatter | 5.5 | 0.8 |
| plan+gen (carries the stamp) | 0.8 | 2.6 |

Gate the linking against the arithmetic. A layout change owes byte-identity, and the gate
runs in five steps:

1. Run the graph row-major.
2. Snapshot every layer.
3. Link the cubes.
4. Run the graph again.
5. Compare every layer the second run still materializes, plus the final logits.

A wrong byte inside a join reaches the next materialization point, so nothing hides in the
middle of a chain. On the measured run, every materialized layer is identical and the logits
are byte-identical. The top-5 is unchanged, and the 4792-element TFLite distance does not
move.

On the linked graph the submit is 62% of the library time: 40 submits of about 190 us each.
That is well under the ~440 us floor, so the lever there is the submit count across layers
and not the floor. A submit removed is worth about 110 us on this graph, measured rather
than inferred. Chaining a tile's row tasks into one hardware kick takes the graph from 40
submits to 29 and from 11.8-12.2 ms to 10.5-11.1. The 40 tasks and the assertions are the
same in both arms. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29]

The saving is 9-11% here against about 3% over the per-op conv gate, because a graph's
layers are cheap enough for the submit to dominate. That is why the lever is default-on, and
`ROCKET_RK3576_BATCH_TASKS=0` turns it off. Chaining across layers is the same lever taken
further and is worth much more (see below). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29]

The sentinel stamp is 0.9-1.9 ms of the 12 whichever path it rides. On the row-major path
it is the fill inside the de-scatter's bracket. On the cube path it is its own
`PREP_BO`/`FINI_BO` pair at the start of the next call. The stamp goes there because the
surface is read as an input before that handle runs again. The two cost the same, so the
cube chain does not make the guard more expensive. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29]

### A retired job is redone whatever it wrote

`rocket` retires a job 125 ms after its kick and signals the fence as if the job had
completed [source-confirmed, patch `rk3576/npu/0025`]. A retired job can leave a partial
surface, which a check for one written byte per task passes. So every guard site also times
its job, from before the submit ioctl to the fence. The kick follows the submit's start, so a
retired job reads at least 125 ms. A healthy job reads under it, because the backstop bounds
it too.

The library redoes a job at or past the backstop after a power cycle, like a job that wrote
nothing. The entry refuses once its attempts run out. The int8 matmul plans its tiles so
that no job stalls. If one does stall, the int8 matmul re-plans a partly written one
narrower. Where the write check is one byte per task, the task extents go back to the
sentinel before the redo. A redo that writes nothing then cannot pass on the retired
attempt's bytes.

The counter `rocket_rk3576_retired_counts()` counts the scored retirements, split by whether
the write check alone passed the surface. The hardware profile's `backstop_ms` holds the
value, and `ROCKET_RK3576_BACKSTOP_US` overrides it.

The guard sites are:

- The conv submit helper, which carries the direct, depthwise, packed-image and fp16
  convolutions.
- The chained kick.
- The int8, int32 and fp16 matmul writers.
- The pool.

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Backstop forced to 1 us | Eight small entry calls and the chained kick refuse, each count equal to its attempts |
| Gate list, 67 rows | 67 pass in 320 s. Scored retirements equal the kernel's `retiring it` count on every row that submits through an entry, 163 in all |
| Gate list, 69 rows, kernel 7.2.8-1, 594 MHz, the rail switched with the power domain (2026-10-02) | 69 pass in 286 s. 165 scored retirements, equal to the per-row counts |
| The three poison rows | 52, 55 and 54 scored, every one unwritten |
| Five stall `K`, §"An int8 matmul job can raise no completion" | Scored equal to the kernel's count at each. The entry computes where a write check alone returned a wrong surface |

The one kernel retirement the list does not score is `uapi_regcmd_fault`'s, a raw uAPI submit
outside every entry.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, `tests/rk3576_retire_probe`, `tools/gates-rk3576.sh`,
2026-09-27]

Timing cannot see a job the kernel retires early, a DPU grace shorter than the drain, which
reads fast. It also cannot see a partial write by a job that completed. A job queued behind
another process's job reads long as well, and costs one redo.

### Sentinel guard brackets

The sentinel guard costs a bracket per surface per call, and the bracket count is the
lever. Once a run of layers is one hardware kick, the guard is the largest single bucket in
the whole graph. Setting `ROCKET_RK3576_I32_SENTINEL=0` takes MobileNetV2 from 10.0 to
5.9 ms and MobileNetV1 from 6.4 to 3.5. Most of the guard is not the fill. On the graphs as
they run today the memsets are 15-18% of the guard, and the cache maintenance is the rest.
The two sections below measure both terms directly rather than inferring them from an A/B.

The `PREP_BO` ioctl syncs a whole BO for the CPU, and `FINI_BO` syncs the whole BO back for
the device. Neither takes a range, so a bracket costs two passes over the surface however
few bytes are touched inside it. Unfolded, the chained path opens two brackets on every
layer's surface per call, for a single CPU visit. One asks whether the layer's tasks wrote,
and one puts the next call's sentinel back.

Folding the re-stamp into the verify bracket is 10.0 -> 8.8 ms on V2 and 6.4 -> 5.4 on V1.
Both arms are byte-identical and run the same submits and tasks. The order is safe because
the redo path re-stamps every surface anyway, so a layer stamped before a later one is found
bad loses nothing.

Two surfaces keep separate handling, because something the kick does not contain reads them
after the kick. The last layer keeps its own handling under the de-scatter, which must read
the surface before anything overwrites it. A surface shared with something outside the
chain still takes its stamp at the start of the next call.

The remaining cost is one bracket per BO per call, and it is bytes-proportional rather than
per-ioctl.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`, 2026-07-31]

### One bracket per BO

One bracket per BO, rather than per layer, is the free lever: unlike the two levers below,
it trades no coverage. A bracket syncs the whole object. Several layers of one run write
slices of one buffer whenever a concatenation is placed, because every operand of a placed
concatenation does. A per-layer pass therefore re-syncs that buffer once per operand,
checking a different slice of it each time. Naming each BO once checks exactly the same
bytes.

Key on the kernel handle. A placed slice carries its own descriptor of the same underlying
object. Keyed on the `rocket_bo` pointer, the redundancy therefore reads as **zero on every
graph**, including one with fourteen placed concatenations. The probe arm that must show a
positive then shows none.

Both arms are byte-identical per layer and return TFLite's own top-1 on the same submits and
tasks [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_net_gate bench 100`, ondemand, 2026-08-03]:

| | brackets | bytes | guard | wall | -> brackets | bytes | guard | wall |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 30.3 | 4987 KiB | 2.03 | 5.0 | 30.3 | 4987 | 2.07 | 5.0 |
| MobileNetV2 | 85.4 | 8665 | 3.36 | 6.9 | **65.4** | **7721** | **3.19** | **6.6** |
| ResNet-18 | 51.1 | 8243 | 2.87 | 7.4 | **35.1** | **5303** | **2.10** | **6.5-6.6** |
| Inception V1 | 122.2 | 14465 | 4.75 | 8.8 | **68.2** | **7207** | **2.83** | **7.0-7.1** |
| Inception V3 | 122.4 | 19713 | 6.35 | 42.3 | **102.4** | **17272** | **5.80** | **40.7-41.9** |

MobileNetV1 is the zero and the control. It has no placed concatenation, shares no BO, and
does not move. The guard-bucket saving equals the wall saving to 0.05 ms on every graph,
which says the lever is the bracket bytes and nothing else.

Inception V3's row is that lever's own A/B, at the numbers it was measured on. With its
fifteenth concatenation placed, that graph's wall is 38.4-38.8 ms. The other four rows are
current.

Ranged brackets stay per layer. Two slices' ranges are disjoint, so there is nothing
redundant to remove, and a pooled bracket would have to carry their union.

The lever adds a linear scan of the open set per layer, bounded by a run's distinct BO
count. It also adds one pointer array per chain.

### Bracket cost

A bracket is a 2.24 us floor plus 0.194 us/KiB, so the bytes are the whole of it. The
ladder below runs 4 KiB to 4 MiB at 400 iterations a rung and takes the median
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_prep_floor.c`, 2026-08-02]:

| | floor | slope | rate |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `PREP_BO` | 1.33 us | 0.0969 us/KiB | 10.57 GB/s |
| `FINI_BO` | 1.11 us | 0.0970 us/KiB | 10.56 GB/s |
| one bracket | 2.24 us | 0.1939 us/KiB | 5.28 GB/s |

An ioctl the kernel refuses at handle lookup, with no fence wait and no cache walk, costs
1.17 us. That is the syscall, the drm dispatch and the GEM lookup. Past a few KiB, the
ioctl is therefore entirely the walk. The memset inside a bracket adds a further
0.113 us/KiB. It is charged once per surface per call rather than once per bracket. Each
surface is stamped either in the verify bracket or in the next call's stamp loop, and never
both.

The chain counts its own guard (`ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1` prints brackets and bytes),
which turns the guard's milliseconds into arithmetic. Brackets times the floor is
0.07-0.27 ms, 4-7% of each graph's guard. The floor is not what needs removing.

### Fill and bracket terms of the guard

The guard is a fill and a bracket, and the fill is a fifth of it. The two halves have
separate levers, so the chain counts them separately (`ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1` prints
`write guard = fill ... + brackets and scan ...`). The fill is the memset that puts the
sentinel back. It is paid per layer, because every slice of a shared buffer still has to be
filled. The remainder is the two cache-maintenance ioctls, the scan and the loop.

Naming each BO once reaches the second half only, so the fill is worth measuring directly
rather than inferring as a residual. Inferred from the bracket cost model, the fill comes
out 2.1x too large.

The table gives the default guard per inference [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`rk3576_net_gate bench 100`, `INT8_PROF=1`, ondemand, 2026-08-03]:

| | guard | fill | fill bytes | rate | brackets + scan |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 2.07 | 0.56 (27%) | 4933 KiB | 0.113 us/KiB | 1.52 |
| MobileNetV2 | 3.19 | 0.81 (25%) | 7116 | 0.114 | 2.38 |
| ResNet-18 | 2.12 | 0.40 (19%) | 3470 | 0.114 | 1.73 |
| Inception V1 | 2.79 | 0.53 (19%) | 4581 | 0.116 | 2.26 |
| Inception V3 | 5.80 | 1.00 (17%) | 8629 | 0.117 | 4.82 |

The rate is flat at 0.113-0.117 us/KiB on all five graphs. That independently reproduces
the 0.113 that `tests/rk3576_prep_floor.c` measures on a synthetic ladder. The fill is
therefore a memset running at the part's own speed, and there is nothing in the loop to
collect.

No coverage-free lever exists on the fill, and that is a property of the check rather than
of the loop. The check reads the whole extent on a task that wrote nothing, so the stamp has
to cover whatever the check can read. Narrowing the fill is narrowing the check. Naming each
BO once is the free lever, and the default already spends it.

### Narrowing's fill and bracket shares

The table sets `GUARD_NARROW=1` against the deduplicated default, on the same run
structure. All five graphs are green and byte-identical with TFLite's own top-1
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_net_gate bench 100`, `INT8_PROF=1`, ondemand, 2026-08-03]:

| | guard | narrowed | lever | of which fill | of which brackets + scan |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 2.07 | 0.67 | 1.40 | 0.53 (38%) | 0.88 |
| MobileNetV2 | 3.19 | 1.20 | 1.99 | 0.75 (38%) | 1.23 |
| ResNet-18 | 2.12 | 0.71 | 1.41 | 0.38 (27%) | 1.04 |
| Inception V1 | 2.79 | 1.50 | 1.29 | 0.49 (38%) | 0.80 |
| Inception V3 | 5.80 | 1.60 | 4.20 | 0.97 (23%) | 3.27 |

The fill is 23-38% of what narrowing buys. The rest is the bracket bytes, which need the
ranged ioctl. The two terms read as conjunctive from their rates, and this table is the
measured split of that conjunction.

The lever is 1.3-4.2 ms across the five graphs, not 1.2-1.3 ms. The smaller figure is
Inception V1's alone, and the table it headed showed 1.2-3.4.

The bracket count rises as the footprint narrows, because ranged brackets are not pooled:
two slices' ranges are disjoint. The bracket counts, default against narrowed:

| | default | narrowed |
|---|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 30 | 30 |
| MobileNetV2 | 65 | 85 |
| ResNet-18 | 35 | 51 |
| Inception V1 | 68 | 122 |
| Inception V3 | 102 | 122 |

Inception V3's narrowed guard is 1.60 ms where brackets times the floor predicts 0.30. About
1.3 ms of it is therefore per-range kernel work. That is the same residual this page records
elsewhere, at a few hundred ns per 64-byte range.

### Narrowed footprint and ranged ioctl

The guard's bytes come off with a narrowed footprint and a ranged ioctl together. The check
asks whether a task wrote anything, so it reads only until the first byte that differs from
the sentinel. The stamp still has to cover whatever the check can read, and on a task that
wrote nothing that is the whole extent. Narrowed to one cache line per (task, channel
group), both ends touch tens of bytes instead of the surface
(`ROCKET_RK3576_GUARD_NARROW=1`).

That alone collects only the fill, because `PREP_BO` and `FINI_BO` sync the whole BO however
few bytes sit inside them. The ranged ioctls `DRM_ROCKET_PREP_BO_RANGES` and
`_FINI_BO_RANGES` (`patches/rk3576/npu/0023`, interface 1.5) take an array of
`{offset, size}` and sync only those ranges. The two are conjunctive by construction. A
ranged ioctl names a small footprint only where the guard has one. The un-narrowed extents'
union is the whole surface.

Together, the two run on the same submits and tasks and stay byte-identical per layer. Each
graph still returns TFLite's own top-1 [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_net_gate bench 100`,
2026-08-02].

The table prices the three forms against each other and against the guard-off floor. All
run at the current default (which already names each BO once) [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`rk3576_net_gate bench 100`, ondemand, 2026-08-03]:

| | default | guard-off floor | per-kick | narrowed |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 4.9 | 3.0 | **3.0** | 3.7 |
| MobileNetV2 | 6.5 | 3.5 | **3.5** | 4.7 |
| ResNet-18 | 6.6 | 4.5 | **4.5** | 5.2 |
| Inception V1 | 7.0 | 4.2 | **4.1** | 5.7 |
| Inception V3 | 38.5-38.8 | 31.9-32.9 | 36.1-36.5 | 34.9-35.5 |

Inception V3's row is re-measured at two runs an arm, with its fifteenth concatenation
placed. The other four rows are unmoved by that placement and are single runs. **That
row's baseline is not the graph's current default wall**: with the divisor-lag check
scoring the tail, the default wall is 33.4-33.7 rather than 38.5-38.8. The guard deltas are
a different term and are not expected to move with it. The row is a stored `ondemand` table
and has not been re-taken.

The `GUARD_PER_KICK` form lands on the guard-off floor on every graph that is one kick. On
Inception V3 it collects only 2.3-2.4 of 5.9-6.6. That graph runs its convolution calls
outside any kick in the twenties, and **a per-kick guard reaches none of them**. Its
coverage is a property of the run structure.

**Pinned to `performance`, the two cheap forms are worth less than that `ondemand` table
reads.** Under that governor the arms' differing host work cannot move the clock. The table
below takes three interleaved repeats an arm on Inception V3, at a within-arm spread of 0.3
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]:

| arm | ms | vs default |
|---|---:|---:|
| default | 29.53 | n/a |
| `GUARD_PER_KICK` | 27.53 | −2.00 |
| `GUARD_NARROW` | 27.20 | −2.33 |
| `POOL_LAG_TAIL` | 26.00 | −3.53 |
| per-kick + tail | 24.07 | −5.46 |
| conv-guard-off floor | 24.50 | −5.03 |

The table supports two readings. First, the guard and the lag check are additive to 0.07 ms
(−2.00 + −3.53 against −5.46 measured), so they are separate terms and not two views of
one. Second, per-kick + tail sits below the conv-guard-off floor while still guarding every
kick. That floor arm removes only the conv write guard, and the pair also collects the lag
check.

**A floor is a property of the term it was measured on, not of the wall.** On `ondemand`
the pair is not separable from the tail alone (33.50 against 33.43, at a 4.7 ms spread).
That is what the "pin the governor for an A/B whose arms differ in host work" rule is about.

On Inception V1 the narrowed form's advantage over the default is 1.2-1.3 ms, against 3.1
over a default with a bracket per layer. Naming each BO once therefore collects, with no
coverage trade, more than half of what narrowing collects over the per-layer default.

Rows earlier in this section for Inception V1 were measured with its stem on the direct
lowering and with a bracket per layer. That graph's stem runs the packed-image encoding at
the current default.

The residual guard is well above brackets-times-floor (0.59 against 0.07 on V1), because a
range costs per-range kernel work of its own. That work is `memdup_user`, the validation
pass, and a scatterlist walk plus an `iommu_iova_to_phys` per range. A bracket costs
12-20 us against a 2.24 us floor and carries tens of 64-byte ranges, so the work is a few
hundred nanoseconds a range. It is what the change adds, and it is a fifth to a third of
what a floor-only model predicts.

**The narrowing is a coverage trade, which is why it is off by default.** A line per group
per task still witnesses the hazard the guard exists for. The wide-output poisoning kills a
whole submit, so every line of every extent still holds the sentinel. What the narrowed
check stops seeing is a partial write that spares exactly those lines, the same class
`GUARD_PER_KICK` trades away.

The narrowing also raises the false-alarm rate. A task whose first 64 bytes per group
genuinely equal the sentinel byte reads as unwritten and costs a redo. The wide check needs
the whole extent to equal the sentinel before it reads a task as unwritten.

Spending fewer brackets is the other lever, and it is nearly all of the guard. Asking one
surface per kick rather than every layer's lands on the guard-off floor on all four one-kick
graphs (table above). It runs on the same submits and tasks, with the logits and every
materialized layer byte-identical. The flag is `ROCKET_RK3576_GUARD_PER_KICK=1`, and the
default asks every layer.

One surface can be enough because the hazard is per submit. A job whose DPU output element
is wider than one byte leaves the next submit of any kind writing nothing. A cross-layer
kick is one submit, so every surface in it witnesses a poisoning equally.

This is measured rather than argued. The test runs a bare int32-output program before each
inference and feeds two alternating inputs. Each inference is scored byte for byte against
its own clean answer. With either guard, 0 of 144 poisoned inferences came back wrong, at
51-57 kicks redone per 48 inferences. The runs were MobileNetV1 and ResNet-18 with every
layer checked, and MobileNetV1 with the last surface only. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 7.2.3-1,
`rocket` 1.6.0, `ROCKET_RK3576_NET_POISON` / `_VARY`, measured 2026-09-24 UTC]

With the guard off, 24 of 48 came back wrong. That is consistent with every kick stale,
since two alternating inputs see a stream that never writes again only on every other
inference. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 7.2.3-1, `rocket` 1.6.0, `ROCKET_RK3576_NET_POISON` /
`_VARY`, measured 2026-09-24 UTC]

**The injection must be a bare program.** The entry `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_i32()`
power-cycles the part on its way out, so the next caller never meets the hazard it made.
Through it, the same rows tripped the guard on 1 of 144 inferences. An injection that clears
its own hazard passes a guard that does nothing.

The measurement does not settle one case. It induces the whole-submit poisoning, so it says
nothing about a program dying in the middle of a stream while the last one still writes.
That is the failure the full guard is shaped for, and the one a single check cannot see by
construction.

**A bench that feeds the same input every iteration is blind to all of this.** The
blindness belongs to any such bench, not to this test. A layer whose program did not run
leaves its surface holding the previous inference's contents. On a repeated input those are
exactly the contents it would have written. The graph then returns the right answer for the
wrong reason, and every check downstream passes. The staleness question has an answer only
once the input varies.

### A run of cube-linked layers goes out as one hardware kick

With the transposes gone, the submit is the largest single bucket. The submit count is what
is left to spend: 29 submits for 29 layers, each ~190 us against 130-250 us of compute. A run
of consecutive cube-linked layers can be one job instead.

A chained stream honors read-after-write between its programs [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`tests/rk3576_chain_raw.c`]. Chaining row tasks never raises the question, because row tasks
write disjoint rows. Across layers the answer is not obvious: `PC_DONE` fires 82-343 us
before the DPU's writes become visible. The probe runs two 1×1 int8 convolutions where the
second's feature cube is the first's output surface, byte for byte. It compares the batched
job against two references: the same pair as two submits, and the second program alone on a
surface nothing wrote. Over five planes from 2 KB to 387 KB, 40 of 40 iterations gave the
two-submit answer.

The probe sweeps the plane because write visibility scales with the bytes in flight. A small
surface would look ordered whether or not the part orders it.

The cube link is the eligibility test, and the caller has already made it. Between two linked
layers there is no host work at all: no de-scatter, no scatter and no cache maintenance.
Nothing has to happen between the two programs. The entry
`rocket_conv2d_int8_chain_new_rk3576()` asserts the links against the BO addresses rather than
the flags. It plans and generates every program once, because the stream is a function of the
frozen geometry and is not regenerated per call. It lays the stream out contiguously in one
BO, with each trailer linked to the next.

MobileNetV1-224 goes 10.4-10.5 ms -> 6.7 ms and 29 submits -> 6, on the same 40 tasks. The
logits are byte-identical, and every materialized layer is identical. Two kicks carry 25 of
the 30 layers. The gate A/Bs the kicked graph against the per-layer cube-chained graph, not
against the row-major one. A comparison with row-major would test both levers at once, and a
wrong byte inside a kick would then read as a cube-chain defect.

Per layer, the 23-layer kick is then 65% of the wall, and nothing outside it reaches 0.31 ms.
The PPU pooling layer is the other 20% at this point, and 2% after the changes described in
"The pooling layer" below.

#### Cost inside a kick

The kick's composition is readable, and its cost is front-loaded on the stem. There is no
per-program wall inside one hardware kick, but the kick's own bucket is timed. The variable
`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_KICK_MAX=<m>` caps how many layers a run can cover. The bucket at cap `m`
is therefore the cost of the run's first `m` programs. The layers the cap excludes leave the
kick entirely and become their own buckets. The finite difference between consecutive caps is
what one program costs inside the kick:

| cap | 2 | 5 | 10 | 15 | 20 | 23 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| kick bucket, ms | 1.78 | 2.53 | 3.25 | 3.52 | 3.77 | 3.98 |
| graph, ms | 8.4 | 7.0 | 6.7 | 6.0 | 5.3 | 4.9 |
| submits | 27 | 24 | 19 | 14 | 9 | 6 |

The first two programs are 1.78 ms of the 3.98, 45% of the kick, and the other 21 cost
0.105 ms each. Those two are the 224×224 stem and the depthwise that follows it, by far the
largest planes in the network. The kick's cost is therefore the stem's compute plus a flat
per-program floor. A program costs ~105 us inside a chained stream against ~270 us as its own
submit (the graph falls 0.167 ms for each layer the cap moves into the kick). Both are well
under the ~440 us driver poll floor. That floor bounds a round trip that waits for a full
surface to drain, not a program.

For further optimization, 21 of 23 programs already sit at a flat floor that chaining cannot
lower. The remaining lever inside the kick is the stem, which is separately settled and off by
default. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-31, `bench 50`]

**A kick's bucket is not all program execution, and on MobileNetV2 most of it is not.** The
same instrument over V2's three large runs reads 84% of a 10.0 ms wall. Its finite difference
puts most of the first run in its first four layers, the biggest planes in the network. That
reads as compute, and it is a trap. Turning the sentinel off moves the same three buckets
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-31, `bench 30`/`bench 100`]:

| V2 run | 1 | 2 | 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| kick bucket, sentinel on, ms | 4.49 | 2.59 | 1.32 |
| kick bucket, sentinel off, ms | 2.57 | 1.02 | 0.91 |

So 41% of the wall attributed to three hardware kicks is the guard's cache maintenance on the
surfaces inside them. That cost is charged to the run's first layer along with everything
else. A kick's
bucket is the whole round trip of `PREP`, submit, verify, re-stamp and de-scatter. Decompose
it against a guard-off control before reading a finite difference as a program cost.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-31, `bench 30`/`bench 100`]

#### Chaining traps

Two traps, both of which write a full and entirely plausible wrong surface:

- **Both trailer fields describe the next segment, and a uniform-length chain cannot tell.**
  The driver programs `PC_BASE_ADDRESS` and `PC_REGISTER_AMOUNTS` from task 0 alone. Every
  later program's address and its length therefore come from its predecessor's trailer. A
  tile's row tasks are all the same size, so passing "this program's count" is
  indistinguishable there. Across layers a depthwise program and a direct one differ, and the
  mis-sized fetch leaves the affected program writing nothing at all.
- **Re-stamping the last layer's surface is safe only when the chain rewrites it before
  anyone reads it.** The next program in the same kick reads every interior layer, so the
  sentinel can ride the epilogue's bracket. That costs one `PREP_BO`/`FINI_BO` pair per
  surface per call. A last layer that leaves a cube is read by a layer outside the chain,
  later in the same inference. Stamping it there replaces the layer's output with the
  sentinel. The consumer then computes on 0xA5, and nothing before the next materialized
  layer can see it.

#### Stream length and the completion wait

The length a chained stream can reach is set by where the kernel starts its completion wait,
not by the part. The patch `patches/rk3576/npu/0012` retires a job when the writing block
raises its own completion in `INTERRUPT_RAW_STATUS`. The wait is bounded by `dpu_grace_us`
(500 us), so a job that raises none cannot stall behind one. That bound is measured on a job
of one task. The wait starts on two signals, and neither says what the patch takes it to say:

- **The `PC_DONE` bit is raised per task.** On a `0016` kick of n tasks, the two `PC_DONE`
  bits alternate as the program counter retires each program.
- **The `PC_OPERATION_ENABLE` register is a self-clearing go bit.** It reads back 0 at every
  poll of a running kick. The `OPERATION_ENABLE == 0` half of the condition is therefore
  vacuously true from the first poll onward.

The wait therefore starts at the first poll tick, and `dpu_grace_us` has to cover the whole
remaining stream rather than a drain. Past that deadline the driver signals the fence while
the core is still writing. The caller reads a surface that never landed, and the driver
programs the next job into a core that has not finished the last one.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, the go bit also source-confirmed: the vendor driver writes 1 then 0
to it back to back. See [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).]

The deadline each stream needs before its answer comes back bit-exact, measured by moving
`dpu_grace_us` under a cross-layer kick [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]:

| programs (layers) | deadline needed |
|---|---|
| 34 (23) | under 200 us |
| 30 (23) | 200 to 500 us |
| 35 (24) | 650 to 700 us |
| 220 (44) | under 150 us |

The deadline is **not monotone in the program count and not predictable from the stream**.
The 500 us default sits inside the range. The streams on the wrong side of it fail, and the
ones on the right side pass by luck. The table records a race, not a property.

What the deadline has to beat is the host reaching the surface. A stream of cheap programs
(the 220-program mix, at 32 channels over a 32×32 plane) can therefore retire early and still
read correctly, while a shorter stream of expensive ones cannot. The same 35-program stream
that fails 3 of 3 is bit-exact 3 of 3 at a longer deadline. It is equally bit-exact at a
coarser `poll_interval_us` (250 us and up), which delays the observation and the deadline
with it. The taint word does not move at either.

Scaling the deadline with the tasks one kick covers retires the bound in practice
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]:

| stream | channels | first failed at | chains, scaled deadline |
|---|---|---|---|
| uniform 3×3 | 128 | 4 | 40 deep |
| 5×5 | 64 | 6 | 24 deep |
| 3×3 | 96 | 9 | 24 deep |
| mixed direct/depthwise | | | 220 programs deep |

**The scaled deadline is still a fitted number, and a stream of slower programs outruns
it.** One 224×224 k3 convolution at 256 channels row-splits into 75 chained tasks. The scaled
deadline, 75 x 500 us, expires 37.5 ms in, with the task counter reading 67 tasks started
of 75. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

#### Completion on the task-status counter

The part exposes the signal the deadline stands in for. The register `PC_TASK_STATUS` sits at
`0x48` here, the vendor `rknpu` config's `pc_task_status_offset`. It is a live pair of
counters, both modulo the programmed `TASK_NUMBER`: tasks started in the low half and tasks
completed in the high half. Mid-stream at least one half is non-zero. A set `PC_DONE`
together with a zero register therefore means the whole kick has retired, and nothing else
does.

The patch `patches/rk3576/npu/0022` anchors the wait there. Under it, `dpu_grace_us` becomes
the drain it was measured as, applied to the program that wrote last. A fixed backstop covers
the case where a job raises no `PC_DONE` at all. The patch raises the interface version
to 1.4. Below 1.4 the library caps a stream at 34 programs, and
`rocket_batch_completion_tracked()` is how it tells.

With the counter gate, the 75-task convolution is bit-exact, 150 programs in one kick
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]. A deadline scaled by the task count retires the same layer at
t=37554 us, 75 x 500 us to the microsecond. The counter then reads 67 started of 75, and the
layer computes wrong. Under the counter gate, 29 gates pass with the taint word unmoved and
zero backstop hits. That also settles that every job class raises `PC_DONE`. The pool, whose
`PC_OPERATION_ENABLE` is `0x60` with no DPU stage, is included, and so is the LUT-load program
at `0x18`.

Neither the graph nor the per-submit floor moves across the change. On each side of it the
graph reads 6.4-6.5 ms and the per-submit floor a median of 348 us. Both were measured
2026-07-31, before the levers above the driver took the graph further. The wait ends on the
DPU's own completion either way, and the change moves only when it begins.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

**A chained stream of slow programs on a kernel without the gate hangs the board hard.** The
same 75-task convolution hung it twice: once against the scaled deadline and once against
plain 0021. Each hang needed a **physical power cycle**. The board does not come back on its
own and answers no ping, and a full LAN sweep finds no host accepting SSH.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

That is the outside cost of a job programmed into a core that has not finished the last one.
The "before" measurement is already recorded above, so it needs no repeat as a control.

The DMA write error and the wedged IOMMU are the same bug. Past the deadline's bound, the NPU
raises a DMA write error, which `rocket_job_irq_handler()` takes as `WARN_ON` (taint +512).
The IOMMU then reports `Enable stall request timed out` and `FORCE_RESET command timed out`,
after which every job on every path fails. That is what programming a job into a core that is
still writing the previous one does. No length on the fixed driver has reached it. It is not
an independent client-reachable defect.

**Nothing measured after the taint moves is a hardware answer about the chain.** A sweep on
the driver without the counter gate reported "the per-layer reference failed at layer 0" at
three lengths in a row. The geometry had passed minutes earlier, and the failures came purely
from the IOMMU already being wedged. Read `/proc/sys/kernel/tainted` after every length. When
it changes, reboot. On a kernel without `0022`, the `sweep` mode therefore leaves `wide` out
and stops a mix at its first non-bit-exact length.

The counter gate's cost is a fence that is not early, and the driver cannot recover it.
MobileNetV1-224 goes 5.0 -> 6.3 ms per inference in one fewer submit (the graph's runs cover
26 layers in two kicks rather than 25). That 1.3 ms is not a late fence brought forward. It
is the deadline-retiring fence signaled while most of the stream has not yet run, and the
trace shows it directly. On the 35-program kick the first `PC_DONE` lands at 12 us, and the
kick ends at 1921 us.

The DPU's own completion sets 10 us after the last program starts. It is already the earliest
safe instant, and the counter gate retires on it at the same microsecond a deadline does. The
caller must schedule any host work it overlaps with NPU work between submit and wait. No
driver signal legitimately arrives sooner.

The instrument is `tests/rk3576_chain_len.c`. Every mode takes three footprint knobs:
`ROCKET_CHAINLEN_C` for channels, `_P` for the plane and `_K` for the direct leg's kernel.
The length, the mix and the footprint therefore move independently. Its defaults are per mode,
and they are the shapes the modes were characterized at:

| mode | channels | plane | direct leg |
|---|---|---|---|
| `uniform`, `mixed`, `geom` | 32 | 32×32 | 1×1 |
| `wide` | 128 | 28×28 | 3×3 |

It asserts the kicked answer against the same handles run one submit each.

### The pooling layer

With the graph at 6.2 ms, its PPU pooling layer is 1.24-1.48 ms, 20% of the wall, for a
`1024x7x7 -> 1024x1x1` average. The work built on the three findings below takes the layer to
0.12 ms and the graph to 5.1 ms, byte-identical. The first finding is what made the other two
worth building.

The profile flag `ROCKET_RK3576_POOL_PROF=1` splits the layer's cost, per call, in
microseconds:

| bucket | us | what it is |
|---|---|---|
| the completion wait | 640 | `PREP_BO` on the output |
| the host cube scatter | 320 | row-major CHW into NC1HWC2 |
| three BO allocations | 300 | feature cube, surface, regcmd |
| the BO teardown | 150 | |
| the stamp | 8 | |
| the submit ioctl | 13 | |
| the de-scatter | 13 | |

#### Pool completion

**A pool raises no DPU completion, because it has no DPU stage.** The `PC_OPERATION_ENABLE`
register is a per-block bitmap. A convolution enables CNA/CORE/DPU/DPU_RDMA with `0x1D`, a
pool enables PPU and PPU_RDMA with `0x60`, and the two bit sets are disjoint.

The patch `patches/rk3576/npu/0012` retires a job when the DPU raises its own completion in
`INTERRUPT_RAW_STATUS`. For a pooling job that bit can never set. The job retires on the
`dpu_grace_us` deadline instead, paid in full on every submit. The wait tracks the parameter
count for count, which shows it is not retiring on a completion at all:

| `dpu_grace_us` | 500 | 250 | 100 |
|---|---|---|---|
| the pool's wait, us | 641 | 389 | 213 |

The PPU raises its own bits two positions over (`PPU_0` at `0x400`, `PPU_1` at `0x800`,
against the DPU's `0x100`/`0x200`). The patch `patches/rk3576/npu/0021` adds
`DRM_ROCKET_JOB_PPU_DONE`, which selects those bits in place of the DPU's, latched per
submit. It is a per-job flag for the same reason `0017`'s is. The driver cannot see which
class a job is in at `PC_DONE` time, and the userspace that wrote `PC_OPERATION_ENABLE` knows
it. The pool's wait goes 641 -> 71 us, which is the poll floor. The patch raises the
interface version to 1.3.

**That flag must be gated on the kernel version, not sent blind.** The submit ioctl
rejects a flag word it does not recognize, so an older kernel fails the submit rather than
ignoring the bit. The `DRM_ROCKET_JOB_NO_DPU_DONE` flag behaves the opposite way: a kernel
that knows the field simply ignores it. The library's `rocket_ppu_done_supported()` asks the
interface version.

**A pooling submit that names no class pays the fallback wait on every kernel, and the
shipped one is not a free baseline.** Every library entry names its class, so no shipping
path is exposed. A raw submit is exposed. The measurement uses one binary with the flag word
as the only variable. It covers the two gates whose harness helpers submitted with
`flags = 0` (`rk3576_row_pitch`, `rk3576_chain_pool`):

| gate | module | `flags = 0`, ms | `PPU_DONE`, ms |
|---|---|---|---|
| `chain_pool` | 1.5.0 shipped, `dpu_grace_us` 500 | 149 | 110 |
| `row_pitch` | 1.5.0 shipped, `dpu_grace_us` 500 | 229 | 186 |
| `chain_pool` | 1.6.0 candidate, wait untimed | 10300 | 104 |
| `row_pitch` | 1.6.0 candidate, wait untimed | 4804 | 185 |
| `pool_gate` | 1.5.0 shipped | 2461 | 2452 |
| `pool_gate` | 1.6.0 candidate | 4494 | 2452 |

Three facts follow. First, the untimed wait is what makes an unnamed class catastrophic
rather than merely slow. It costs 0022's 125 ms backstop times the submit count, so removing
the deadline cannot land until every caller names a class. Second, the shipped module already
charges these submits the full 500 us grace apiece. The 149 and 229 ms that read like a
baseline are therefore the cost itself. Flagged, the two modules agree to within the
run-to-run spread.

Third, the cost is invisible in exactly the places it is small, so enumerating the call sites
beats reading the walls. On the shipped module `pool_gate` moves 8 ms against a 2452 ms row,
~16 submits at 500 us. On the candidate module it moves 2033 ms. No shipped-module
measurement could therefore have pointed at it. The population is every caller of
`gen_pool_rk3576`: the library entry, which has always named its class, plus four raw test
submits in three files (`rk3576_row_pitch.c`, `rk3576_chain_pool.c`, and
`rk3576_pool_probe.c`'s `run_pool` and `dst_arm`). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]

With all four named, a full-list backstop count is module-independent (28 shipped against 29
candidate). Before they were named, it differed by ~10. **The wide-output class
(`NO_DPU_DONE`) is a separate population and was not enumerated here.**
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]

#### Cube-in pooling and residency

The PPU honors a source surface stride that is not a multiple of four, and that is what makes
a cube-in pool available at all. Every vendor pooling program carries `round4(iw*ih)` in
`PPU_RDMA`'s source-surface register. A direct convolution's output surface stride is
`ow*oh` exactly, and at a 7×7 plane those are 52 and 49. The join is therefore either a
register the part takes verbatim or nothing. The part takes it: bit-exact at 49, 25 and 9
elements per channel group (`rk3576_pool_probe lib`, both strides,
`ROCKET_RK3576_POOL_PACK_SRC=1` forcing the packed one). **A wrong stride there computes a
full and plausible surface rather than faulting**, so a gate checks it rather than assuming
it.

The pool is resident. The entries `rocket_pool_int8_pack_rk3576()`, `_prepacked_rk3576()`
and `_free_rk3576()` hold the three BOs and the register program across calls. The entry
`rocket_pool_int8_cube_in_rk3576()` points the handle at a producer's output surface, so
neither the scatter nor its cache maintenance runs. The transient `rocket_pool_int8_rk3576()`
is pack-run-free, so a prepacked call is bit-identical to a transient one by construction
rather than by two implementations agreeing.

What is left of the layer at 0.12 ms is the completion wait, 71 us of it.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-31]

#### Pool write guard

**The pool's write guard is whole-surface, so a partial write would pass it, and the rate
is under 0.025% of cells.** The guard asks whether any byte of the output differs from the
sentinel. The convolution path asks it of each row task's own extent. One observation raised
the question. Inside a full gate run, `rk3576_pool_probe bound` reported 1 wrong cell of 40
once: 128 elements at `c=16` on a 16×16 plane. That is exactly one output row across all
sixteen channels, which is the shape a dropped block of atoms has.

Measured as a rate, it does not reproduce. The `bound` mode ran 150 times quiet and 150 times
under four streaming-memcpy threads, 6000 cells an arm. Its sweep is max pooling k3 s2 with a
leading pad, over planes 16-112 and channels 16-128. It gives 0 wrong cells in 12000. By the
rule of three, that bounds the rate at 0.025% of cells (95%), which is two orders below the
cost of a per-row check.

The load arm has its positive control from the same session and the same load. There the
int32 wide writer drops atoms on 30.8% of its row tasks. The memory system was therefore under
the pressure the hazard tracks, and the pool did not follow it. That is consistent with every
other measurement of the drop. The drop has only ever been seen on a writer whose output
element is wider than one byte. The conv entries and the narrow int8 matmul were clean at this
same load.

**The trigger is the context, and it is a preceding unit-kernel pool.** The event reproduced
in the very next full gate run, and then on demand. The test runs three arms of 30 runs each,
which differ only in what runs before each `bound` sweep:

| what precedes each sweep | runs with a wrong cell |
|---|---:|
| `rk3576_pool_probe pad` (pooling with a unit kernel on one axis) | **2 of 30** |
| a wide-output int32 matmul (the poisoning source) | 0 of 30 |
| nothing (the control) | 0 of 30, plus the 300 above |

Both failures are byte-identical to each other and to what the gate run showed. They are the
first two cells of the sweep, at `c=16`. The 16×16 plane has 128 wrong elements, exactly one
output row across all sixteen channels, and the 24×24 one has 2302 of 2304 wrong. That
128-element signature is also exactly the original single observation.

This is therefore
neither a partial write slipping past a whole-surface check nor the atom drop. **A pooling
program with a unit kernel leaves the part in a state where the next process's first pooling
submits compute wrong.** The state clears itself after two.

This joins the other open pooling question to this one. The unit-kernel cells of
`rk3576_pool_probe pad` are themselves unstable, which is why that mode is not an asserting
gate. The same unit-kernel program is what poisons the next process here. Treat them as one
question. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-02]

#### Pool cube output

A pool is a cube producer too, through `rocket_pool_int8_cube_out_rk3576()`, `_cube_of_` and
`_cube_out_at_`. That lets a pool sit between two convolutions with no host transpose on
either side. It also lets a pool write its output into a slice of a residual add's buffer,
beside another producer's. The PPU's own output surface stride is `round4(ow*oh)` rather than
the plane. That is no bound on the consumer: the CNA's DDR channel-group jump is a register
the emitter fills. The part honors it at any value at or above the plane, so the cube carries
it [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_net_gate`, 2026-08-02].

**The destination stride is refused rather than worked around.** The pooling program derives
it from the output plane, and no register to move it is decoded. A slice at any other
channel-group stride is therefore rejected, and a plane whose element count is already a
multiple of four needs nothing. Both entries also refuse a channel count that is not a
multiple of 16, because pooling reduces within a channel. A partial group's tail is then
whatever the input cube's padding held, not a value the handle can declare.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_net_gate`, 2026-08-02]

### Finding the runs is the library's, not the caller's

The entry `rocket_chain_new_rk3576()` takes one run of already-linked handles and asserts it.
That is right for the primitive and wrong for a frontend, which has a graph and no reason to
know about runs. The planner `rocket_chain_plan_rk3576()` groups an array of nodes into
maximal runs and splits them at the program-count bound. It is pure: it uses no hardware and
writes nothing on the handles.

An unset node breaks a run. That is how a caller says "this layer is not a resident
convolution or pool" or "a run must not start here". A chain scatters the tensor it is
handed, so a layer whose input the caller prepares on the host must not begin one. The
