convolution-only `rocket_conv2d_int8_chain_plan_rk3576()` and `_chain_new_rk3576()` are the
same functions with every node a convolution. There is one implementation, because two would
be a second set of rules to keep in agreement.

The run's rule is weaker than the pair's, and the part is what sets it. A pairwise finder
grows a run while `cube_linked(h[i], h[i+1])` holds, that is, while layer j reads layer j-1's
surface. That is a sufficient condition and not the necessary one. A chained stream honors
read-after-write between its programs, so what a stream needs is two conditions:

- Every layer but the last leaves a cube.
- Every layer but the first reads a cube that some earlier member wrote, not necessarily the
  one immediately before it.

The rule is sound because the chain emits its programs in array order, so "an earlier layer"
is "an earlier program". It is also a strict superset of the pairwise test, so every run the
pairwise test admits still chains.

A ResNet block that changes width is exactly the difference. Its 1×1 shortcut convolution
reads the block input three layers back and writes a slice of the add's buffer. A pairwise
rule therefore strands each of the three between two kicks, even though no host work happens
on either side of it. Widening the rule takes ResNet-18 from four runs covering 24 layers to
one of 27 covering 29 programs, byte-identical. It goes 11 submits -> 5 and 10.5 -> 9.9 ms.
Neither MobileNet moves, because each is already one run.

Requiring an in-run producer rather than merely a stable buffer is the conservative half. An
operand no member of the stream writes is a BO the job never names. The layout's BO dedup
rests on every intermediate surface being some member's output.

### A pooling layer sits inside the stream

A classifier puts a pooling layer in the middle of a run. A chain built from convolution
handles alone breaks at a pool, and on ResNet-18 that isolates four layers:

- The stem
- The maxpool after it
- The final average pool
- The classifier

The reason to doubt that a pool can sit in the stream is `PC_OPERATION_ENABLE`, a per-block
bitmap rather than a constant. A convolution enables CNA/CORE/DPU with `0x1D` and a pool
enables PPU/PPU_RDMA with `0x60`, and the two sets are disjoint.

The bitmap does not partition a stream. The probe runs `conv -> pool -> conv` as one batched
job. It compares that job against two references: the same three as three submits, and each
consumer alone on an input nothing wrote. Over five geometries (planes 16 to 110, channels 32
and 64), 20 of 20 iterations gave the same pool output and the same output from the
convolution after it. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_chain_pool.c`, 2026-08-02]

Both intermediates are read, so a failure would name the boundary it happened at. The
sentinel separates "never ran" from "ran on stale input", which a fresh BO's zeros cannot.

A run is therefore described by nodes rather than by convolution handles. Each node, a
`rocket_chain_node_rk3576`, holds exactly one of a convolution handle and a pooling handle.

A pool can only be an **interior** node. A pool that began a run would need the chain to
scatter into its cube. A pool that ended a run would need its output de-scattered. Both are
the host work the stream exists to remove. The finder therefore trims a trailing pool off a
run and never opens one on a pool.

A pool has two bookkeeping consequences. It names only its surface in the job's BO lists,
because it has no weight cube and no coefficient group. Its surface is also **never
re-stamped inside the verify bracket**, because the pooling handle keeps no "already stamped"
flag for the chain to clear. Its sentinel therefore goes on at the start of the next call,
one extra bracket for at most one layer.

All three networks then go out as one hardware kick in one submit. All three are
byte-identical, with the same task counts and TFLite's own top-1:

| network | wall, ms | submits |
|---|---|---|
| ResNet-18 | 9.9 -> 9.2 | 5 -> 1 |
| MobileNetV1 | 5.1 -> 4.9 | 3 -> 1 |
| MobileNetV2 | 6.8 -> 6.7 | 3 -> 1 |

A marginal submit prices differently here than in the earlier kick A/B. It is worth 0.13 ms
on ResNet-18 (10 submits removed for 1.3 ms) and 0.05-0.10 ms on the MobileNets (2 removed
for 0.1-0.2). The earlier A/B measured 0.145-0.25 when a graph still had tens of submits.
What a submit is worth is a function of what else is in flight. Re-measure it rather than
scaling the earlier figure.

The finder applies the program bound itself, in place of a build-and-shorten loop in the
caller. The finder is the better place for it. A layer is one or several programs, so the
longest legal run is not a function of the layer count. A run that is too long is split
rather than refused. The bound rests on two queries, `rocket_conv2d_int8_programs_rk3576()`
and `rocket_conv2d_int8_chain_max_programs_rk3576()`.

**No graph-execution object goes inside the library.** The frontends already own scheduling.
The one thing they cannot own is which streams the part will run.

### Narrow input-channel counts on the direct datapath

A narrow input-channel count runs on the direct datapath. The int8 direct cube is a 32-channel
MAC group at every count: `pad_ic(3)` and `pad_ic(32)` are both 32. An `ic` of 3 and an `ic`
of 32 therefore give the same register program, the same weight-cube size and the same
feature-cube size. The channels past `ic` are the cube's own padding.

Routing every `ic <= 4` to the CNA's packed-image sub-encoding hides a path that needs no host
widening. That path also carries none of the packed-image encoding's three geometry bounds:

- A non-zero left pad
- An output width of exactly `iw/stride`
- That width also a multiple of 16

The field `rocket_conv2d_desc.direct_datapath` asks for it. The int8 entries honor it. The
RK3576 fp16 entry refuses it for two reasons. Its float cube groups sixteen input channels
rather than thirty-two, and no `ic` below five has been run through it. The RK3588 generator
satisfies it by construction, having only one convolution encoding.

The route is bit-exact on 24 shapes, including a TFLite 224 stem at its own asymmetric
extent. On the graph the stem goes 2.21-2.24 ms to 1.97-1.99, byte-identical over all 401408
elements against both the widened lowering and the packed-image one.

The saving is the host buffer, not the scatter. The NEON packer stores whole 16-byte atoms
whatever the live channel count is. The scatter therefore writes the same 802 KB either way,
and only its reads shrink (150 KB against 401 KB). What goes away is the widened image's own
allocation, fill and copy. The stem's feature bucket moves 0.71-0.91 ms to 0.60, and the rest
of its wall is the submit.

**The CNA's border substitution covers the whole programmed channel group.** At a pad tap the
cube's padding channels therefore read the border constant, while at an interior tap they read
whatever was memset into them. The `B` term sums over every tap. The disagreement lands in the
accumulator scaled by that output position's pad-tap count. That count varies per position
and cannot be folded into `A` at all. With both the input and the weight zero point non-zero,
every `ic` from 1 to 31 computes a wrong border with an exact interior.

The defect does not depend on the direct-datapath flag: `ic` 8, 16 and 24 reach it with no
flag involved. The existing gates could not see it, because every existing shape had `ic` a
multiple of 32 or `w_zp` zero.

The fix has two halves, and both are required, because either half alone is wrong everywhere
instead of only on the border. First, the cube's padding channels hold the border constant
itself, the input zero point. That includes the allocation's fill and the dead lanes of a
partial C2 atom, which the NEON packer must not zero. Second, the zero-point fold counts the
programmed taps: `A = bias - in_zp*sum_w + in_zp*w_zp*icreg*kh*kw`. The fix is inert in two
cases: at `ic` a multiple of 32 the two counts are equal, and at `w_zp == 0` the term is zero.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_conv_lib_gate.c` `nic`, 2026-07-30]

### Asymmetric padding as an output extent

An asymmetric pad is an output extent, not a materialized border. The CNA has no configured
trailing pad. It takes the pad its last window consumes, derived from the output extent and
the leading pad. The emitter's `r76_trail_pad()` is that derivation, and `CNA_PAD_CON0`'s
bottom and right fields carry the result. TFLite's SAME at an even input and stride two pads
`pad_before = 0` and `pad_after = 1`. That is a leading pad of zero against an output extent
one larger than the symmetric formula gives, and nothing has to be copied.

That geometry is inside the int8 correctness envelope. Six shapes of
`tests/rk3576_conv_shapes.h` carry a zero leading pad against a consumed trailing one, direct
and depthwise, and all are bit-exact. A descriptor that can only derive the output from a
symmetric pad forces a graph runner to materialize the border into a row-major buffer. That
buffer breaks the cube chain at every stride-2 depthwise layer.

`rocket_conv2d_desc.oh/.ow` name the extent. Zero means "whatever `rocket_conv2d_oh/ow`
derive", so every caller that memsets the struct keeps its existing behavior. The functions
`rocket_conv2d_trail_y/_x` return the pad the extent implies. **The bound is the kernel**: a
trailing pad of `kh` or more is an output row whose whole window is pad, and it is refused.
The fp16 RK3576 path, both packed-image paths and `rocket_conv2d_plan` (the RK3588 fp16
generator) refuse an extent they did not derive themselves. That geometry is claimed at int8
and measured nowhere else.

On MobileNetV1-224, five layers pad asymmetrically: the stem and the four stride-2 depthwise
layers. The derived trailing pad matches the model on 30 of 30 compute layers. Every layer
stays bit-exact against a CPU model of the part's own arithmetic.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29]

**Trap: a per-layer reference must be told the same lowering.** The gate's per-axis pass
builds its own descriptor. Without the extent, its entry computes 55 rows and its reference
computes 56. The five asymmetric layers then report maxdiff 255 at 5-29% exact. That is a
lowering disagreement, and it reads exactly like a hardware fault.

### The depthwise weight zero point

A depthwise weight zero point rides in the weight cube, and the graph's thirteen depthwise
layers run because of it. Every per-tensor TFLite depthwise filter carries a zero point, and
the 48-byte depthwise coefficient group has no `B` field to fold it into. It needs none,
because the int8 depthwise cube gives every (channel, tap) two live bytes and the datapath
adds them. The sum is a nine-bit effective weight, so `w - w_zp` is carried exactly across
the pair, bounded only by the pair's own [-256, 254]. The coefficient `A` already computes
`-in_zp*(sum_w - w_zp*taps)`, which is the fold a pre-centered cube wants, so nothing
downstream changes.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_conv_lib_gate.c` group `zp`]

Re-centering in int8 instead is not available, because 12 of this model's 13 depthwise layers
overflow. Re-quantizing to a symmetric scale would be an accuracy loss of up to 1.65x
coarsening rather than an exact answer. The lowering is bit-exact at both signs, at both
channel-group boundaries, at k=5 and at stride 2.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_conv_lib_gate.c` group `zp`]

### Per-axis weights on the depthwise path

Per-axis weight quantization reaches the depthwise path, and depthwise is the cheaper half of
it. The 48-byte depthwise coefficient group carries the same per-output-channel int16 `C` as
the 64-byte one, and nothing else stands in the way. Depthwise is the easy case because its
accumulator bound, `128*sum|w|` over `kh*kw` taps rather than `ic*kh*kw`, is one to two
orders of magnitude smaller. The `C` ramp therefore reaches the int16 field's own 32767
ceiling. The gain resolution a single `OUT_CVT` shift leaves is then finer than the direct
path's at the same channel count. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_perchannel_gate.c`]

A depthwise layer is also one task whatever the channel count, because output channel `c` is
bound to input channel `c`. With no output-channel window to program and no scale sort to
make, the accuracy comes without the submits the direct path spends on it. Over eight shapes
(kernels 1/3/5, both strides, 32-256 channels), the worst distance to an exact per-axis
requant is 0.5 counts at spreads to 100x. The worst distance is 0.8 at 1000x, against 0.5-1.9
on the direct path. A MobileNet is mostly depthwise, which is what a per-axis model needs from
this path. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_perchannel_gate.c`]

### Per-axis weights in a chained graph

Per-axis weight quantization also runs as a graph, where the error does not compound. A
per-axis requant's error is a per-layer quantity. What one layer gives up to the single
`OUT_CVT` shift is not what twenty-nine of them give up, and only a network answers that.
The gate `rk3576_net_gate peraxis` runs MobileNetV1-224 with every convolution and depthwise
convolution on the per-axis entry, chained.

The per-axis model comes from re-quantizing this model's own weights per output channel. A
TFLite int8 converter does the same to the same float tensor. Each channel moves from the
shared grid onto `max|w_c - w_zp| * w_scale / 127`, with its weights and bias rescaled. The
activations keep the model's per-tensor scales and zero points. The resulting spread is real
and wide: 150x at the stem, 145x and 129x at the early depthwise layers, and 105x at layer 25.

Every one of the 29 compute layers is within one count of an exact per-axis requant. On each
layer, 87.7% to 99.9% of elements are identical to it. The chained network returns TFLite's
own top-1 and its whole top-5.

The per-op gate's worst-case reading of 26.6 counts stands where it was measured (a synthetic
100x spread at ic=128 with the whole layer in one tile). A real model's spread does not land
there. The planner sizes `C` against the actual accumulator bound, and this model's
wide-scale channels are also its small-accumulator ones. The output-channel tile lever is
never taken. The predicted worst-case gain error is 0.003%-0.82% per layer, under the 1%
target. The planner therefore keeps each layer in one task and pays no extra submit.

That pass asserts what a graph is for. No layer refuses, and no layer is far enough from an
exact per-axis requant to be a defect rather than the single shift. The compounded chain still
returns the framework's label. The bit-exact assertion against the chip's own arithmetic stays
in `rk3576_perchannel_gate`. There the planner's `C` multipliers and `OUT_CVT` shift are
visible to a model, and in a graph they are not.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_net_gate.c peraxis`]

**Neither of those two gates can see the channel order, and both miss it the same way.** Every
cell of the per-op gate and every layer of that per-axis pass runs a materializing call. The
de-scatter walks the sort permutation, so a surface written in scale-sorted channel order is
read back in model order and scores exact. The order is observable only where a surface is
consumed as a cube, and no per-axis shape in the corpus is consumed that way. The corpus's
per-axis coverage is a synthetic re-quantization of one graph, run one layer at a time.

Reaching a channel-order defect takes a real per-axis model through a frontend, and its
symptom is a whole network's label rather than any layer's numbers. **A gate that
materializes every intermediate cannot see a layout defect.** The same is true of any accuracy
pass whose reference is fed the same tensor the part was.

## The residual add on the convolution datapath

This part computes the residual add on the convolution datapath, not on the DPU's elementwise
stage. A skip connection is the one primitive between this part's op library and a second
network. MobileNetV2 puts one on ten of its seventeen inverted residuals, and every ResNet
block ends in one. MobileNetV1, the graph gated here, is a straight chain and has none.

The DPU's elementwise stage computes an affine requantization of exactly one cube. The search
for a second operand is exhaustive: six sweeps over the whole register interface, set out in
[rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).

Two of those sweeps close the question, because they cross axes. The first crosses every
register the program leaves at zero with the full 32-rung output gain ladder. A placement
sweep at one gain and a gain sweep at one placement are each blind to one kind of operand.
That operand both sits somewhere unexpected and enters the accumulator unscaled. The second
is a delta probe that walks a single non-zero atom over an operand buffer eight cubes long.
It shows that the program reads exactly the addressed cube and nothing within two cubes
either side of it.

The add is therefore lowered onto a convolution. Concatenate the two operands along the
channel axis, and convolve with a 1×1 kernel of two diagonal blocks (`W[o][o] = w1`,
`W[o][C+o] = w2`, zero elsewhere):

```
out[o] = requant( w1 * (a[o] - a_zp) + w2 * (b[o] - b_zp) )
```

The lowering is bit-exact against a CPU model of the part's own arithmetic over nine
MobileNetV2 and ResNet-18 residual shapes. The shapes span channels 24 to 512 and planes 7×7
to 56×56. At every shape it is within one count of an exact float residual add, at both
unequal operand scales and unequal zero points (`tests/rk3576_residual_add.c`, 12 of 12).

Two of its properties are better than the vendor's own elementwise program:

- **The operands can carry different scales.** The ratio rides in the weights as `w2/w1`, so
  the pair only has to be representable as two int8s, about one part in 127. The vendor's
  program has one operand converter, and its compiler works around that by quantizing both
  operands to a common scale. Two graph inputs calibrated 64x apart still compile to a single
  converter. The OUT_CVT gain then comes out as the ratio of one shared input scale to the
  output's.
- **The two zero points ride in the bias, exactly.** The datapath has one input zero point,
  but `w2 * (a_zp - b_zp)` is a per-output-channel constant, which is what the bias is.
  Nothing is approximated.

### Fusion into a block's last convolution

The datapath also fuses the add into a convolution, and that is measured rather than argued.
A real block's quantization then refuses the weight the fusion needs, as the next paragraphs
set out.

The concatenation is along channels. A feature cube's channel-group stride is the plane, and
a direct conv's output surface stride is `ow*oh`. Two convolutions writing adjacent halves of
one buffer are therefore already the concatenated operand, byte for byte, with no host work.
That is the same identity the cube chain rests on. A block's last convolution absorbs its own
skip with `C` more input channels and an identity block at its kernel's center tap. The add
then costs no program at all, against the one program the vendor pays.

It is the hardware's own idiom. The vendor compiler folds `Add(Conv(x), x)` into exactly this
shape, which is why that graph is useless as a capture of an add. The fused form is bit-exact
at MobileNetV2's project convolution for every residual width (1×1, `ic` 168 to 1120) and at
ResNet-18's second convolution (3×3, `ic` 128 to 512).

**What stops the fusion is the identity weight, and the weight-slice rule never binds
first.** The slice rule `ic*kh*kw <= 4608` is real. A 3×3 reaches it exactly at `C = 256`
(`512*9 = 4608`) and is refused at `C = 512`. A real block is refused long before that, by
its own quantization.

A standalone add chooses its own weight scale, because its weights are two diagonal blocks
rather than a quantized tensor. There `w_scale = 1/w1` is a free parameter, which is how the
operand ratio fits in a pair of int8s. A fused add inherits the convolution's weight scale,
which the convolution's own filter fixes. The skip therefore has to reach the output through
the accumulator unit the main term already uses:

    w_skip = skip_scale / (in_scale * w_scale) = (skip_scale / out_scale) / gain

Here `gain` is the convolution's own requant gain. A residual's two operands have comparable
ranges, so `skip_scale/out_scale` is order one (0.64 to 0.98 on this model). The fusion
therefore needs `gain > ~1/127`, a shallow contraction. A project convolution over 576 to 960
channels runs at 1/242 to 1/893. **All ten of MobileNetV2's residual blocks want an identity
weight of 173 to 865, and not one fits int8.**

Nothing else in the program can move that weight. The coefficient group's `C` multiplies the
whole accumulator, main term and skip together, so a per-channel gain cannot separate them. A
per-axis quantization makes it strictly worse, because the per-tensor `w_scale` is the
largest of the per-channel ones.

`rocket_residual_fuse_weight_rk3576()` is that arithmetic and that refusal, and it is pure.
Where the weight does fit, it also carries the bias correction `w_skip*(in_zp - skip_zp)`. A
block inside the envelope lands one count from an exact float main+skip on the part.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_residual_add.c`, 2026-07-31]

The standalone add is therefore the form a real network uses. The same slice rule caps it at
`C = 2304`, well past any real residual width. The standalone weight cube is `2C*C` bytes,
512 KiB at ResNet-18's widest stage, a resident-weights cost paid once rather than per
inference. Measured transiently (no resident handle, so mostly host packing), a standalone
add is 0.6-3.3 ms across the shape table.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_residual_add.c`, 2026-07-31]

## MobileNetV2

MobileNetV2-224 at int8, the second network, runs end to end on this part. The graph has 65
layers:

- 36 convolutions
- 17 depthwise convolutions
- 10 residual adds
- A PPU pooling layer
- A classifier

Every one of the 64 compute layers is bit-exact against a CPU model of the part's own
arithmetic, and the graph returns TFLite's own top-1. It uses the same gate and the same blob
format as MobileNetV1: `rk3576_net_gate --net v2`, built by the same `mknet.py`.

The datapath needs nothing new for it. The add needs the lowering above, and the graph needs
two pieces of bookkeeping. One is a layer that names the layers its operands come from,
rather than assuming "the one before". The other is a buffer that outlives the ping-pong for
the ten tensors a skip reads three to five layers later.

Against TFLite, the graph differs on 0.0573% of elements (3951 of 6896777), every one of them
by a single count. That is a lower rate than MobileNetV1's 0.095%, over a graph twice as deep.
The ten adds are inside that one count, although the add is not TFLite's arithmetic at all.
TFLite scales both operands onto a common intermediate. This lowering carries their scale
ratio in a pair of int8 weights and requantizes once.

The ratio is the term that can cost accuracy, and the remedy is to search every denominator
rather than anchor the larger term at 127. On these ten skips, the best rational
approximation under 128 is 1e-5 to 5e-4 relative, where the anchored choice is 1e-3 to 4e-3.
At 1e-4, the second operand's full int8 range moves an output by a tenth of a count. That is
why no add exceeds the single count a requant difference already owes.

When each layer is fed the graph's own previous output, the disagreements compound as they
do on V1, and the top-1 survives. The tail of the top-5 reorders, and on this image that is
not a disagreement worth reading. TFLite's own second through fifth classes all sit at
probability 2/255, so their order is a tie-break between equal values.

### Chain fragmentation on MobileNetV2

The residual topology, not the channel alignment, is what fragments a chain. The cube chain is
the measurement to read, because a residual network is where the join rules first have
something to refuse. MobileNetV1 links 25 of its 28 adjacent pairs into 2 runs covering 26
layers. With every producer owning its own output buffer, MobileNetV2 links 32 of 63 into 16
runs of two or three layers. The gate counts the refusals by reason rather than reporting the
total:

| refused | why |
|---:|---|
| 10 | the consumer is an ADD, whose input is a host-built concatenation |
| 10 | the producer feeds a skip and so must leave a row-major tensor |
| 6 | the consumer's input channel count is not a multiple of 32 |
| 4 | the producer's output surface is not a feature cube (padded stride, or tiled) |
| 1 | the consumer is not a resident convolution |

Twenty of the thirty-one refusals are the skip topology and six are the alignment, the
opposite of what the channel counts suggest. MobileNetV2's widths 16, 24 and 144 are not
multiples of 32, and they do cost joins. Three times as many are lost to the skips. An add
reads a concatenation the host builds, and the tensor that add reads has to survive in
row-major form until the add runs. A part with no alignment rule at all would still fragment
this graph into runs of three.

Both skip classes, the twenty refusals, want one thing: an address. That address is a producer
writing its output surface at a channel-group offset inside a larger buffer, and the next
section sets it out. With three changes, the same graph links 62 of 63 into one run covering
62 layers. The three are that address, the padding-channel rule and the producer's own group
stride.

### A cube's base is a plain address, so a concatenation costs nothing

A convolution's feature base and its output base are both plain addresses on this part. A
program whose base moves by G channel groups inside a bigger buffer has these properties:

- It computes exactly what it computes at offset 0.
- It writes only its own slice.
- It leaves every byte outside that slice untouched.
- It composes with the row-task offset already added to the same register.

The measurement covers 34 placements at three geometries, one of them row-split, with an
unaligned group index (`tests/rk3576_offset_cube.c`).

The two buckets that hold twenty of the refusals are therefore not a hardware bound. Allocate
one buffer per add, and give operand A the low slice and operand B the high one. Point the two
producers at those slices with `rocket_conv2d_int8_cube_out_at_rk3576()`. The add then reads
the whole buffer as its feature cube. No concatenation is built, and the skip source does not
have to materialize a row-major tensor for it. Its output is the second operand, in place, and
the layer after it reads the same slice back as its own cube.

The library supports this through buffer ownership, not through the encoding. A handle can be
told to write into a caller's buffer instead of allocating its own. The functions
`rocket_rk3576_cube_alloc()` and `_cube_slice()` are that buffer and its views. A
`rocket_rk3576_cube` carries a byte offset that `_cube_in_` and the pooling entry honor on the
read side. The PPU is a separate program with its own DMA. A pool over a producer's high slice
is bit-identical to the same pool over that producer's row-major output.

The scheme needs four things, three of them bookkeeping and one a real bound:

- **A slice starts every sixteen channels.** Sixteen channels share every atom, so channel 24
  is not an address. A concatenation of two 24-channel operands therefore puts operand B at
  channel 32, with the gap carrying zero weights. Zero weights contribute nothing at any
  content, so the same weight matrix, descriptor and CPU reference serve both layouts.
- **The wiring is a pair, refused as one.** Operand B's producer is also the layer before the
  next block's expand convolution. Placing it therefore means that layer has to read the same
  slice as its cube. A producer that leaves no row-major tensor for a consumer that cannot take
  a cube would leave the graph with nothing at all.
- **A BO is named once per job.** The driver locks each BO through `drm_exec`, and locking one
  twice fails with `EALREADY`, which rejects the submit. A layer reading one slice of a
  concatenation buffer while writing another is exactly one BO in both roles. That BO is named
  as the output, and a chained stream deduplicates its whole BO list the same way.
- **A shared surface is never re-stamped after a kick.** The sentinel normally goes back on a
  surface at the end of the call that read it. A slice is read later in the same inference, by
  a program outside the run that wrote it. Its stamp therefore goes on at the start of the next
  call instead. Stamping it in place would replace the layer's output with 0xA5, invisibly.

**Take a placed producer's slice from the producer's own cube, not from the slice the link
pass built.** The two name the same bytes, but only the producer can say what its tail holds.
The consumer that reads operand B's slice back can be one whose channel count is not a
multiple of 32. Taking the slice from `rocket_conv2d_int8_cube_of_rk3576()` after placing the
producer is what makes that statement travel. It is the difference between nine of the ten
adds wiring up and all ten.

The alignment refusal belongs to the weight zero point, not to the channel count. A handle
whose `ic` is not a multiple of 32 fills the channels past `ic` with its border constant. A
producer's surface instead carries whatever the DPU wrote into its own padded output channels.
Those channels' weights are the zeros the cube was memset with, so their MAC contribution is
zero whatever they hold.

What is not zero at a non-zero weight zero point is the coefficient group's B term. The DPU
adds `B*sum(x)` over every tap of the whole programmed group, padding channels included.

A symmetric weight quantization therefore joins at any channel count. That covers every TFLite
int8 filter, and every per-axis one by construction. An asymmetric one from a direct producer
also joins, because that producer's own partial output group lands on its output zero point.
That is the constant the consumer's fold assumes (see "What a producer leaves past its live
output channels" above). So does an asymmetric one from a depthwise producer, once its surface
carries the deeper tail the host fills at pack time.

**No channel count refuses a join on this part**, and neither does a producer's padded group
stride. What remains, on 63 adjacent pairs:

| refused | why |
|---:|---|
| 0 | every adjacent pair joins |

### Inference time by lever

Measured at `bench 100`, warm, with one hardware kick per run of cube-linked layers:

| | ms/inference | submits |
|---|---:|---:|
| transient weights | 112.9 | 64 |
| resident weights | 26.1 | 64 |
| + tensors kept in cube layout | 18.2-18.5 | 64 |
| + cross-layer kick | 15.7-16.3 | 40 |
| + concatenation buffers | 13.8 | 37 |
| + the padding-channel join | 10.0-10.4 | 9 |
| + one bracket for the sentinel | 8.8-8.9 | 9 |
| + the depthwise producer's tail | 7.5-7.7 | 7 |
| + the depthwise producer's group stride | 6.9-7.1 | 3 |
| + a pooling layer inside the stream | 6.7 | 1 |

Every rung is byte-identical, with the same logits, the same top-5 and the same 77 tasks. The
value of the concatenation buffers, the two padding-channel rungs and the group stride is not
the memcpys they remove. With them, an add, an unaligned channel count and a 7×7 depthwise all
stop being walls between two chained runs. The submit count falls with the walls.

At the last rung the whole graph is a single run. The pool rides in the kick and the
classifier behind it, which is worth 0.1 ms here. The submit count moves and the wall does
not, because at this point a marginal submit prices about 0.05-0.10 ms on this graph.

The same ladder on MobileNetV1 ends at 4.9-5.1 ms in 1 submit. The V1 graph does not move
under the concatenation or padding-channel levers at all. Its widths are all multiples of 32,
and it has no skips. The sentinel bracket does reach it (6.4 -> 5.4), and so does the group
stride, which closes its two 7×7 depthwise joins (5.4 -> 5.1, 5 submits -> 3). Residency
remains the largest single lever (4.3x), and no single layer dominates.

Per-output-channel weight scales run here too, with the same result as on V1. Every one of the
53 weighted layers lands within one count of an exact per-axis requant, at real scale spreads
to 200x. The output-channel tile lever is never taken.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_net_gate.c`, 2026-07-31]

## ResNet-18

ResNet-18-224 at int8, the third network, runs on this part. The graph has 32 layers:

- 21 convolutions
- 8 residual adds
- A max pool
- An average pool
- A classifier

It is a different question from the two MobileNets rather than a bigger instance of the same
one. It is 3×3-heavy where both of those are 1×1-heavy, and it also has the following:

- A 7×7 stem
- A max pool
- A fused ReLU on every add
- Explicit torchvision padding

The padding puts a lead pad of 3 against a trailing 2 on the stem, an asymmetry in the
direction TFLite's SAME never produces.

No per-tensor quantized ResNet-18 is published. MobileNetV1 and V2 arrive from
google-coral/test_data already quantized per tensor. Every quantized ResNet-18 in circulation
is per-axis or float. The script `tests/data/rk3576-net/mkresnet18.py` therefore assembles
one:

- The timm `resnet18.a1_in1k` safetensors checkpoint (torchvision's parameter layout under
  torchvision's names, read with a 30-line parser rather than a framework)
- Batch norm folded into each convolution
- The ImageNet normalization folded into conv1, so the network's input is the raw pixel and
  the quantized input tensor is the identity on bytes
- TFLite post-training quantization with per-channel disabled

The script asserts both the byte-identity input and the per-tensor weights. A quantizer flag
that silently stopped working would produce a model the gate reads wrong, rather than one it
refuses. The tail is `AveragePooling2D 7x7 -> a 1x1 CONV_2D classifier -> softmax`, which is
MobileNetV1's own tail and keeps `FULLY_CONNECTED` and `MEAN` out of the op set.

The model deviates from torchvision in one place. The explicit 3-pixel border of conv1 is
padded in the raw pixel domain, so it is black where torchvision's is the channel mean. It is
a 3-pixel frame on a 224-pixel plane, and everything is scored against this model's own
TFLite output.

Against TFLite, the graph differs on 0.0386% of elements (1327 of 3438568), all by a single
count. That is the lowest rate of the three networks. Every add, every 3×3 convolution, both
pools and the classifier are bit-exact against a CPU model of the part's own arithmetic. The
chained graph returns TFLite's own top-1.

| | ms/inference | submits |
|---|---:|---:|
| transient weights | 144.9 | 31 |
| resident weights | 35.4 | 31 |
| + tensors kept in cube layout | 32.8 | 31 |
| + cross-layer kick | 18.3 | 25 |
| + every refusal closed | **10.5** | 11 |

Every rung is byte-identical, with the same logits, the same top-5 and the same 38 tasks. With
the refusals open, the chain fragments far more than either MobileNet's. It makes 18 joins of
30 adjacent pairs, in 5 runs covering 11 layers, against V2's 62 of 63. None of the three
refusal classes is a property of the part, as the section on them below sets out. The channel
counts play no part: every width here is a multiple of 64.

### Lever decomposition

A count is the wrong currency for what each lever is worth here. Four flags move
independently, so the rungs above decompose (`bench 100`, 2 repeats, spread under 0.3 ms):

| arm | joins | submits | ms |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| no cube layout | 0 | 31 | 34.5-34.6 |
| + adjacent-pair joins | 11 | 31 | 22.8-23.1 |
| + cross-layer kick | 11 | 26 | 20.7-20.8 |
| + concatenation-wired adds | 24 + 8 far | 17 | 13.7-13.9 |
| + shared surfaces | 27 + 11 far | 11 | **10.6** |
| + the run rule, not the pair rule | 27 + 11 far | 5 | 9.9 |
| + a pooling layer inside the stream | 27 + 11 far | **1** | **9.2** |

Eleven adjacent-pair joins are worth about 11.6 ms at a constant submit count. All of that is
host transposes and the buffer machinery around them, and none of it is dispatch. The kick's
five submits there are worth 2.2 ms. The transposes are the larger term by about 5x, and **a
cap taken over submits alone misses that**. A submit prices 0.15-0.45 ms depending on which
A/B is asked, and a join 0.10-6.80 ms depending on the bytes it carries.

The last two rungs are dispatch alone, with no join change, no task change and the same 38
programs. They take the graph from 11 submits to 1 for 1.4 ms, about 0.13 ms per submit. That
is at the bottom of the earlier 0.145-0.25 band, and the MobileNets price it lower still
(0.05-0.10). **What a marginal submit is worth falls as a graph runs out of them.** Re-measure
it rather than scale a figure taken on a thirty-submit graph.

**Fix the iteration count across the arms, and 10 is not enough.** The row-major arm reads
43.3-45.1 ms at `bench 10` and 34.5-34.6 at `bench 100`, and every other arm in the table is
identical at both. The row-major arm does the most host allocation, a `calloc`/`free` of a
concatenation buffer per add per inference on top of the ping-pong. The first few inferences
pay something the rest do not. One explanation is glibc's dynamic mmap threshold rising once
those blocks are freed [hypothesis, not measured]. An A/B read across arms at different
counts overstates this one lever by 1.8x.

The refusals are therefore counted in bytes as well as in reasons. A join removes two
transposes over the producer's whole output surface, and a 56×56 pair moves 64x what a 7×7
one does:

| refused | KiB | why |
|---:|---:|---|
| 6 | 514 | the producer feeds a skip and must leave a row-major tensor |
| 4 | 368 | the consumer is an ADD reading a host-built concatenation |
| 2 | 196 | the consumer is not a resident convolution |

The 18 joins that exist at that stage carry 2278 KiB, against the table's refused bytes.
**That ratio is an ordering and not a cap.** The 0.47 share of 23.0 ms is still most of a wall
that contains a 2.94 ms stem. The per-join value is not uniform either: the two classes
already closed differ by 8x. What prices a pair is a finite difference, and
`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_NOJOIN=<producer>` is the instrument. It refuses exactly the join that
producer owns and leaves every other one intact.

A join's price is its bytes, across a 32x range. Each arm below forces one of the 18 existing
joins off, at 100 iterations and 3 repeats, against a base of 18.40 ms (spread under 0.2 ms):

| producer's tensor | joins removed | ms |
|---:|---:|---:|
| 784 KiB (the stem) | 1 | 6.80 |
| 196 KiB | 1 | 0.57 - 1.10 |
| 98 KiB | 1 | 0.53 - 0.63 |
| 49 KiB | 1 | 0.20 - 0.33 |
| 24.5 KiB | 1 | 0.10 - 0.17 |

The 18 arms sum to 15.0 ms over 2278 KiB, about 6.6 us/KiB. With the kick turned off and
submits pinned at 31, the difference is pure host, and the arms sum to 17.25 ms over the same
bytes. A join is therefore paid in bytes moved, and the stem's is the single most valuable one
in the graph. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_net_gate bench 100`]

The per-layer profile covers 18.4 ms in 25 submits and 38 tasks. The single largest layer is
the 7×7 stem at 2.94 ms (19.4%), and the next nine run 0.53-1.02 ms each. Every convolution is
one output-channel tile and one task, so `oc` tiling is not what any of them spend. The two
MobileNets give the same reading.

### The three refusal classes

None of the three refusal classes is a property of the part, and all twelve refusals close.
ResNet-18 goes 18.4 -> 10.5 ms and 25 -> 11 submits on the same 38 tasks. The result is
byte-identical: the same logits, the same top-5 and the same TFLite distance. Each class needs
a different change, and none of them needs a register.

If every layer that reads a skip source can read a cube, the skip source can write one. The
rule that a producer some later layer reads must materialize is a statement about host
buffers, not about the part. It holds where the reader three layers on wants a row-major
tensor.

A resident handle owns its output surface, and nothing writes there until that handle runs
again. The surface therefore already outlives the whole inference, which is exactly the
lifetime a distant reader needs. Two readers of one surface are the same cube described twice,
not a second copy.

ResNet-18's identity blocks end in an add whose output is read twice. The next block's 3×3
convolution reads it, and so does that block's 1×1 downsample three layers later. Both are
ordinary convolutions. There are three such producers, 343 KiB.

A shared surface has a trap that the chain owns. **A cross-layer kick re-stamps every interior
layer's surface inside its verify bracket.** That is sound only because the next kick rewrites
the surface before anything reads it, and a shared surface is read first. Stamping a shared
surface replaces the layer's output with 0xA5. The outside reader then computes a full and
plausible surface from the sentinel, invisible until the next materialized layer.

The declaration `rocket_conv2d_int8_cube_shared_rk3576()` moves the stamp to the start of the
producer's next call, where cube-out already puts it. A handle writing into a caller's buffer
is already that case and needs no declaration.

Neither operand of an ADD is "the layer before", and that is not the placement rule either.
Concatenation buffers keyed on operand A's producer being the layer immediately before the add
fit MobileNetV2's shape. That keying is the exact reverse of a ResNet block that changes width.
There, operand B is the 1×1 downsample sitting immediately before the add, and operand A is the
3×3 leg past it.

The buffer is a dedicated allocation per add, and each slice has exactly one writer. The order
the two producers run in therefore does not matter. Two conditions must hold. Both producers
precede the add, which they do by construction, and every other reader of either producer can
read the slice back. Stated that way, the rule wires both families. ResNet-18 goes from 4
concatenation-wired adds to 8, and MobileNetV2 stays at 10.

A pool is a cube producer. The last two refusals are both the max pool, which feeds the next
convolution and a residual add three layers on. Neither closes until the PPU can leave its
output in place. The functions `rocket_pool_int8_cube_out_rk3576()`, `_cube_of_` and
`_cube_out_at_` are that side of the join.

The PPU's own surface stride is `round4(ow*oh)` rather than the plane. That is no bound on the
consumer. The CNA's DDR channel-group jump is a register the emitter fills, and the part honors
it at any value at or above the plane. The cube therefore carries it, and a conv consumer joins
at any plane.

**The destination stride is a different matter and is refused rather than worked around.** The
pooling program derives it from the output plane, and there is no decoded register to move it.
A slice at any other stride would be written at the wrong offsets. A plane whose element count
is already a multiple of four (56×56 is) needs nothing.

With those three changes, every adjacent pair of all three networks joins. ResNet-18 joins 27
of 30, MobileNetV1 28 of 28 and MobileNetV2 63 of 63. ResNet-18's other three index-adjacent
pairs are not dataflow edges at all, because its downsample reads across them. The two
MobileNets' walls do not move (5.0 and 6.9 ms). That confirms the earlier reading that their
last unjoined pair, the pool's output into the classifier, is worth nothing.

**A join and a run are different questions, and closing every join does not make a graph one
stream.** Even with all 27 joins in place, ResNet-18 runs in 11 submits under a run finder with
two rules that are not the part's. A finder that grows a run only while consecutive pairs are
linked strands a shortcut convolution reading three layers back. A finder that takes a pooling
layer as a break splits the stream, and a pool need not be a break.

The sections "Finding the runs is the library's" and "A pooling layer sits inside the stream"
above replace both rules. With them, the graph is one kick in one submit, 10.5 -> 9.2 ms.
Count the runs as well as the joins. A pair can be joined and still sit at a run boundary.

Each class is priced on its own, with all three flags otherwise on (`bench 100`):

| off | joins | submits | ms |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| shared surfaces (`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_MULTI=0`) | 24 + 8 far | 17 | 13.7-13.9 |
| concatenation buffers (`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_CAT=0`) | 14 + 3 far | 26 | 18.9-19.0 |
| cross-layer kick (`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_KICK=0`) | 27 + 11 far | 31 | 13.4-13.8 |
| none (everything on) | 27 + 11 far | 11 | **10.6** |

The per-pair cap is too low by 1.8x, and both missing terms are in the accounting. Priced per
refused pair at its own size, the twelve come to about 4.4 ms of 18.4, and the measurement is
7.9. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_net_gate`, 2026-08-02]

Two terms sit outside the refused-pair table. The closures also remove 11 non-adjacent links
carrying 1078 KiB, a distant reader's scatter, which no adjacent-pair row can hold. They also
merge runs, taking 25 submits to 11. The refusal table counts what a pair-shaped instrument
can see. **Read it as an ordering over the classes, not as a bound on the win.**
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_net_gate`, 2026-08-02]

Three facts below come from ResNet-18. Two are registers, and neither is reachable from a
MobileNet. The third is about how a harness reads a graph.

### The PPU's pad nibbles

`0x6040` packs four four-bit pads. **Bits [3:0] are the left pad and bits [7:4] the top**:
the leading pads are the low half, not the trailing ones.

Every pool in every vendor capture is padded symmetrically, and a symmetric pad cannot
distinguish this map from its transpose. A transposed map raises no error. With the pairs
swapped, an asymmetric pool computes a full, correctly sized, entirely plausible surface
that is wrong nearly everywhere. A classifier's first max pool has exactly that shape. It
is TFLite's SAME on an even plane at stride 2, or torchvision's explicit pad whose trailing
row no window reaches. So it is the first thing a real graph hits, and no per-op gate tested
it.

A one-dimensional sweep pins the map (`rk3576_pool_probe pad`). The sweep drives a single
row with a unit kernel on the other axis. It scores the part's line against the window grid
each candidate leading pad implies. The grid follows the nibble at [3:0] on x and [7:4] on
y, at pads 0, 1 and 2, on both axes. The `rk3576_pool_probe bound` walk runs max k3 s2 with
one leading pad and none trailing, over planes 16 to 112 and channel counts 16 to 128. With
the map above it is exact in all 40 cells, and with the pairs swapped every one of them is
wrong.

The trailing pads are the high pair, and nothing the emitter produces depends on them. The
emitter programs the window count explicitly in `PPU_OUT_WIDTH`/`_HEIGHT` and the span in
`PPU_IN_WIDTH`/`_HEIGHT`.

**Trap: give the pool the pad its last window consumes.** The descriptor's trailing pad can
legitimately be smaller than the pad the model declares. For example, torchvision's
stride-2 3×3 pads one row at each end of an even plane, and the last window never reaches
the trailing one. The geometry must agree with the consumed value.

### The direct path's drain deadline

There is no row-allowance law on the direct path: the wall is the driver's grace.

**A single convolution program whose DPU drain outlasts `dpu_grace_us` loses the tail of its
output surface, silently, and nothing faults.** The driver signals the job's fence while the
core is still writing. What the DPU has not yet written is the last rows of the last
output-channel group. So the caller reads a full, correctly sized surface whose corner is
wrong. This deadline is the whole of what reads as a CBUF "row allowance" that the planner
is over.

One module parameter decides it. At `dpu_grace_us = 500` (the shipping default), a
`112x112 k7 ic32` convolution at four output-channel groups is wrong at every plane height
from 45 rows up. At 750 every one of those heights is bit-exact, at the same shape, the same
program and the same CBUF `F`. Restoring 500 brings the failure back at the same heights
with the same signature. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]

The threshold sits between 500 and 750 us at four groups and at six alike. It is a fixed time
and not a function of the shape, which is what a deadline is and what no capacity bound can
be. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]

Every measured wall is the same amount of work, and no CBUF sum can be written down for
them. Each probe is one VALID-padded plane, laid out as exactly one task. The walk runs
`rk3576_conv_lib_gate rowmap` row by row, with three runs per height (618 heights):

| groups | oc | tallest exact plane | feature granules | MMACs |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 32 | never fails to 109, the reachable top | 6104 | 548 |
| 2 | 64 | 78 | 4368 | 766 |
| 3 | 96 | 56 | 3136 | 798 |
| 4 | 128 | 44 | 2464 | 808 |
| 5 | 160 | 36 | 2016 | 798 |
| 6 | 192 | 32 | 1792 | 830 |

The walls span 766-830 MMACs, an 8% band, while the feature footprint they sit at falls 2.4x
across the same rows. One output-channel group never fails at any reachable height. Its work
tops out at 548 MMACs, under the band. This is why the k7 cells recorded at one or two groups
all read as agreeing. And `feature + weights <= pool` cannot be fitted at all. One group
computes at a feature-plus-cube total of 6888 granules, where two groups fail at 5992.

#### The quantity the deadline is spent on

The deadline is spent on the program's execution, not on the surface it writes. The six rows
above cannot tell those two apart. They hold `ic` and `k` fixed, and there the MACs are
exactly proportional to the output elements. So a work law and an output-bytes law fit the
same table.

Five further cells break that proportionality, through the kernel, the plane width and `ic`.
They separate the two by factors, not by margins. The instrument is the same, with one VALID
task per height and three runs each. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]

| cell | ic | oc | iw | k | first wrong height | output there | work there |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `k7g2-112` | 32 | 64 | 112 | 7 | 79 | 495 KB | 777 MMAC |
| `k7g3-112` | 32 | 96 | 112 | 7 | 57 | 519 KB | 814 MMAC |
| `k7g4-112` | 32 | 128 | 112 | 7 | 45 | 529 KB | 830 MMAC |
| `k7g5-112` | 32 | 160 | 112 | 7 | 37 | 526 KB | 824 MMAC |
| `k7g6-112` | 32 | 192 | 112 | 7 | 33 | 550 KB | 862 MMAC |
| `k5g4-112` | 32 | 128 | 112 | 5 | 77 | 1009 KB | 807 MMAC |
| `k7g4-224` | 32 | 128 | 224 | 7 | 25 | 530 KB | 831 MMAC |
| `k7g4-56` | 32 | 128 | 56 | 7 | 60, dense from 89 | 346 KB, 531 KB | 542, 833 MMAC |
| `k7ic64-g2` | 64 | 64 | 112 | 7 | 34 | 190 KB | 596 MMAC |
| `k3g4-112` | 32 | 128 | 112 | 3 | none to 109 | 1507 KB exact | 434 MMAC |
| `k7g1-112` | 32 | 32 | 112 | 7 | none to 109 | 349 KB exact | 548 MMAC |

The output surface is not the quantity. Across the first failures it spans 190 KB to
1009 KB, a factor of 5.3, where the work spans 542-862 MMACs, a factor of 1.6. The
contradiction is direct rather than statistical. The `k3g4-112` cell writes 1507 KB with no
error at any reachable height, while `k7ic64-g2` is wrong at 190 KB, eight times less. At
three taps the MACs per output element fall 5.4x and the surface grows, and the wall goes
away.

Nor is it the output rows. The `k7g4-224` cell is wrong 3 of 3 at 19 output rows, and
`k7g4-56` is exact past 78. A row count fitted on the `112`-wide family, 38 output rows, is
wrong in both directions by about a factor of two. So the width moves the wall by the total,
not by the row count.

Work is a proxy for the program's execution time, and it is a good one only where the weight
traffic per output is fixed. The five `112`-wide k7 cells and `k7g4-224` land in 777-862
MMACs, and the dense region of `k7g4-56` starts at 833. The `k7ic64-g2` cell fails 27% below
that band, at 596 MMACs. It carries twice the resident weight slice per output-channel group
(1568 granules against 784). So each output element costs more DMA than a MAC count sees. The
explanation of the residual spread is [hypothesis], and what is measured is the ordering.

These cells ran at 786 MHz (see "The clock does not park"). At that rate, 800 MMACs in
500 us is about 2050 MACs per cycle, and two cores at that rate make the part's marketed
6 TOPS. So a one-task program's whole compute sits inside the grace. The part raises
`PC_DONE` when the program counter retires the descriptor, not when the pipeline behind it
has finished.

**Trap: the heights above a refusal read as exact.** The `k7ic64-g2` cell is wrong from 34
to 45 and then reports `0/3 wrong` from 46 up. That looks like a band with a clean upper
edge, exactly the depthwise rung signature. It is not one: those rows carry
`(3 refused or skipped)`, and the entry emitted no program for them. An untouched output BO
is not evidence about anything. Read the annotation, not the count.

The failure signature of `k7ic64-g2` changes shape at 37, and that is two mechanisms, not
one. Below 37 the wrong elements are a row tail of the last output-channel group, which is
what a marginal overrun looks like. From 37 up they are the whole surface of that group (rows
0-30 of 31). The planner's CBUF `F` moves from 0 to 1024 at the same height.

Removing the deadline separates the two. On a kernel that waits for the writing block, the
low heights read 0 of 3, against 1 and 3 of 3 under the deadline. Heights 37-45 stay wrong
at exactly the same element counts under both (104936 at 37, 108489 at 38, 111936 at 39).
That says the second mechanism is deterministic and owes nothing to timing. The library
refuses the heights from 37, because the slice cap's `F`-aware form does not cover them.

#### The residue below the refusal

The residue below the refusal is the deadline, and both its rate and its shape are wider
than 3 reps can see. The planner still honors `F=0` at heights 33 to 36 on `k7ic64-g2`.
There the plane needs 3696 to 4032 of 4096 granules, and the 98 KiB slice sits inside the
156 KiB `F=0` cap. These heights are wrong on the shipped module and bit-exact under either
of two independent removals of the deadline. The walk is `rowmap` with `MAP_REPS=10`, one
task per cell, with the planner choosing `F` [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]:

| arm | ih 33 | ih 34 | ih 35 | ih 36 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| shipped 1.5.0, `dpu_grace_us` 500 | 2 of 30 | 14 of 60 | 26 of 60 | 56 of 60 |
| shipped 1.5.0, `dpu_grace_us` 1000 | n/a | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 |
| the waiting kernel (1.6.0), its own default | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 |

The 500-vs-1000 comparison is interleaved, in three alternating passes on 2026-08-05. It
reads 40 of 90 wrong at 500 against 0 of 90 at 1000, and the failure returns every time the
grace does. The interleave matters because the rate at a fixed height wanders across
identical walks. Over six runs of the same command at the same grace, ih 35 reads 0, 1, 1,
6, 8 and 10 of 10. So a single walk's zero is not evidence, and neither is a single walk's
localization.

A walk of 3 reps localizes the failure to 34-36, and that range is an artifact of the rep
count. The failure reaches ih 33 too. At ih 35 the first walk on 2026-08-05 read 0 of 10, and
the fourth read 10 of 10.

The deadline has a severe shape, and it is the same drop with the cut landing earlier. It is
one mechanism on a continuum, and the extent is a readout of the cut time rather than a
property of the failure. One shape is the row tail of one output-channel group (hundreds to a
few thousand wrong elements, `chans 32-47`). The same cells sometimes return a surface wrong
across every row and all 64 channels, 150000 to 170000 of 203520 elements. Sweeping
`dpu_grace_us` as an instrument moves the extent smoothly from one to the other. The sweep is
`k7ic64-g2` at ih 33-36, `rowmap` with `MAP_REPS=10` and `MAP_SEQ=1`, every rep printed, 131
wrong reps [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]:

| `dpu_grace_us` | wrong reps | wrong elements per rep | channel span | row span |
|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| 500 | 11 of 40 | 1184 - 3808 | `c 32-47` | a 1-3 row suffix |
| 250 | 40 of 40 | 27798 - 68768 | `c 32-63` | from row 5-16 down |
| 100 | 40 of 40 | 84638 - 131008 | `c 0-63` | every row |
| 0 | 40 of 40 | 116364 - 155008 | `c 0-63` | every row |

Grace 0 at ih 36 reaches 155008 elements, inside the severe shape's own band. So the severe
shape at grace 500 is the tail of one distribution and not a second event. Of the 131 wrong
reps, 107 land between the two shapes, in a range a two-mechanism reading requires to be
empty. The severe shape did not occur once in 240 reps at grace 500 on 2026-08-05. At that
rarity, walks of 3 to 10 reps read the two ends as two separate events, as three sessions of
such walks did.

Every wrong element is a dropped write, never a wrong value. The library stamps each output
surface with `0xA5` before the submit (`rocket_rk3576_sentinel_on`, on by default). In 131 of
131 wrong reps, every wrong element still holds the stamp, and zero elements carry data. That
holds at every grace value, including 0, where the driver retires each job as early as it can.
So a lost drain costs a corner of the job's *own* surface. It does not leave the core writing
into an unrelated job's window.

That result is scoped to what the arm reaches: one process, sequential conv submits ~10 ms
apart, one geometry. It says nothing about a victim in another process, of another job type,
or in a graph submitting back to back.

The drop grows in the order `c 32-47` first, then `c 48-63`, then `c 0-31`, never the natural
channel order, at any grace value [HW sweep]. One reading takes that as the part's DDR write
order: the last channel group this geometry writes is 32-47 rather than the highest-numbered
one. That reading is [hypothesis], because nothing in this arm separates write order from
another cause of the same ordering.

**The surface's shape alone does not separate the slice cap from the deadline.** What
separates them is that the slice loses an integral number of trailing output-channel groups
*completely*: every row of `chans 32-63`, exactly 50% here. The deadline's loss is a suffix in
write order, at whatever depth the cut fell. A "whole surface" reading alone does not tell
them apart. The stamp does: the slice cap's loss is also unwritten, but it lands on group
boundaries.

### The weight slice and the feature allowance

The weight slice is charged against what the feature allowance leaves, and `F` is the other
half of that pool. The second mechanism at 37 is the resident weight slice overrunning the
CBUF pool once the feature allowance grows. It is not a dead `F=1024` rung. The slice cap
already models a graded output-channel-group loss, and that loss is the same bound met from
the other end of one shared budget.

**The emitter programs `4096+F` granules for the feature side, whatever the plane's actual
height is.** So every granule of `F` is a granule the weight path does not get. The cap table
above was measured at `F=0`, where the weight path has 3072 granules. At `F` it has `3072-F`,
and the usable part falls granule for granule with it.

The table covers two shapes, four `F` values and two group counts, with one task per cell
and `MAP_REPS=3`. The plane is held well inside the `F=0` budget, so that a rung the part
declines cannot matter:

| shape | groups | slice | `F` | usable | the part |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `112x112 k7 ic 64 oc 64` | 2 | 98 KiB | 0 | 156 KiB | exact |
| | | | 1024 | **92 KiB** | wrong, `chans 32-63`, 1 of 2 groups |
| | | | 2048 | 28 KiB | **no `PC_DONE` at all** |
| `112x112 k7 ic 32 oc 128` | 4 | 49 KiB | 0 | 144 KiB | exact |
| | | | 1024 | 80 KiB | exact |
| | | | 2048 | **16 KiB** | wrong, `chans 32-127`, 3 of 4 groups |

Both wrong cells are shapes the pool check accepts (6688 and 6928 granules against 7168).
They are wrong at *every* row of the groups they lose, which is exactly the slice cap's
recorded signature. The dead rung's signature differs: a full surface with a wrong row tail
past `4096/entries`.

Forcing the rungs refutes the rung reading. Forcing `F=1024` on the two-group cell is 3 of 3
wrong at all 30 plane heights from 7 to 36. That includes heights needing 784 granules of a
4096-granule budget, where a rung delivering only `4096` would be harmless. At every one,
`worst` is exactly half the surface. The same forced rung beside a 16-granule slice is exact
at 42 of 42 cells over 1, 2 and 3 output-channel groups. That also rules out the reading that
"programming an allowance larger than the plane is itself invalid".

The rung table records `F=2048` as delivering at every slice tried, and here it does not
deliver at all. The job raises no `PC_DONE`, the surface guard spends eight power cycles, and
the entry gives up with `-4`. The governing quantity is the slice against what `F` leaves,
not the rung.

Past the pool, the failure stops being quiet. That `-4` is the useful boundary. Inside the
pool, a slice that is over computes a plausible wrong surface, and past the pool the program
does not execute. So the pool check guards a real cliff, and this bound sits inside it.

#### Slope and reserve

The slope is 1:1 to the granule, and the reserve is what the group count buys. The sweep
reads the boundary directly rather than bracketing it. It uses a 4×2 plane where nothing
drains, and `k=1`, so the slice is `32*ic` and moves in 1 KiB steps. It forces `F` so that
it is the only thing that changes, and it reports the walk as a map. The table gives the last
exact slice, one task per cell:

| groups (`oc`) | `F=1024` | `F=2048` | the weight path holds | usable = hold - reserve |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 (64) | **96 KiB** (1536 gran) | **32 KiB** (512 gran) | 2048 / 1024 granules | reserve **512** granules |
| 3 (96) | **85 KiB** (1360 gran) | **21 KiB** (336 gran) | | reserve **688** |
| 4 (128) | **80 KiB** (1280 gran) | **16 KiB** (256 gran) | | reserve **768** |

The weight path holds `3072-F` granules, and the reserve is constant in `F` at every group
count. It is 512, 688 and 768 granules, identical at both allowances. So the slope is 1:1 to
the granule at three group counts. What the group count costs is a fixed reserve, not a
scaling. Each boundary is deterministic: 3 of 3 wrong at the first KiB over and 0 of 3 at the
last KiB under. [HW sweep, `rk3576_conv_lib_gate slicemap`]

Those reserves put the `F=0` intercepts at or above the cap table's values:

| groups | intercept from the reserve | cap table | over-charge |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2 | 160 KiB | 156 KiB | 4 KiB |
| 3 | 149 KiB | 148 KiB | 1 KiB |
| 4 | 144 KiB | 144 KiB | 0 KiB |

The two are consistent, because the cap table's sweep sampled six slices (144, 145, 148,
152, 156 and 162). It took the last measured-good sample rather than the boundary. The
shipped numbers over-charge and never under-charge, at six directly measured cells. They are not raised to
the implied intercepts, because `F=0` is the one point this instrument cannot reach. The
output-channel tile splits a conv above 156 KiB, so the conv never runs as two groups there.
Raising a cap on an extrapolation is the direction that computes a wrong surface.

**A slice that is an exact multiple of 512 granules (32 KiB) computes anyway**, far above the
cap. At `F=1024`, both 96 KiB and 128 KiB are exact at all three group counts, while every
neighboring KiB is wrong. At `F=2048`, so are 64 KiB (2 groups) and 32 KiB (3 groups). A
multiple of 16 KiB that is not one of 32 does not escape: 112 KiB is wrong. This rules out two
readings: 96 KiB is neither the pool edge (128 KiB is at `F=1024`) nor a power-of-two `ic`
(3072). [HW sweep]

The bound therefore has an alignment term that nothing models. The library deliberately does
not model it, since modeling it can only make the library accept more. The mechanism is
[hypothesis]: the weight path stages the slice in 32 KiB units, and a partial trailing unit
is what costs the trailing output-channel group.

The escape is a property of the slice's byte count, not of the kernel or the plane the sweep
was taken at. The walks below move both conditions that every recorded cell shared. They run
at `F=1024` and `oc=128`, where the cap is 80 KiB and 96 and 128 KiB are both well above it:

| walk | cap | 81-95 KiB | **96 KiB** | 97-127 KiB | **128 KiB** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `k=1`, 4×2 plane (the record) | 80 KiB | 1 group lost to 85, 2 from 86 | **0 of 3** | 3 groups | **0 of 3** |
| `k=2`, 4×2 plane | 80 KiB | 1 at 84, 2 at 88 and 92 | **0 of 20** | 3 (20 of 20 each) | **0 of 20** |
| `k=1`, 4×19 plane | 80 KiB | 1 to 85, 2 from 86 | **0 of 10** | 3 to 107, **108-127 do not complete** | **0 of 10** |

For the same 96 KiB, `ic` is 3072 at `k=1` and 768 at `k=2`. So nothing about the cube
survives as an explanation. The graded group loss also steps at the same slice sizes on both
lattices. The 4×19 plane consumes 3040-4864 granules of the forced 5120 allowance, against
160-256 for the 4×2 plane. It moves neither the cap nor the escape. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`rk3576_conv_lib_gate slicemap` with `ROCKET_LG_SLICE_K`]

These walks hold one group count and one allowance. So the invariance is measured at one of
each, where the slope table has three group counts and two allowances. And `k=4`, the only
other kernel that reaches the lattice below the pool, was not tried.

The tall plane does move the quiet-to-cliff boundary. At 4×2 every cell to 128 KiB computes a
wrong surface. At 4×19 the entry returns `-4` with no completion at all from 108 to 127 KiB.
So the non-execution cliff is a function of the plane and the slice together. The graded
loss and the escape are functions of the slice alone.

At 4×19, 128 KiB then runs and is exact. The escape jumps clean over a band where the program
does not execute. No reading of the escape as "a little more headroom" survives that.

Arithmetic fixes which kernels can ask this question at all. The slice is `32*ic*k*k`, and
the direct path requires `ic` to be a multiple of 32. So the reachable slices are exactly the
multiples of `k*k` KiB. A 32 KiB multiple therefore needs `k` in {1, 2, 4}. With `k=3` the
first one is 288 KiB, and with `k=5` it is 800 KiB. Both are far past the 128 KiB the pool
leaves at `F=1024`, so an arm that repeats the walk at `k=3` cannot be built.

The library charges the slice in both places that read the cap. The plan refuses, and
`rocket_rk3576_max_task_rows()` skips a rung whose F-aware cap does not cover the slice.
Without that second check, the function returns 45 input rows for `112x112 k7 ic 64 oc 64`.
That is `F=1024`'s window and the whole failing band. So every task the row planner cuts
there is a full surface with its trailing group wrong. With the check, it returns 36, and
heights 37-45 refuse.

With both checks in place, `claimplan` reports 160 shapes, 0 accept-a-refusal and 0
over-refusal.

The bound is invisible in the corpus. No model reaches the geometry, as measured by the five
net gates keeping their structure across the change. Under the deadline, the bound also sits
inside the deadline's own failure region.

Why no model reaches it is [hypothesis], not enumerated layer by layer. Reaching the bound
needs a slice near the cap *and* a plane large enough to need a rung. A 92 KiB slice is
`ic*kh*kw >= 2944`, and a layer carrying that much `ic` tends to have a plane small enough to
sit at `F=0`. So the two requirements look anti-correlated. The instrument is
`rk3576_conv_lib_gate rowmap`'s `ROCKET_LG_MAP_F`, and the cells are `k7ic64-g2` and
`k7g4-112`.

The shipped planner matches the part. At `dpu_grace_us = 750`,
`rk3576_conv_lib_gate rowbound` puts the two in exact agreement on 23 of 23 geometries. They
include `56x56 k7 ic 64 oc 64`, the one geometry recorded as over and deliberately left so.
It reads 58 rows at 500 and the planner's own 91 at 750. At 500 it is the only disagreement,
and the deadline is its cause.

**The corpus is protected from the deadline by an accident.** The row planner windows a task
to fit the CBUF allowance. On the shapes real models carry, that window keeps every task's
drain under 500 us. Nothing in the five classifiers, the two detectors or the per-axis
ResNet-18 reaches the deadline. The probe knob `ROCKET_RK3576_ROW_CAP_PROBE` is what emits
windows past it. So the class is unreachable in the corpus and entirely reachable by a caller
with a wider plane or a deeper output-channel count.

**Trap: the edge moves between runs, so a bisection over it reports a bound and not a law.**
At two groups, the boundary height is wrong on every run inside a long sequential walk and
clean in a short one. The binary and the shape are the same. Read as a capacity bound, the
edge looks like a ceiling that falls with the group count. A movement of 1-4 rows between
runs is this same effect.

Read the edge as a rate, with `ROCKET_LG_MAP_REPS`. Below the height where the failure is 3
of 3, there is a region where it is 1 of 3. The `k7g4-56` cell is wrong once at 60 and once
at 68, then clean to 84. The `k7g4-224` cell is wrong once at 19, then clean to 24.

The edge moves with the host's timing, not the part's [hypothesis]. The NPU clock holds one
rate for a whole boot, 786 MHz for these runs, and does not park. There is no NPU devfreq
node (only the GPU has one), so nothing on the part's side of this varies. What does vary is
when a 50 us poll tick lands relative to `PC_DONE`, and how late the hrtimer callback runs
under load. Both move the start of the grace window by a poll period on a 500 us budget.

A reading in which a slower clock lengthens the drain contradicts the fixed clock recorded in
this file. It sends the next person to the wrong register.

**Trap: `dpu_grace_us` is a fitted number in a shipping path, and 0022 already removed the
other one.** That patch makes a job's wait depend on the hardware's own account of the kick
rather than on a deadline. It does so only for a kick of more than one task. A job of one
task programs `TASK_NUMBER = 1`, where both `PC_TASK_STATUS` counters always read zero. So
`PC_DONE` alone signals it, and the grace bounds the drain after it. The same is true of the
last program of any kick.

Raising the grace is a workaround, not the fix: how long one program can take is not a
property of the driver's timing.

#### The deadline under the write guard

A task past the deadline by a wide enough margin writes nothing the guard can see. The
whole-surface write guard cannot tell that from a poisoned submit, because both look empty.
It spends eight power cycles on each before the entry returns `ROCKET_E_DEVICE` and the
caller falls back to the host. **A deterministic failure at the same row task every time is
the plan or the deadline, not the poisoning hazard.**

Past a smaller margin, the surface is written in full and is wrong. There are 0
dropped-to-zero elements in 12 of 12 failing cells, and every wrong element carries a
plausible value. Both readings are the same mechanism at different distances past the same
fence.

#### Output-channel group loss at a large weight slice

The output-channel group loss at a large weight slice is a separate bound. The cap
`r76_weight_slice_cap()` is measured on a 4×2 plane, where the drain is nowhere near the
grace. It is about the slice rather than the window. At four output-channel groups, a
resident slice of 144 KiB leaves every group exact, 145 KiB loses one, 152 KiB loses two, and
162 KiB leaves one. That table holds. A large-window failure at a small slice, 49 KiB against
that 144 KiB cap, is not the same rule met from the other end.

#### On the DEPTHWISE path the two low CBUF rungs do not deliver, and the failure is a band

The allowance is a ladder. F takes one of five measured values, and the planner programs the
smallest rung whose `4096+F` granules cover the task's window. A rung the part does not honor
delivers the F=0 budget instead. The surface is then exact below the rung's reach, **wrong
across the band of windows that select it, and exact again above**. The failure is not
monotone in the window height. So a probe that bisects the window cannot see it, and it
reports whichever edge it walks into.

The walk runs one task per height at 160×160 ic = oc = 32 k3 depthwise, 80 granules a row
(`rk3576_conv_lib_gate rowmap`). The planner chooses F:

| plane rows | granules needed | planner F | surface |
|---:|---:|---:|---|
| 45-51 | 3600-4080 | 0 | exact |
| **52-54** | **4160-4320** | **256** | **wrong** |
| **55-57** | **4400-4560** | **512** | **wrong** |
| 58-64 | 4640-5120 | 1024 | exact |
| 65-76 | 5200-6080 | 2048 | exact |

On the direct path, the same walk is exact at every height from 49 through 60. It uses the
same plane, the same channels and the same entries per row. In the band, the wrong elements
are exactly the output rows past `4096/entries`. At a 52-row plane that is one row: the F=0
budget's 51 rows staged under a programmed 4352.

Forcing each rung under one fixed window shows that no threshold on the depthwise weight
footprint fits. The table uses one window that F=0 does not buy, with `ROCKET_RK3576_CBUF_F`
forcing each rung beneath it:

| path | kernel | oc | resident footprint | F=256 / F=512 |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| direct | 1×1 | 32 | 1024 B = 16 granules | deliver |
| direct | 1×1 | 64 | 2048 B = 32 granules | fall back to 4096 |
| depthwise | 1×1 | 32 | 64 B = 1 granule | deliver |
| depthwise | 1×1 | 256 | 512 B = 8 granules | deliver |
| depthwise | 1×1 | 1024 | 2048 B = 32 granules | deliver |
| depthwise | 2×2 | 32 | 256 B = 4 granules | fall back to 4096 |
| depthwise | 3×3 | 32 | 576 B = 9 granules | fall back to 4096 |
| depthwise | 5×5 | 32 | 1600 B = 25 granules | fall back to 4096 |

On the depthwise path, 4 granules is dead where 32 is live, so a threshold on that footprint
is refuted in both directions. What survives the eight cells is the tap count. Every
single-tap cell delivers, at three channel counts spanning 32x, and every multi-tap one falls
back. The 2×2 cell refutes a 64-channel-group footprint against the direct path's 16-granule
threshold (which fits k1, k3 and k5). F=1024 and F=2048 deliver at every footprint tried on
both paths.

So **the library declines the low rungs on the depthwise path** rather than gating them on a
fitted quantity. The refusal costs nothing measurable. The fallback rung is strictly larger,
always live, and needs no extra submit. Every window and task count in the ten `rowbound`
depthwise cells is unchanged, and only the emitted F moves. An unhonored rung, in contrast,
costs a full, correctly sized surface with wrong rows and nothing to fault on.

Two cells in practice select a low rung. They are EfficientDet-Lite0's `blocks_0` (160×160,
k3 s1, three windows of 55, 56 and 53 rows, all selecting F=512) and the same shape at
stride two. All ten depthwise `rowbound` cells agree with the planner. The `dw-k5-s1-160`
cell does not refute a capacity bound. It needs 4640 granules, which lands it one rung
higher, so it computes *because* it asks for more.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rk3576_conv_lib_gate rowmap`]

**Trap: "the layer is bit-exact" is a claim about the shapes the cells drive.** Six per-op
cells measured this datapath at zero counts of distance from an exact per-axis requant. The
model's own layer was wrong on 28 of its 32 live channels, because no cell was at the model's
plane.

The instrument that finds this compares every delegated partition's output against CPU
TFLite on the model's own weights and input. That comparison is a distance rather than a
pass. Under `experimental_preserve_all_tensors`, the runtime gives every claimed node its own
partition. So the same run is also the layout-or-arithmetic discriminator, with no joins and
no cross-layer kick in play. Set that flag on both arms. Without it, the arena reuses a
partition output's bytes and the control reports hundreds of counts.

### Operand producers in a graph

Neither operand of a graph layer is "the layer before". A feed-forward graph's operand A is
always the previous layer's output, and a residual graph's is not. MobileNetV2 reads a skip
three to five layers back on operand B, and the main line from the layer before. A ResNet
block that changes width reads them the other way round. Operand B is the 1×1 downsample
immediately before the add, and operand A is the 3×3 leg past it. That downsample reads the
block input again three layers later, which is a distant read on a convolution's first
operand.

A harness can give a private buffer only to "some add's `src2`" and thread operand A through
a ping-pong. Such a harness feeds three of ResNet-18's layers the wrong tensor. It does not
fault: each layer computes a full, correctly sized, plausible surface, and the graph returns
a confident label for an unrelated class.

Only the chain pass can see it. The oracle hands every layer TFLite's golden input. So it
scored all 32 layers exact, and the per-layer table was clean while the label was garbage.
The test is the distance, not the operand. Any producer read by anything other than the layer
immediately after it needs a buffer of its own. Both operands resolve by looking up their
producer rather than by threading.

**Trap: a wiring rule that is applied per branch will be applied to only some of the
branches.** The same
harness resolved operand A by producer on its pooling and add branch, and by position on its
convolution branch. The three affected layers are on the convolution branch, and the two
branches were four lines apart. So the defect survived in the per-axis pass after the chain
pass was fixed. There it presented as an unrelated top-5 that read like an accuracy result.

The graph property that decides whether this matters is countable up front. The count comes
from `rk3576_net_gate hostchain`, which reports how many layers take operand A from a
producer that is not the layer before. It is 0 for MobileNetV1 and 0 for MobileNetV2, against
3 convolutions and 3 other layers for ResNet-18. On the two MobileNets the two resolutions
are the same function. So every arm of every pass agrees for free, and no gate there can fail.
A wiring defect therefore passes every gate on both MobileNets, and it reached ResNet-18
twice.

### Per-axis requant on ResNet-18

ResNet-18 re-quantized per output channel returns TFLite's own top-1 on this part, like both
MobileNets. All 31 compute layers are within one count of an exact per-output-channel
requant, at spreads to 42x, and only the tail of the top-5 reorders. An unrelated class at
the output comes from the operand wiring above, not from the requant.

Nothing the per-layer table holds separates the two candidates. A layer fed the wrong tensor
is scored against a reference built from that same wrong tensor, and it reports exact. So
"the requant costs the label" and "some layer reads the wrong producer" produce an identical
table. The discriminator is the graph run three ways on the CPU
(`rk3576_net_gate hostchain`, no device required). There, no part is to blame, and the
operand resolution is the only variable:

| arm | ResNet-18 top-1 | TFLite's top-1 falls to |
|---|---|---|
| per-tensor, operands by producer, the control | `suit`, TFLite's own | n/a |
| per-axis, operands by producer | `bulletproof vest`, TFLite's own **number 2** | 2 counts behind |
| per-axis, operand A threaded | `nematode`, outside TFLite's top-5 | **120 counts** behind |

The control arm makes the other two readable. It is the arithmetic every layer of the chain
pass is bit-exact against, so it must return TFLite's top-1, and it does. Two orders of
