the kernel. At one granule total and one kernel, the same shape is exact at `ic` 32 and wrong at
`ic` 64 and 128. The detail is in [rk3576-regcmd.md](rk3576-regcmd.md).

The planner is keyed on the footprint, and every cell of the map is bit-exact with it.
`regcmd_rk3576_gate`'s sixteen planner cells and `rk3576_conv_lib_gate`'s 244 are unmoved.
**That footprint does not carry to the depthwise path**, where 4 granules is dead, 32 is live
and the low rungs are declined outright. See "On the DEPTHWISE path the two low CBUF rungs do
not deliver".

The probe's own control located the finding. `k1x1` is the arm that must succeed, because it is
where the rungs were measured to deliver. It failed, so the finding concerns the control and not
the `k1xN` cells the probe was written for. A probe whose must-succeed arm fails must become a
map before it becomes a verdict. Here the map took three walks:

- The F=0 boundary, walked row by row
- The same granule total, reached through different (row size, row count) factorizations
- The same planes forced under the boundary, as the arm that must be exact

## What the wall is made of, once the graph work is collected

This section measures the graph with every lever above in place, and the earlier readings
describe graphs without them. The operating point is `bench 100` with resident weights, cube
layout and cross-layer kicks, under `ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1`. That profile covers the chain
as well as the per-call path, so a kick is not a single opaque number.

Every graph is one chain call, so the profile covers the whole wall rather than a sample of it.
The accounting is therefore exact rather than approximate:

| | ms | submits | tasks | submit + wait | verify | stamp | feature scatter | de-scatter |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 5.0 | 1 | 40 | 2.36 (47%) | 2.04 (40%) | 0.03 | 0.61 (12%) | 0.01 |
| MobileNetV2 | 6.6 | 1 | 77 | 2.88 (43%) | 2.93 (44%) | 0.26 (4%) | 0.61 (9%) | 0.01 |
| ResNet-18 | 6.6 | 1 | 33 | 4.38 (66%) | 1.32 (20%) | 0.78 (12%) | 0.17 (3%) | 0.02 |
| Inception V1 | 7.1 | 1 | 72 | 4.08 (58%) | 1.71 (24%) | 1.12 (16%) | 0.09 (1%) | 0.01 |

The write guard is `verify` + `stamp`. A `ROCKET_RK3576_I32_SENTINEL=0` A/B confirms it
independently:

| | `verify` + `stamp` | `I32_SENTINEL=0` A/B |
|---|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 2.07 ms | 1.9 ms |
| MobileNetV2 | 3.19 | 3.0 |
| ResNet-18 | 2.10 | 2.1 |
| Inception V1 | 2.83 | 2.8 |

The table has no `plan+gen` column. A chain generates its whole stream once, at construction,
so a graph that is one kick does no per-inference regcmd work at all. Inception V3 is not in
the table because it is not one chain call. It is 18 kicks and 21 per-layer convolution calls,
beside 16 pooling ones. Its own accounting is under "The pooling path's read interval is three
things".

These graphs are not host-bound. The reading "The submit is 1-16% of a layer against a host
share of 84-99%" was measured on a 40-submit graph. That graph transposed at every layer
boundary and repacked its weights every inference. Residency, the cube layout and the kick
remove all three of those terms. What is left is dominated by the two terms that were never
host arithmetic. The host operand packing is 6-12% of the wall: one feature scatter per graph,
at the stem, because every other layer reads a cube.

The write guard is the second term on every graph, and on MobileNetV2 it is the first, at
3.0 ms of 6.7 against the submit's 3.0. It is a `PREP_BO`/`FINI_BO` pair per surface per call,
and both halves sync the whole BO. Its cost is therefore proportional to bytes, and independent of
how few bytes the check reads.

The guard's cheaper form, `ROCKET_RK3576_GUARD_PER_KICK=1`, asks one surface per kick rather
than every layer's. It reaches exactly as much of the guard as the graph keeps inside kicks.
That is a property of the run structure, not of the mechanism. With ResNet-18 in eleven
submits, the flag collected the whole guard on both MobileNets, each already one kick. On
ResNet-18 it collected only 65%, 10.6 -> 8.9 against a guard-off floor of 8.0. Seven of
ResNet-18's submits sat outside any kick, and each paid its own per-call bracket.

With every graph in one kick the flag has no shortfall. All four land on the guard-off floor
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `bench 100`, 2026-08-02]:

| | default | `GUARD_PER_KICK=1` | `I32_SENTINEL=0` floor | guard, of the default |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 4.8 | **3.0** | 3.0 | 1.8 ms of 4.8 (38%) |
| MobileNetV2 | 6.8 | **3.5** | 3.5 | 3.3 of 6.8 (49%) |
| ResNet-18 | 9.2 | **6.5** | 6.4 | 2.8 of 9.2 (30%) |
| Inception V1 | 10.8 | **6.2** | 6.2 | 4.6 of 10.8 (43%) |

So the guard is the largest single term on three of the four. The cheaper form's coverage is
not network-dependent.

What the two forms cover is a correctness question, not a performance one. The reduced guard
catches the whole-submit poisoning (0 of 144 poisoned inferences wrong under either form). By
construction, it cannot see a single program dying mid-stream. Which one ships is the
maintainer's call.

A ratio measured on one run structure goes stale when the structure changes, even with the flag
untouched. "The flag collects 65% on ResNet-18" is a correct measurement of the eleven-submit
graph, and it does not describe the one-kick graph.

The stamp column scales with the residual topology, the placed slices and the pooling layers,
not with the layer count. In the steady state a surface carries its sentinel from the previous
call's verify bracket and costs nothing. A surface the chain does not rewrite before someone
reads it cannot be re-stamped there. It pays a second bracket at the start of the next call
instead. Three kinds of surface pay it: a placed slice of a concatenation buffer, a declared
shared surface, and a pool. A pool's handle keeps no flag for the chain to clear.

| | what pays the second bracket | stamp |
|---|---|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | no adds at all | 0.01 ms |
| MobileNetV2 | ten adds | 0.39 |
| ResNet-18 | eight adds plus three shared skip sources plus two pools | 1.16 |
| Inception V1 | 37 placed producers plus thirteen pools | **2.07** |

Inception V1's is the largest stamp of the four. It is the reason wiring its concatenations
gave 3.26 ms of the lever back to the guard.

The submit column is a round trip plus the execution inside it, since the caller waits on the
fence through the last layer's surface. A marginal submit, priced from the A/Bs that add or
remove one, costs between 0.145 and 0.25 ms depending on the surface it drains. ResNet-18's stem
is six row tasks in one submit at 2.13 ms. Setting `ROCKET_RK3576_BATCH_TASKS=0` makes it six
submits at 3.39 ms, 0.25 ms each on a task writing a 112×112×64 surface. Over twenty small
layers a submit costs 0.145.

A submit also costs less as a graph runs out of submits. Taking ResNet-18 from 11 submits to 1
is 0.13 ms each, and the MobileNets' last two are 0.05-0.10. Re-measure it against the graph in
front of you rather than scaling one of these figures. The next section splits that column.

### The submit bucket's composition

The submit bucket is the part running programs, and the rest of it is 1-4%. At one kick per
graph the submit column is 61-81% of every wall. That makes it the only term above 20%, and the
one any further performance claim must be quoted against. It is a single interval covering the
ioctl, the kick's execution and the completion wait. Userspace cannot separate those three, so
a share of the interval is not a cap until something says which of the three it is.

Two instruments split it. The chain's own profile splits its interval into three terms: the
submit ioctl, the fence wait (a `PREP_BO` with a timeout on the last layer's surface), and the
`FINI_BO` that hands the surface back. The second instrument is
`rocket-userspace/tools/rk3576-job-timing-debug.patch`, a debug module rather than a series
patch. It adds five microsecond accumulators to the driver's polled completion path, exposed as
writable module parameters. They split the wait at the events only the driver can see:

- Scheduler wakeup: push to `.run_job` entry
- Prologue: `.run_job` to `OPERATION_ENABLE`, carrying `pm_runtime_get_sync`, the IOMMU attach
  test and the first task's register programming
- Execution: the kick to the poll that sees every task retired
- Drain: that poll to the writing block's own completion
- Signal: that completion to `dma_fence_signal`

The table is per inference, at `bench 100` with the guard narrowed, resident weights and cube
layout, on the 0023 module plus the instrument. The driver's terms are `bench(200) - bench(100)`
over 100, so the gate's fixed setup jobs cancel [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-02]:

| | submit bucket | ioctl | wakeup | prologue | **execution** | drain | signal | `FINI_BO` |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 2.34 ms | 0.057 | 0.012 | 0.003 | **2.252** | 0.001 | 0.008 | 0.004 |
| MobileNetV2 | 2.86 | 0.134 | 0.017 | 0.002 | **2.698** | 0.000 | 0.007 | 0.004 |
| ResNet-18 | 5.84 | 0.055 | 0.014 | 0.002 | **5.752** | 0.000 | 0.008 | 0.004 |
| Inception V1 | 5.56 | 0.107 | 0.018 | 0.003 | **5.450** | 0.001 | 0.008 | 0.004 |

The accounting closes to under 1%. The terms sum to the measured bucket, and the driver's
independent `push -> fence signal` total reproduces the caller's own fence wait to within tens
of microseconds:

| | terms sum | measured bucket | driver `push -> fence signal` | caller's fence wait |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 2.34 ms | 2.34 ms | 2.21 ms | 2.28 ms |
| MobileNetV2 | 2.86 | 2.86 | 2.73 | 2.72 |
| ResNet-18 | 5.84 | 5.84 | 5.78 | 5.78 |
| Inception V1 | 5.59 | 5.56 | 5.48 | 5.45 |

So this is a decomposition rather than a residual: nothing is attributed to what was not
measured.

Everything that is not the part running programs is 0.08-0.17 ms, 1.4-5.8% of the bucket and
1.1-3.7% of the wall. The submit ioctl is the largest of those terms. It scales with the BO
count rather than the work (~0.7 us per BO: 83 BOs on ResNet-18 for 0.055 ms, 182 on
MobileNetV2 for 0.134). The scheduler wakeup is 12-18 us, the prologue 2-3, the fence signal
about 8, the `FINI_BO` 4. **This bucket has no dispatch lever left.** A proposal against it
must make the part do less work, or do it faster.

**The ~340 us per-submit floor does not appear as a fixed cost inside a one-kick graph.** That
figure belongs to a one-task job, where the execution is nil and the number is the drain plus
the poll's quantization. A kick pays 0.08-0.17 ms instead.

The `drain` column shows why. The `drain0` case is a job whose completion bit was already up on
the tick that first saw the kick over. It fires on every job of all four graphs, so an interior
program's drain overlaps the next program's execution. The last program's drain finishes inside
the poll interval that detects the kick being over.

The drain is a term of a per-layer graph, not of a chained one. Run the same networks with
`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_KICK=0` and it is 0.20-3.17 ms per inference across 29-72 submits.

The poll period is worth at most one period per inference, and it is not a lever. Moving
`poll_interval_us` 50 -> 10 takes MobileNetV1's execution 1764 -> 1740 us. That is 24 us, half
a period, exactly the mean quantization. Moving it 50 -> 200 costs 145, and neither change moves
a wall outside run-to-run spread. The execution column is therefore an upper bound on the
part's own time, by up to one poll period. At 25 us against 2.2-5.8 ms, that changes nothing.

The NPU clock was 786 MHz for this table, and it does not park. The clocks `aclk_rknn0`,
`aclk_rknn1`, `aclk_rknn_cbuf` and `clk_rknn_dsu0` all read 786431998 Hz idle and under load
alike, with no devfreq node. The RK3576 therefore has no analogue of the RK3588's 200 MHz boot
park to undo.

#### The stem's share of the execution

The execution is front-loaded: the stem is a third of it. The per-layer arm
(`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_KICK=0`) shows which program the execution is spent on. In that arm each
layer prints its own profile, with its feature scatter in a separate column from its submit.
The driver's counters give ~0.05 ms of per-job non-execution for that arm. Subtracting it
leaves a layer's submit column as its execution plus its own drain [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`bench 20`]:

| | stem | its submit | its execution | next layers | graph's execution | stem's share |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | `ic=3 oc=32 224x224 k3 s2` | 0.50 ms | ~0.44 | 0.26-0.47 | 2.25 | **20%** |
| ResNet-18 | `ic=3 oc=64 224x224 k7 s2` | 2.11 | ~2.05 | 0.32-0.39 | 5.75 | **36%** |

Past the stem the layers are flat, at 0.15-0.35 ms of execution each across 28-30 of them.
They sum with the stem to the kick arm's total (30 x ~0.12 + 2.05 = 5.65 against 5.75
measured), so the accounting closes here too. One program of 29 to 81 is a third of
ResNet-18's and Inception V1's whole execution. It is a three-channel image on a datapath whose
int8 direct MAC group is 32 channels wide. The part contracts 32 where 3 carry data. Whether
that is reducible is a question about the encoding, not about the host or the driver: is there
a narrower int8 program?

The one narrower encoding that exists is the packed-image path. It reaches both 7×7 stem
geometries, computes bit-exactly there, and costs 7.2x less. The next section is that
measurement. The path requires a non-zero left pad rather than refusing one, which is why
TFLite's SAME stems are the ones it cannot take.

A chained kick does not make the part compute faster. It takes the per-job drain and the poll
quantization off the critical path. The only quantity the two arms measure the same way is
`execution + drain`. A one-task job's `exec` ends at `PC_DONE` and its drain is separate, while
a kick absorbs interior drains into `exec`. On that quantity ResNet-18 is 6.62 ms per inference
per-layer against 5.75 chained. The 0.87 ms difference over 30 extra submits is 29 us each,
which is the poll quantization.

The per-layer arm's drain is 101 us a job on ResNet-18 and 18 on MobileNetV1. It tracks the
bytes each program writes, as the DPU completion's own 82-343 us range implies.

With ResNet-18 in eleven submits, the stem was the largest single layer of any of the three,
at 29% of ResNet-18. It took 3.08 ms: 2.13 submit and wait, 0.62 the image scatter, and 0.34
plan+gen on a resident handle. Inside the kick the stem has no bucket of its own. Its
arithmetic is the same, and it is the dominant program in that stream.

The stem runs on the direct datapath at the model's own three input channels. The int8 direct
cube is a 32-channel MAC group at every count. So the part contracts 32 channels where 3 carry
data, and the program does about 10.6x the MACs the layer needs.

### The execution cost model

The execution is the programmed MAC count, and the feature DMA is 8% of it. A convolution's
execution has two candidate terms, and a stem is where they are hardest to tell apart. The
direct int8 datapath contracts a 32-channel MAC group at any input-channel count. It also
streams a 32-channel cube through the CBUF, so a narrower encoding would shrink both. Crossing
the two axes separates them, where sweeping either alone cannot (`tests/rk3576_stem_cost.c`,
resident weights, driver `stat_exec_us` via the job-timing debug module,
`bench(2N) - bench(N)`) [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-02]:

| arm | tasks | programmed MMAC | feature KiB read | execution |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **A** `ic=3 oc=64 224x224 s2`, k=1 | 5 | 25.7 | 1533 | 0.301 ms |
| k=3 | 5 | 231.2 | 1596 | 0.452 |
| k=5 | 5 | 642.3 | 1652 | 1.003 |
| k=7 | 6 | 1258.8 | 1743 | 1.802 |
| **B** same MACs, 4.2x fewer bytes: `112x112 k7 s1` | 2 | 1258.8 | 413 | **1.651** |
| **C** k7 `oc=16` (programs a 32-channel group) | 5 | 629.4 | 1708 | 0.953 |
| k7 `oc=32` | 5 | 629.4 | 1708 | 0.952 |
| **D** `k3 oc=32 ic=32` | 5 | 115.6 | 1596 | 0.302 |
| `ic=64` | 9 | 231.2 | 3248 | 0.602 |
| `ic=128` | 19 | 462.4 | 6776 | 1.249 |

Every arm is bit-exact against a CPU model of the part's own arithmetic. Group A holds the
feature cube constant and moves the MAC count 49x. A pure feature-DMA bound predicts a flat
column, and a pure MAC bound predicts 49x. The measured column moves 6.0x, which is a two-term
sum. Least squares on group A gives:

> **execution = 0.218 ms + 1.246e-3 ms per programmed MMAC**: 1.246 ps/MAC, **0.803 TMAC/s**

The fit reproduces the independent output-channel sweep to 0.05 ms, and the two low
input-channel arms to 0.06 and 0.10. Group B prices a byte directly. It holds the MAC count
identical and cuts the feature read 4.2x, and the execution goes 1.802 -> 1.651 ms. The feature
DMA is therefore 0.151 ms of the stem's execution, 8.4%.

The one arm the model misses is `ic=128`, by +0.46 ms. That arm's row allowance collapses to 19
tasks re-reading 6776 KiB. The byte term is small at a stem and grows with the task count, not
with the plane.

In group C, `oc=16` and `oc=32` cost the same to three decimal places. The direct cube groups
output channels by 32, and both program one group. The MAC count that predicts execution is the
programmed one throughout: `round32(oc)`, `round32(ic)`, and every tap of the kernel. That is
why a 3-channel stem is expensive.

#### The packed-image encoding at the stem

The packed-image encoding is 7.2x, and it is 8x fewer MACs. This is a measurement, not an
extrapolation. The packed-image first conv interleaves four lanes per pixel instead of
contracting a 32-lane group. At the same geometry it therefore programs 8.0x fewer MACs, and it
runs bit-exactly at both stem geometries [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]:

| `ic=3 224x224 s2`, in_zp 0 | programmed MMAC | exec | drain | exec+drain | push -> fence signal |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| k7 `oc=64` direct | 1258.8 | 1.803 ms | 0.000 | 1.803 | 1.856 ms |
| k7 `oc=64` **packed** | 157.4 | 0.053 | 0.198 | **0.251** | **0.290** |
| k3 `oc=32` direct | 115.6 | 0.302 | 0.000 | 0.302 | 0.344 |
| k3 `oc=32` **packed** | 14.5 | 0.053 | 0.049 | **0.102** | **0.138** |

Compare `exec + drain`, not `exec`. A short program's drain is exposed, where a long one's
finishes inside the poll interval that detects the job over. On `exec + drain` the packed form
is 7.2x at the 7×7 stem and 3.0x at the 3×3. That is a lever of 1.566 ms of driver-measured job
time per inference at the 7×7 geometry. The two encodings run at the same rate, 1.59e-3 against
1.43e-3 ms per programmed MMAC. The packed path is not a faster datapath, it is fewer MACs,
which is the cost model closing on itself.

#### The packed encoding's bounds on the four graphs

What blocks the packed encoding is different on each graph, and only one block is the part's.
The encoding has four decoded bounds:

- A non-zero left pad
- An output width of `iw/stride`
- An output width that is a multiple of 16
- For an exact border, a zero input zero point

The table reads each stem out of the four `.rnet` blobs against those bounds:

| graph | stem | `pad_left` | `in_zp` | packed encoding |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|
| MobileNetV1 | `ic=3 oc=32 k3 s2` | 0 | 0 | refused: TFLite SAME has no leading pad |
| MobileNetV2 | `ic=3 oc=32 k3 s2` | 0 | 0 | refused: same |
| ResNet-18 | `ic=3 oc=64 k7 s2` | 3 | **-128** | refused: the zero point, on the pad columns |
| Inception V1 | `ic=3 oc=64 k7 s2` | **2** | **0** | **runs** |

So the encoding requires a leading pad rather than refusing one. The two graphs where the stem
is 36% of the execution fail its bounds for two different reasons. ResNet-18's is a measured
hardware bound: the pad columns feed 0 at both ends whatever `CNA_PAD_CON1` holds. Repairing it
on the host costs 9.8 ms against a ~2 ms saving.

But the columns are materializable. Extending the image at the zero point and cropping the
surplus output column is bit-exact against the direct lowering on that exact stem (above). The
bound is therefore on the naive descriptor rather than on the encoding.

Inception V1's block is not a hardware bound at all, and it is closed. The three refusals are
the library's own messages, read out of a graph run rather than computed here. The gate
`rk3576_net_gate` attempts the packed pack for every `ic <= 4` convolution and prints what came
back, so the count is a measurement.

#### The asymmetric output extent on the packed encoding

The packed encoding honors an asymmetric output extent. The CNA takes no configured trailing
pad. It derives the pad its last window consumes from the output extent and the leading pad.
The emitter writes that derivation into `CNA_PAD_CON0`'s high bytes for both sub-encodings,
from one shared `r76_trail_pad(OW, stride, KW, pad_left, IW)`. The packed converter reads those
bytes exactly as the direct path does [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `tests/rk3576_argb_extent.c`].
Eight asymmetric cells are bit-exact, over five axes:

- Kernels 5 and 7
- Strides 1 and 2
- Planes 64/128/224
- Trailing pads 2/3/4
- Each axis asymmetric while the other stays derived

The probe scores by pad region. It classifies every output position by which pads its window
reaches: interior, leading, trailing or both. That classification separates the two ways the
answer could have been no. A derived pad the sub-encoding does not read shows as an exact
interior against a wrong trailing edge. A mis-programmed geometry is wrong everywhere.

Each cell carries two controls: the direct path at the same extent, and the packed path at the
symmetric lead. The direct path is the arm that must succeed, since it is what makes the
reference trustworthy.

So the refusal was the library's. The bound on an extent on this path is the shared rule every
int8 entry applies. A trailing pad of `kh` or more is a whole window of pad.

#### A packed program at the head of a chained stream

A packed program can head a chained stream, which is the other half of reachability. All four
graphs are one hardware kick in one submit. A stem that cannot chain splits the run and pays a
784 KiB join, which the per-join sweep priced at 6.80 ms. That join would make the cheaper
program a large net loss. The packed program chains in 20 of 20 cells over five geometries,
with both boundaries scored [HW sweep, `tests/rk3576_chain_argb.c`]. The consumer's stale form
is distinguishable throughout.

Chaining needs its own test because the packed sub-encoding is the one place a convolution
program configures the CNA front end differently. It has a folded channel count that disagrees
with the DMA's, a live CVT stage, and a packed row stride instead of a cube's. If any of that
crossed a chained boundary, the program after it is where it would show.

A packed handle can only begin a run. Its feature buffer is an interleaved image, and no
producer's surface is one. Beginning a run is the only position a stem occupies anyway. Its
output side is an ordinary NC1HWC2 cube and joins normally.

#### Residency and the vectorized image pack

The lever lands once the encoding is resident and its pack is vectorized. Neither is encoding
work, and both were worth more than the register question. The table measures the whole
prepacked entry, resident on both arms, with the governor pinned [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`rk3576_stem_cost` E and F]:

| `ic=3 224x224 s2`, resident | tasks | programmed MMAC | wall | direct | packed saves |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Inception V1 stem, k7 `oc=64` pad 2, extent 112 | 1 | 157.4 | **1.24 ms** | 3.09 | **1.85 ms (60%)** |
| k7 `oc=64` pad 3 | 1 | 157.4 | **1.23** | 3.12 | **1.89 (61%)** |
| k3 `oc=32` pad 1 | 1 | 14.5 | **0.63** | 1.13 | **0.50 (44%)** |

The packed program plans into one row task where the direct lowering takes five or six, and
every arm is bit-exact against the CPU model.

Two library facts carry the lever, and each is worth about a millisecond:

- **Residency.** Run through the transient entry, the packed encoding pays a per-call BO
  teardown that the resident direct arms do not. Profiled transiently at the Inception V1
  geometry, the call was 4.79 ms, of which teardown was 1.82 and `plan+gen` 0.85. One handle
  serves both encodings: `rocket_conv2d_int8_pack_rk3576()` packs the packed cube as well as
  the direct one. For a transient call, `rocket_conv2d_int8_rk3576()` builds the same handle,
  so there is no second arithmetic to keep in agreement. The per-axis requant and the fused LUT
  activation are refused on it. Both are the direct path's levers, and neither is measured on
  this encoding.
- **The host image pack.** Converting a CHW tensor to an interleaved image is a 2/3/4-way
  interleave of whole planes, one `vst2`/`vst3`/`vst4`, not the cube path's 16×16 block
  transpose. Written an element at a time, the pack gives most of the lever back. At the
  Inception V1 stem the wall was 2.80 ms scalar against 1.64 vectorized, under the same run
  conditions. The profiled split says the encoding saves 1.70 ms of submit, and the scalar form
  spent 1.88 ms of it on that loop. The shared entry is `rocket_rk3576_argb_pack()`.

**Trap: the RK3576 board runs the `ondemand` governor, and a host-bound arm is sensitive to
it.** The direct arm's wall read 3.82-3.86 ms before the interleave landed and 4.53-4.61 after,
with nothing about the direct arm changed. The packed arm ahead of it in the same process got
1.2 ms faster. The governor had therefore ramped less by the time the direct arm ran. The same
arm reads 4.6-4.8 run alone through the name filter, and 3.86 as the last row of a full sweep.

**Pin the governor for any A/B whose arms differ in host work. Discard the first process as
well as the first iteration.** With `performance` set, runs 2 and 3 agree to 0.01 ms while
run 1 is 25% high.

#### What the packed form adds and removes

Against the direct lowering, the packed form removes the MAC count and five row tasks. It adds
these:

- A host interleave, which is vectorized
- A weight cube that is a different object
- Four geometry bounds that are silent if violated, and are therefore refused
- A zero-input-zero-point bound
- No per-axis requant
- No fused activation
- No cube in

None of it changes reachability across the corpus. Reachability stays at one graph of four,
Inception V1, because the bounds that block the other three are the part's.

#### The packed stem in the Inception V1 graph

The packed stem lands on the graph at 19% of Inception V1's wall. Wiring it into
`rk3576_net_gate` takes one descriptor field and leaves the rest of the run structure
untouched. The whole graph goes 10.7-10.8 -> 8.7 ms, byte-identical, in the same one submit and
one kick over 81 layers. Its task count goes 77 -> 72 [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `bench 100`,
three runs an arm, `ondemand`]. The saving is the same absolute 2.1 ms in every guard arm,
since the guard does not move (122 brackets over 14445 KiB either way):

| Inception V1, `bench 100` | direct stem | packed stem | saves |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| default guard | 10.7-10.8 ms | **8.7** | 2.0-2.1 (19%) |
| `GUARD_NARROW=1` | 7.7 | **5.6** | 2.1 (27%) |
| `GUARD_PER_KICK=1` | 6.2 | **4.1** | 2.1 (34%) |

The other three graphs do not move, at 4.8-4.9 / 6.7 / 9.2-9.3. Each prints the library's own
refusal for its stem, which is what makes "one graph of four" a measurement.

Two terms move and the profile names both [`ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1`, ratios from the
profiled run and walls from an unprofiled one]:

| term | direct | packed | delta |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| submit (the part running programs) | 5.59 ms | 4.05-4.15 | **-1.48** |
| feature (the host operand pack) | 0.50-0.55 | 0.07-0.09 | **-0.44** |
| stamp + verify (the write guard) | 4.28-4.45 | 4.36-4.63 | 0 |
| de-scatter | 0.01 | 0.01 | 0 |

The feature term is the second half of the saving, and the cap did not include it. The direct
lowering scatters the image into a 32-channel cube, `round32(3)`. The packed one interleaves
three bytes per pixel instead. That is about 10x fewer bytes, through a loop that is vectorized
on both paths.

The cap was 1.85 ms (17%) and the measurement 2.0-2.1 (19%): a hit, low by the feature term.
Treating the per-op A/B as an upper bound gets the sign wrong. A per-op A/B compares two whole
calls that each pay `plan+gen` and a readback the chain does not. It is not an upper bound on
anything, because it is a different quantity.

The packed handle's surface is the next layer's cube, as measured on the graph. The graph
keeps all 53 joins of 80 adjacent pairs and its single 81-layer run, so the stem is an ordinary
interior-adjacent producer.

The encoding is bit-exact at a non-zero weight zero point. Inception V1's stem carries
`w_zp = 13`, and every layer of the graph scores `hw exact` against the CPU model. The per-op
probes all ran at `w_zp = 0`, so the graph is where that axis first varies. The packed path's
tap count is the live `ic*kh*kw`, and its feature buffer has no padded MAC group. The `B` term
therefore folds over exactly the taps the program walks.

The packed stem is default-on in the gate. Its control is `ROCKET_RK3576_NET_ARGB=0`, which is
itself a gate: the two lowerings must agree byte for byte and return the same label.

### Open questions

**A convolution program whose DPU drain outlasts `dpu_grace_us` loses its output tail, and
the fix is the driver's.** Raising the parameter from 500 to 750 us takes the shipped planner
to exact agreement with the part on 23 of 23 geometries (see above). Every measured wall is the
same 766-830 MMACs of work rather than any CBUF footprint. Raising the grace is a workaround,
because one program's duration is not a property of the driver's timing. Patch 0022 already
removes the same magic number for a kick of more than one task.

What is open is whether a job of one task can be given the same treatment. Such a job programs
`TASK_NUMBER = 1`, where both `PC_TASK_STATUS` counters always read zero. The register that
closed the multi-task case therefore says nothing here. The case is unreachable in this corpus,
where the row window keeps every task's drain under the deadline by accident.

Whether the planner's whole-cube charge can go once the driver stops retiring early is also
open. The function `rocket_rk3576_max_task_rows()` charges every output-channel group's slice
where a rung still fits. That charge was fitted to the ResNet stem's 48-row measurement, and
that measurement is the deadline. It costs the stem a fifth row task.

The one geometry that keeps the one-slice model, `56x56 k7 ic 64 oc 64`, is honored in full at a
grace of 750. So the charge is [expected] to be unnecessary as well, and that is a prediction.
Relaxing a bound whose safety would then rest on a module parameter's value is not a trade to
take before the driver side is settled.

**A unit-kernel pooling program poisons the next process's first pooling submits.** The rate
depends on what runs before:

| what runs before | runs with a wrong cell |
|---|---:|
| `rk3576_pool_probe pad` | 2 of 30 |
| a wide-output int32 program | 0 of 30 |
| nothing | 0 of 30 |
| nothing, in an earlier run | 0 of 300 |

The failure is byte-identical every time, in the first two cells at `c=16`. One whole output row
is wrong on the 16×16 plane, and 2302 of 2304 on the 24×24. It is the same question as
`pool_probe pad`'s own unstable unit-kernel cells. Three things are not known:

- Which register or state carries it
- Whether a non-pooling submit in between clears it
- Whether any geometry a real graph runs can produce it

**Whether a pool can start or end a chained run is unmeasured, and refused.** A pool that began
a run would need the chain to scatter a row-major tensor into its cube. One that ended a run
would need its output de-scattered. Both are host work the stream exists to remove, so the
question is a library one rather than a hardware one. No graph here asks it: both MobileNets
and ResNet-18 pool in the middle. It is worth nothing on this corpus.
